**Dated rather than aliased** because `06-TESTING-STRATEGY.md` records the model with every eval run, and an accuracy curve measured against a pointer that can move underneath it is not reproducible — which is the single property that makes the curve worth showing.
**Trade-off accepted:** **The pin is roughly two generations old and a 2024 snapshot can be retired.** That is foreseen rather than discovered: if the snapshot stops being served, the swap is one constant in `config.py` and one eval run at 1.11, and the two environment variables are already there to override it without a code change in the meantime. Also accepted: this entry rests on a limitation worth stating, which is that the newer models postdate the assistant's knowledge cutoff, so nothing here claims anything about their pricing, their vision quality or their Structured Outputs behaviour. The bake-off exists because those claims could not be made, not merely because measurement is tidy.

## 079 — The OpenAI client is built on first use, never at import

**Decision:** `vision.py` exposes `_client()`, an `lru_cache(maxsize=1)` function returning `AsyncOpenAI`. No client exists at module scope.
**Alternative:** `client = AsyncOpenAI(api_key=settings.OPENAI_API_KEY)` at module level, which is what `storage.py` does one line down from its imports and what almost every example shows.
**Reasoning:** Measured before it was written:

```
AsyncOpenAI(api_key='')   RAISED openai.OpenAIError: Missing credentials.
AsyncOpenAI()             RAISED openai.OpenAIError: Missing credentials.
```

`Settings.OPENAI_API_KEY` defaults to `""`, and `07-DEPLOYMENT.md` states plainly that the API keys default to empty strings *so that CI can run without an OpenAI account*. A module-level client therefore raises on **import**, not on call — and from task 1.3 `vision.py` sits on the import path to `app.main`, so the entire backend suite would stop collecting in CI with an error about credentials, on a run that never intended to make an AI call. `USE_FAKE_AI` cannot save it, because the flag is read inside `tag_item` and import happens first.

This is 046's lesson arriving through a second door. There, `cloudinary.config()` at import means monkeypatching `settings.CLOUDINARY_API_KEY` afterwards changes nothing, and the cost was an hour of confusion in a test. Here the same shape fails harder and earlier, and the difference is only that the SDK validates its credentials in the constructor rather than at call time.
**Trade-off accepted:** A cached factory where a reader expects a module constant, and a client that outlives a settings change — a test that needs a different key must call `_client.cache_clear()`. That is explicit, which is the point: `storage.py`'s equivalent staleness is invisible and had to be written down in 046 for anyone to find it.

## 080 — The schema is the document's, the vocabulary is generated, and the placeholder is asserted

**Decision:** `VISION_SCHEMA` is a literal JSON Schema dict sent through `response_format`, not a Pydantic model through `chat.completions.parse`. Its enum arrays are built from `app/enums.py`. `required` is derived from the properties rather than restated. The prompt file carries a `{{VOCABULARY}}` placeholder that `_load_system_prompt` replaces, raising at import if it is absent.
**Alternative:** A Pydantic model and `.parse()`, which is idiomatic in openai 2.x and gives a typed result. Enum arrays written out as `03-AI-CONTRACTS.md` prints them. The vocabulary block appended to the end of the prompt file with no placeholder.
**Reasoning:** On `.parse()`: it generates a schema from the model, and **the schema this task exists to verify is the one the document wrote down** — including a nullable enum expressed as `{"type": ["string", "null"], "enum": [...]}`, where Pydantic emits `anyOf`. Verifying a generated variant would prove nothing about the contract, and the strict-subset notes in `03` are specifically about hand-written JSON Schema. On deriving `required`: strict mode rejects a schema where `required` and `properties` differ, so a second hand-written list is one forgotten edit from a `400` that reads as a model fault.

On generating the enum arrays, the cost is real and is recorded rather than glossed. **`06-TESTING-STRATEGY.md`'s Layer 2 contract test becomes a tautology.** Its stated purpose was to catch someone adding `burgundy` to the prompt but not to the enum; with prompt, schema and validator all rendered from `enums.py`, that drift is structurally impossible and a comparison between two expressions of one source proves nothing. The trade is still right — a structural guarantee beats a test — but a test whose docstring claims a coverage it no longer has is worse than no test, so `06` is corrected in the same commit and names the real remaining seam: `02-DATA-MODEL.md` → `enums.py` → `enums.ts`, hand-maintained, three copies, nothing comparing them.

On the placeholder: appending would work identically, and the placeholder makes the prompt file honest about what it becomes — someone reading it alone otherwise sees a prompt with no vocabulary and no sign one is coming. The guard raises at import because a prompt that silently lost its vocabulary still returns plausible tags. **`fit`, `length` and `rise` are plain strings in the schema** — an out-of-vocabulary value there is coerced to null rather than retried, so constraining them would buy nothing — which means the rendered block is the *only* place the model ever learns those three vocabularies. Losing it degrades exactly the three fields nothing else would catch. The guard is covered by `test_a_prompt_file_without_the_placeholder_raises_at_load`, not merely intended.
**Trade-off accepted:** No typed result object — `tag_item` returns `dict[str, Any]` and 1.2's `ItemTags` is where types arrive. And `03-AI-CONTRACTS.md`'s printed schema is now a *description* of generated output rather than the literal source, so the two can drift if `enums.py` changes and the document is not updated. That is the same seam named above and it is the one this project has always had.

## 081 — `USE_FAKE_AI` returns a hand-written placeholder, breaking 06's recorded-fixtures rule on purpose

**Decision:** `_FAKE_TAGS` is a hand-written constant returned by `tag_item` when `settings.USE_FAKE_AI` is true. Task 5.1 replaces it with responses recorded from the live API and keyed by input.
**Alternative:** Have the flag raise `NotImplementedError` until 5.1 records real fixtures, which is the only way to honour `06-TESTING-STRATEGY.md` literally from day one. Or defer the branch entirely to 5.1.
**Reasoning:** `06-TESTING-STRATEGY.md` says fixtures are "**real recorded responses**, captured once from the live API and committed. Hand-written fixtures drift from reality; recorded ones do not." That rule is right and it is broken here knowingly.

A flag that raises is useless for four stages — and `STAGE-1` 1.1 requires the branch "from day one, not as an afterthought", because 1.3's background task, 1.5's grid, 1.7's polling and every Playwright journey need *some* deterministic tagging long before 5.1 exists. Deferring the branch means retrofitting it through a `BackgroundTask`, which the stage file specifically warns against.

What the rule is actually protecting against is a fixture that *claims* to represent model output and does not. The mitigation is that this one says so: `display_name` reads **"placeholder white shirt"**, so a demo accidentally run with the flag on is visible on screen rather than passing for a real tagging result. **A rule broken on purpose with a name on it is fine; one broken quietly is not.**
**Trade-off accepted:** Between 1.1 and 5.1 every fake-tagged item is the same white shirt, so nothing exercises tag variety — 1.10's seed script is what provides a varied wardrobe, and it makes no AI calls, so the two do not collide. The fake is asserted to pass `validate_tag_dict` with no errors and no coercions, so a tightening at 1.2 breaks it loudly here rather than at 5.1. **That assertion does not cover the fake being *sensible*, only valid**: found by mutation, `layer: "standalone"` on a `top` validates just as cleanly, because the vocabulary's layer rules only force `standalone` where it is required and never reject it where it is nonsense. The gap is the validator's and belongs to 1.2.

## 082 — The vocabulary's `layer` rules run in one direction only

**Decision:** Recorded here and **not fixed at task 1.1**. `validate_tag_dict` forces `layer` to `standalone` for `dress`, `shoes`, `bag` and `accessory`, and to `outer` for `outerwear`. It does not reject `standalone` on a category that cannot have it: `{"category": "top", "subcategory": "shirt", "layer": "standalone"}` validates with **no error and no coercion**. Task 1.2 owns the validator and owns the choice — either add the reverse rule and say what it coerces to, or record the asymmetry as deliberate and cite this entry.
**Alternative:** Fix it at 1.1, which means editing `enums.py` from a task that owns neither the validator nor the vocabulary. Or leave it as a note in `STAGE-1-wardrobe.md` alone.
**Reasoning:** Found by mutation, and by accident. The mutation was to the *fake tags* in `vision.py` — changing `layer` from `base` to `standalone` to confirm that `test_the_fake_is_valid_input_to_the_validator` defended the choice. It did not fail, because there is nothing to fail: the rule does not exist.

**This is a Stage 2 problem discovered at Stage 1, which is the only reason it is worth a number.** `layer` is the field the stylist's layering constraint keys on — `02-DATA-MODEL.md` states that the stylist may not put two `outer` items in one look, and `03-AI-CONTRACTS.md`'s prompt gives the base → mid → outer ordering as a styling principle. A top mis-tagged `standalone` is therefore not a cosmetic error: it sits outside that reasoning entirely, and the look it appears in will be silently wrong rather than visibly invalid.

Nothing upstream catches it. Structured Outputs cannot — `standalone` is a legal member of the `layer` enum and the schema has no way to express "legal, but not for this category", which is the same limitation that made `subcategory` a plain string. The prompt says which categories are standalone, but a prompt is not a guarantee. `PATCH /items/{id}` runs the same validator (030), so a hand-built request can set it too, and there the failure is a `200` rather than a `422`.

It is left for 1.2 rather than taken here because `CONVENTIONS.md`'s one-task-at-a-time rule is what keeps scope from riding into a task's orientation, and because the fix is a genuine choice rather than an obvious repair: the reverse rule has to decide what a `top` tagged `standalone` *becomes*, and `base` is a guess dressed as a correction.
**Trade-off accepted:** An entry that describes a defect and defers the decision, which is not what this log is normally for. It is numbered anyway, so that 2.x can cite a number when it either relies on the invariant or works around its absence — a stage-file note would have been read once, by whoever built 1.2, and never found again from Stage 2.

**A second instance arrived before 1.2 started, and it changes what this entry is.** Seven JPEGs run through `tag_item` returned `fit: "skinny"` on a tank top — a legal `Fit` member, meaningless on that category, passing with no error and no coercion. That is this entry's shape on a different field, so the title understates it: the subject is not `layer`, it is that **`validate_tag_dict` checks membership and never checks category-appropriateness**. `DECISIONS.md` 084 states the general rule, lists the third candidate (`length`), and records that 029's premise for leaving `length` alone has been falsified.

**And the deferral this entry made is now closed as a deferral.** 1.2 must decide rather than defer again. What a `top` tagged `standalone` becomes is the whole content of the fix, and leaving it unpicked is not the same as choosing to leave it — "the asymmetry stays" is an acceptable answer and has to be written down as one.

**Closed at task 1.2a. `{"category": "top", "layer": "standalone"}` is now an error, and it becomes nothing.** The question this entry left open had no answer because none exists: `02-DATA-MODEL.md` names a layer for every other category, and for `top` it names none — a sweater, a hoodie or an open shirt is legitimately `base` or `mid`. So the vocabulary refuses instead of substituting. The vision path retries once with the violation named, and `PATCH /items/{id}` returns a `422` naming `layer`; a second failure is a `TaggingError` and the item finishes `failed`. `base` is never written by anything. The asymmetry this entry described does **not** stay, but the reason it looked like an asymmetry does: the rules that coerce are the ones whose category determines an answer, and the rule that errors is the one whose category does not. 085 has the full argument, and `test_a_top_refusing_a_layer_is_an_error_and_the_value_is_left_alone` is what fails if anyone reinstates the guess.

## 083 — The `vision` transform pins the format, and what that did and did not catch

**Decision:** `Transform.VISION` moves from `w_800,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto` to `w_800,c_limit,f_jpg,q_auto`. `detail` keeps `f_auto` and is unchanged. `07-DEPLOYMENT.md`'s table, `03-AI-CONTRACTS.md`'s input line and `01-ARCHITECTURE.md`'s flow step 7a are corrected to match.
**Alternative:** Keep `f_auto`, which the measurement below does not disprove.
**Reasoning — and the first thing to say is what was *not* found.** 046 left this open as a suspected failure: `f_auto` on a HEIC original, delivered to a client sending no `Accept` header, might fall back to the format named by the file extension, and these URLs deliberately carry none. **That failure did not occur.** One real iPhone HEIC, 2,047,391 bytes, uploaded and fetched three ways through the `vision` transform:

```
no Accept header         image/jpeg
*/*                      image/jpeg
browser-like             image/webp
```

The two requests that most resemble what a non-browser fetcher sends both returned JPEG, and only a browser-like `Accept` produced WebP — which OpenAI reads anyway. **The live vision call in task 1.1 was then made against the `f_auto` URL and it worked**, returning fifteen valid fields. So on this sample the path OpenAI most likely took was already safe, and nothing was broken at the moment the change was made.

**What was caught is an unobservable variable, not a live failure.** `f_auto`'s answer is a function of the `Accept` header of whoever fetches the URL. For every other transform in the table that client is a browser, which is what `f_auto` exists to serve. For `vision` it is OpenAI's fetcher — we cannot see the header it sends, we cannot control it, and it can change without notice on their side or on Cloudinary's. **Format negotiation cannot help us here: OpenAI is not a browser and gains nothing from a smaller modern format, so the only thing negotiation can do on this URL is one day return something the model cannot read.** Removing a variable that only has a downside is worth one word in a string, and it is worth it *before* the golden-set run at 1.11 rather than in the middle of it, where a silent format change would look like an accuracy regression.

**This is exactly why the criterion was three requests rather than one.** The stage file's original test — "if it comes back `image/heic`, change it" — would have passed on the first line and closed the question with `f_auto` still in place and the real variable undisturbed. The three-header form is what surfaced the disagreement, and the disagreement is the finding.
**Trade-off accepted:** `vision` no longer benefits from any future format Cloudinary adds, which for a machine consumer is no loss. Delivery is marginally larger than a negotiated WebP, on one fetch per item, which is invisible next to the model call it feeds. And the split between `detail` and `vision` that 046 defended as hypothetical is now load-bearing: they hold different strings, so anyone tidying them back into one entry breaks this. `test_the_vision_transform_pins_the_format_rather_than_negotiating` exists to say so by name rather than only through the table-comparison test, which would fail with a message about a string.

## 084 — `validate_tag_dict` checks membership and never checks whether the member belongs with the category

**Decision:** Recorded as **one gap with three instances**, not three unrelated bugs. Task 1.2 owns the decision and must make it rather than defer it again; "the asymmetry stays" is an acceptable answer that has to be chosen and written down.
**Alternative:** Treat 082's `layer` finding and the `fit` finding below as separate patches, fixing whichever is in front of you.
**Reasoning:** The validator tests every descriptive field against a **flat** vocabulary. Two fields already escape that — `subcategory` through the `SUBCATEGORIES` map, and `rise` through an explicit bottoms-only gate — so the shape of the fix already exists in the file twice over. Everything else is membership only, which means a value that is a legal member of its own enum and nonsense beside its category passes with no error and no coercion.

Three instances, in descending order of how much they cost:

- **`layer`** (082). `{"category": "top", "layer": "standalone"}` validates clean. `layer` is what the stylist's layering constraint keys on, so the resulting look is silently wrong rather than visibly invalid.
- **`fit`**. `{"category": "top", "subcategory": "tank", "fit": "skinny"}` validates clean. `skinny` is a trouser word. The stylist prompt reasons on `fit` for proportion — *"Skinny and slim bottoms balance volume above"* — so a wrong `fit` degrades styling advice rather than breaking structure.
- **`length`**. Untested and unobserved, and 029 accepted the risk deliberately.

**029's premise has been falsified in the neighbouring field, which is why this is a decision and not a bug report.** That entry rejected a per-category map for `length` on the grounds that "the three axes do not collide in practice — the vision model does not offer `maxi` for a t-shirt", and set the condition for revisiting as the Stage 1 golden-set run. The model offered `skinny` for a tank top on the seventh image anyone ran, before the golden set exists. `maxi` on a t-shirt and `skinny` on a tank top are the same sentence.

**A second and genuinely separate problem surfaced beside it, and the two must not be conflated.** `fit: "flared"` — a value in no vocabulary at all — is caught by the membership check and coerced to null. The validator works exactly as designed. What is missing is any *record*: `TagValidation.coerced` carries the discarded value at validation time, `03-AI-CONTRACTS.md` has the vision path coerce silently by design, and nothing persists it. So a real garment attribute the model observed is dropped, the item screen shows an empty field, and no trace survives that a value was ever returned. `items.attributes` is a `JSONB` column that exists to absorb exactly this kind of thing without a migration; whether rejected values belong there is 1.3's call, and logging them is 1.2's. **That gap is about membership working and nobody watching, where the gap above is about membership not being enough.**
**Trade-off accepted:** Naming this as one rule invites one generic mechanism, and a generic per-category allow-map is what 029 rejected for reasons that have not gone away — it doubles the validation surface, the seed data's exposure to it and the test matrix. The mapping is also not equally writable per field: `layer`'s is close to total (standalone categories → `standalone`, outerwear → `mid` or `outer`, bottoms → `base`), while `fit`'s is fuzzy and a fuzzy allow-map manufactures false coercions. A per-category **deny** list is a smaller and more defensible claim than an allow-map and is worth considering for `fit` alone. None of that is decided here, deliberately — 1.2 owns the validator.

**Amended at task 1.2a — the `layer` list above is wrong in the one place that mattered, and it would have propagated.** It describes the map as "close to total" and then names only four of the seven categories: standalone categories, outerwear, and bottoms. **`bottoms → base` was not implemented anywhere** — it is a claim this entry made about code that did not exist — and **`top` is missing from the list altogether**, which is the single category that makes the map *close to* total rather than total. Anyone implementing from this sentence would have written a total map and, in doing so, silently decided that a top tagged `standalone` becomes `base` — the guess 082 exists to refuse. Both are settled in 085; the general rule this entry states is unchanged and correct.

## 085 — Category-dependent validity is one rule with one shape, and `top` is the only category it cannot answer for

**Decision:** `app/enums.py` gains three tables and `validate_tag_dict` gains one judgement: **is this value one the category it arrived beside can be described by?** The response is not new policy — it is the field's existing policy. `FIELD_APPLIES_TO` says which categories a field describes at all (`fit`: top, bottom, dress, outerwear; `length`: everything but bag and accessory; `rise`: bottom, which generalises the gate already in the file). `VALUE_APPLIES_TO` narrows individual words that are narrower than their field: `skinny` to bottoms, `wide` to bottoms and dresses, `bodycon` to everything but outerwear, the three sleeve words to top/dress/outerwear, the three hem words to bottom/dress/outerwear. `LAYERS_BY_CATEGORY` gives every category an admitted set and an answer. `CATEGORY_DEPENDENT_FIELDS` exports the five field names so `PATCH /items/{id}` reads the list rather than restating it. `_STANDALONE_CATEGORIES` is deleted: it is now four rows of the layer table. All three tables are public because task 1.2b renders them into the prompt's `{{VOCABULARY}}` block.

**Alternative:** One generic per-category allow-map per field, which is what 084's framing invites. Coercing a `top` tagged `standalone` to `base`, which is what 082 called a guess and what 084's own sentence would have produced. Leaving the asymmetry, which 082 said was an acceptable answer if chosen. Mirroring the rules into `enums.ts` so 1.9's tag editor can filter its selects.

**Reasoning — the rule is one pair, applied five times.** Which values the category admits, and what the category says the answer is when the value is not admitted. Where the category determines a single answer the rule coerces to it; where it does not, the vocabulary reports an error, which the vision path turns into one retry naming the violation and `PATCH` turns into a `422`. **Nothing invents a value.** Both halves already existed in the file — `SUBCATEGORIES` is the error case, the bottoms-only `rise` gate is the coercion case — so this is a generalisation of two rules rather than a new mechanism.

**The answer comes from the document naming one, not from the arithmetic of the admissible set.** This is the part most likely to be re-derived wrongly. `outerwear` admits **two** values, `mid` and `outer`, and still has a determinate answer — `outer` — because `02-DATA-MODEL.md` says a coat is outer. `top` also admits two, `base` and `mid`, and has **no** answer, because the document names none and both are legitimate. So cardinality is not the test; the document is. **An implementation that derived the answer from the size of `admits` would turn `outerwear` into an error path and break `test_outerwear_with_a_base_layer_is_coerced_to_outer`** — a documented, tested coercion silently reversed by a plausible-looking simplification. That is precisely why `LayerRule` stores `answer` as a field rather than computing it, and why a later reader should not tidy it into a derivation.

**The three tables have three shapes, and the asymmetry is structural rather than untidy.** `fit`, `length` and `rise` are the three fields `02-DATA-MODEL.md` declares always nullable, so their answer is `NULL` by construction: any other answer would be a substituted value, which is the one thing this rule forbids. There is nothing for a second field to hold, so those two tables hold only categories. `layer` is not nullable by design — it sits in `_STRICT_ENUM_FIELDS` and the stylist's layering constraint keys on it — so it is the only field that has to name something, and therefore the only table with room to. Three tables, three shapes; do not make them uniform. `VALUE_APPLIES_TO` is separately nested by field rather than keyed by value alone because **`mid` is a member of both `Rise` and `Layer`**, so a flat value-keyed map would be ambiguous the moment a rule touched either.

**Which fields opt in, including the part that does not.** `layer` opts in fully. `fit` opts in twice — an applicability gate and three narrowed words. `length` opts in twice as well, but only at the two ends of its flat list: the sleeve words and the hem words, which belong to exactly one axis each. The middle five — `crop`, `regular`, `longline`, `ankle`, `full` — are left unenforced on purpose, because they genuinely span axes (cropped trousers and a cropped top, ankle boots and ankle trousers) and a rule over them would coerce correct answers away. 029's cost argument survives intact in that gap; what does not survive is its premise, and 029 is closed.

**An unrecognised `category` fires no category-dependent rule at all.** Every rule here is keyed by the category, so when it is not in the vocabulary there is nothing to look up rather than something to report a second time — and the membership check has already reported the one real fault. Reporting four consequences of it would bury the cause in the retry text 1.2b builds from `TagValidation.reason`. `subcategory` predates this and still double-reports; unifying that would be re-litigating a rule from task 0.4 and is deliberately not done here.

**The rules stay server-side.** `enums.ts` mirrors the vocabulary's *values* and not its rules. The alternative adds a hand-written copy in a second language with nothing comparing it to anything — the same position `CONVENTIONS.md` already records for the upload limits and the password rules — and it would have been written before the screen that wants it existed. What 1.9 gets instead: the editor may offer `skinny` for a tank, and the server answers `422` naming the field. That is a visible, recoverable failure, and it is only safe because the clearing rule below keeps a category change from producing the same `422` on an otherwise correct request. If it ever matters enough to fix, the fix is an endpoint publishing the vocabulary — which needs `04-API-SPEC.md` changed first, since that document forbids adding endpoints.

**Measured rather than asserted.** The eight responses task 1.1 observed were replayed through the new rules, each row carrying only the fields `STAGE-1` 1.11 recorded, since an absent key is never invented: both known-wrong answers are caught by two different mechanisms — `fit: "flared"` by membership, which already worked, and `fit: "skinny"` on a tank by this entry's rule — and the other six are untouched, including `bodycon` on a bodysuit and `wide` on jeans, which the narrow tables must not reach. Seven mutations plus a fatal control were run from a pristine copy with a green baseline verified at both ends; all eight were caught. `tests/unit/test_enums.py` pins each table **literally** as well as behaviourally, because a behavioural test that reads its expectation from the table adapts to a mutation of the table — mutation 5 is caught by exactly one test in 376, and that test is the literal pin.

**Trade-off accepted:** **An item can now finish `failed` with no tags at all where it previously finished `ready` with a wrong `layer`.** That is the cost of refusing to guess, and it is the right side of the trade: a failed tile is visible and carries a retry button and an "Add manually" link, whereas a wrong `layer` is invisible until Stage 2 builds a look on it, with nothing pointing back here. The alternative — correcting a top to `base` — would push a hoodie the model answered `mid` about, correctly, to the wrong value across a whole class of garments, silently, and far more often than the error will fire. Two smaller costs: the validation surface and the test matrix are larger, which is exactly what 029 was unwilling to pay and is now paid deliberately; and `PATCH` clears five fields on a category change rather than three, so a `top → dress` edit discards a `fit` that was valid. 030 accepted that shape of loss for `layer` already and now says five.

## 086 — `validate_tags` judges one answer at a time, and the two failures it can end in are not the same failure

**Decision:** `async validate_tags(raw: dict[str, Any], image_url: str) -> ItemTags` in `app/services/vision.py`. It reads `validate_tag_dict`'s report, logs every coercion, retries **once** through `tag_item(image_url, correction=…)` naming the violation, and raises `TaggingError` — defined here, beside `StorageError`'s shape (044) — when the second answer is unacceptable too. `ItemTags` is a frozen slotted dataclass typed with the vocabulary classes, carrying the accepted answer's `coerced` issues and nothing else. Eleven non-nullable fields absent, `null` or blank are a violation like any other. The `confidence < 0.35` branch is **not** built and no settings field is added. 028 is struck in the two places this contradicts.
**Alternative:** `validate_tags(raw)` as three documents printed it, which cannot retry. A third function owning call-validate-retry so 1.3 has one entry point. A Pydantic `ItemTags`. Carrying both attempts' coercions. Building the confidence branch as 028 and `03`'s table specified.
**Reasoning — the signature was the contradiction, and the fix names a boundary rather than patching a parameter list.** A retry is a second call to the model, so the function that owns the retry needs the URL; that much is arithmetic. What the arithmetic exposed is that **`tag_item`'s failures and `validate_tags`'s failures are different facts about the same item.** A `ValueError` or a provider exception says no usable answer arrived. `TaggingError` says the model answered and the answer could not be accepted. 1.3 writes `failed` for both and writes different `error_message`s, so the two are worth keeping apart — which is what settles the third-function alternative against itself: an orchestrator would hand 1.3 one exception type and throw the distinction away to save it three lines. The cost is that 1.3 wraps the first call itself and `TaggingError` is raised from one module while the naked call sits in another. `vision.py`'s docstring said "task 1.2 wraps both", which was never achievable under the documented signature and is corrected rather than honoured.

**The eleven required fields are what makes `ItemTags` constructible, and they belong here, not in the vocabulary.** `validate_tag_dict` reads every field with `.get`, so an absent key and a `null` one are one thing to it and both pass. That is *correct* — `PATCH /items/{id}` sends three fields and must not be told the other twelve are missing — and it means `{}` is a clean report. Building a typed object from a clean report that has no `category` is a `TypeError` escaping a background task, where the row stays `processing` until 1.3's sweep instead of finishing `failed` with a message. So the check lives in the service, keyed to the schema: the eleven are exactly the properties with no `null` in their type, asserted against `VISION_SCHEMA` rather than restated. Strict mode should make the row unfireable, which puts it in the same class as the first and third rows of `03`'s table — a check that exists because the guarantee is somebody else's.

**A blank `display_name` counts as missing, which is a narrowing and is chosen rather than inherited.** `validate_tag_dict` checks `isinstance(str)` and nothing more, so `""` passes and 1.3 writes a tile with nothing on it. One condition, and the failure it prevents is the only one in the eleven that a user would see rather than a developer.

**`ItemTags.coerced` carries the accepted answer's coercions and no others, and this is the part most likely to be widened by someone being generous.** When the first answer is rejected and the retry is accepted, the first answer's discarded values describe tags that were never written — carrying them would let 1.3 record "`fit` was discarded" in `items.attributes` beside a row whose `fit` came back fine on the retry, which is not a fuller record but a false one. Nothing is lost: **every coercion from every attempt is logged**, with the field, the rejected value and the category it arrived beside. That log is the record 084 found missing — `fit: "flared"` on real jeans, correctly nulled, remembered by nothing — and it is deliberately not classified into "out of vocabulary" and "wrong for its category" at write time, because 1.11 has to count those apart and the field plus the value already answers which is which. Classifying it would mean a `kind` on `TagIssue`, which is 1.2a's file and its literal pins reopened for something already in the line.

**The confidence branch is not built because both of its branches do the same thing.** `03`'s row read *accept, but set `status='ready'` and flag for review in UI* — and an item above the threshold is also `status='ready'`. The only difference was a flag, and `05-FRONTEND-SPEC.md` names no review surface, no stage file mentions confidence, and 028's "against `settings`" named a field nobody had written. A branch whose sole output is a flag nothing reads is dead code. The evidence says the same thing louder: eight live responses at 1.1 returned `0.9` every time **including both wrong ones**, so the threshold would have flagged nothing in eight images while missing both errors. The number is not discarded — 1.3 persists it as `ai_confidence` and 1.11 mines it — so a threshold introduced later can be applied over the whole table, which is a better instrument than one frozen at tag time. The prompt still asks for the self-assessment and still names 0.35, because that is what the number honestly describes: the photograph, not the answer.
**Trade-off accepted:** **Three things a later reader may mistake for oversights.** `TaggingError` is raised from `vision.py` while an equally fatal `ValueError` is raised from a different call in the same module, so 1.3 must catch two types — deliberate, and the reason is above. `_REQUIRED_FIELDS` duplicates a fact the schema already states, guarded by a test that compares them rather than by a comment. And `03`'s table now has a row that strict mode makes unreachable on model output, which reads as belt-and-braces until `PATCH` or a future provider makes it the only thing standing between a short answer and an unhandled exception.

## 087 — The prompt is told the rules, not only the words, and the rendered result has a version

**Decision:** `_vocabulary_block()` renders `FIELD_APPLIES_TO`, `VALUE_APPLIES_TO` and `LAYERS_BY_CATEGORY` into `{{VOCABULARY}}` the way it already rendered `SUBCATEGORIES` — 080's property extended from the values to the rules. Each category's **answer** is not rendered, and the five unenforced `length` words get no rule line. `vision_system.md` loses the two sentences that hand-wrote rules the block now generates. `PROMPT_VERSION` is the first twelve hex characters of the SHA-256 of `SYSTEM_PROMPT`, taken after the render.
**Alternative:** Leaving the block as nine `fit` words with no category guidance, which is what made every category-dependent coercion a silent one. Rendering the answers as well. A hand-bumped version string, or none at all until 1.11 needs one.
**Reasoning:** Strict mode guarantees membership and nothing else, so `layer: "standalone"` on a top passes the schema perfectly and `fit: "skinny"` on a tank passed everything until 1.2a. The validator now catches both, but a validator that catches without telling produces exactly what 084 complained about — a coercion nobody learns from, and in the `top` case an item that finishes `failed`. Telling the model is the cheap half of the fix and it does not weaken the validator, because a prompt is not a guarantee (082).

**What is deliberately not rendered is the more interesting half.** The **answer** — bottom becomes `base`, outerwear becomes `outer` — is what *this layer does with a wrong value*, not something the model needs; printing it invites the model to reason about our correction rather than about the garment. And `crop`, `regular`, `longline`, `ankle` and `full` get no rule line at all, because `02-DATA-MODEL.md` leaves them unenforced on purpose: rendering a narrowing the validator does not apply would teach the model a rule this project does not hold, which is the one way this block can make the output worse rather than better. `test_the_middle_of_the_length_list_gets_no_rule_of_its_own` is what fails if someone renders all eleven for symmetry.

**The version is hashed because half the prompt is generated.** Task 1.11 measures accuracy against this prompt and records the version beside the model id, for the same reason 078 pinned a dated snapshot rather than an alias: a curve measured against something that moved underneath it is not reproducible. A hand-bumped constant is wrong the moment a table in `enums.py` changes and nobody remembers the constant — and that error is invisible until an eval run is compared against a prompt no one can reconstruct. Hashing the **rendered** text rather than the file is the whole point; hashing the file would hold still while the vocabulary moved. **Persisting it beside the tags is 1.3's**, so a row tagged today can be traced to the prompt that tagged it; 1.11 records it in `docs/eval-results.md`.
**Trade-off accepted:** The prompt is roughly 40% longer, at about 150 extra tokens per call — a fraction of a cent across the whole project, and cost was never the constraint here. Two costs that matter more. **Nothing measures whether the longer prompt helps**, and it could plausibly hurt: more instruction is more to ignore, and the `top`/`layer` error may now fire so rarely that the give-up path is exercised only by tests. That is 1.11's to find, which is exactly why the version exists. And `PROMPT_VERSION` is imported by nothing until 1.11 — a symbol with no reader for one task, accepted because the alternative is 1.11 hand-typing a version for a prompt that has already moved twice.

## 088 — The background task owns its session, its failures and the three ways a row stops being `processing`

**Decision:** `app/services/tagging.py`. `async tag_and_store(item_id, session_factory=SessionLocal)` is the body of the background task: it opens its own session, builds the `vision` transform URL from the row, calls `tag_item` then `validate_tags`, and writes either `ready` with fifteen tags, `ai_confidence` and an `attributes["tagging"]` record, or `failed` with an `error_message` that says which kind of failure it was. `Item.updated_at` gains `onupdate=text("now()")`. `sweep_stale_processing(db)` fails every `processing` row whose `updated_at` is older than `STALE_AFTER`, ten minutes; `run_startup_sweep()` gives it a session and swallows a database failure; a `lifespan` in `app/main.py` calls it at boot. `POST /items/upload` gains a `BackgroundTasks` parameter and queues one task per row after the commit.
**Alternative:** Passing the request's `Session` into the task. Passing the `public_id` alongside the `item_id`. Building the image URL in the route. One `error_message` for every failure. Keying the sweep on `created_at`. `asyncio.gather` over a batch instead of one task per item. A trigger in a migration instead of `onupdate`.

**Reasoning — the task opens its own session, and not for the reason it looks like.** The plausible reason is that FastAPI has already closed the request's session by the time a background task runs, which is what the 0.106 release notes established and what most material still says. **It is false in the version this project pins.** In fastapi 0.141, `routing.py` enters yield dependencies into `request_stack` and awaits the response — and therefore Starlette's background tasks, which run inside `Response.__call__` — *within* that stack, so `get_db`'s session is open the whole time. That was checked against the installed library rather than asserted, and the conclusion did not move, because the two reasons that actually decide it survive: sharing the request's session pins one Neon connection for the whole of a twenty-file batch rather than for one item, and it would couple a service function to a request lifetime for no gain. **A later reader who inherits the wrong reason will "fix" this by using the request session, so the wrong reason is written down here as wrong.**

**The image URL is built inside the task rather than passed in.** `build_url` raises `StorageError` on an empty `CLOUDINARY_CLOUD_NAME`, and built in the route that would `502` an entire upload *after* the rows were committed and the assets stored — a whole batch lost to a configuration fault that concerns one call. Built in the task it fails exactly one row, which is the correct blast radius, and it is why `StorageError` is a third exception this module catches rather than two.

**Three `error_message` texts, because there are three facts and one column.** `TaggingError` means the model answered and the answer could not be accepted; its message is already the violation, naming the field and the value, so it is prefixed and stored as it stands. A `ValueError`, a `StorageError` or a provider exception means no usable answer arrived. And a row swept at boot was never answered for at all. 086 kept the first two apart deliberately and this is where that distinction becomes readable: `SELECT error_message` sorts a failed wardrobe into the model's fault, the pipeline's fault, and the process that died. The column is developer-facing — `05-FRONTEND-SPEC.md` never renders it and `03`'s failure table gives the user a fixed "Couldn't read this one" — which is what licenses an exception type name inside it.

**`attributes["tagging"]` is a guest key, and the record is written on both paths.** `02-DATA-MODEL.md` gives `attributes` to user-level fields — brand, purchase price — so a `coerced` list at the top level would read as their sibling, and replacing the column on a retag would eat them. The write is a spread rather than a mutation for a second reason: `JSONB` is not mutation-tracked, so `item.attributes["tagging"] = …` flushes nothing at all. `prompt_version` is written on the **failed** path too: which prompt could not read a photograph is the same question as which prompt read one, and a 1.11 baseline that records only successes measures a biased sample. `coerced` is `[]` when nothing was discarded and absent when there was no accepted answer to discard from — once 1.4's retag rewrites this column, an absent key and "nothing was discarded" stop being the same claim.

**The sweep keys on `updated_at`, and that is only correct because of the `onupdate` fix.** `created_at` looks equivalent today and breaks at 1.4: `retag` puts a week-old row back to `processing`, and a `created_at`-keyed sweep would call it abandoned on the next boot before its task had run. `updated_at` is the age of the *processing state*, which is what the ten minutes measures. **The two halves are one decision. Neither survives the other's removal** — delete `onupdate` and the sweep reads insert times and fails live rows; key on `created_at` and the `onupdate` fix stops mattering here and 1.4 inherits both problems.

**Ten minutes rather than "everything still `processing` at boot".** The stronger rule is defensible on its own terms: a `BackgroundTask` dies with its process, `07-DEPLOYMENT.md` runs one instance with no `--workers`, so at startup no task can be in flight and every `processing` row is orphaned. It is rejected because this project routinely has a second process against the same database — a local `uvicorn` and the Render instance read the same `DATABASE_URL`, and the free tier sleeps after fifteen minutes so boots are frequent. Without the threshold, starting the dev server fails work in flight in production. Ten minutes clears the worst case a live task can take, which is two OpenAI calls at a thirty-second timeout.

**One broad catch, and it is the one `CONVENTIONS.md` now carves out.** `_tagged` ends in `except Exception`, after the four specific types, because an exception escaping a `BackgroundTask` leaves the row `processing` until a boot up to ten minutes away — the sweep is a backstop, not a handler. The specific branches log a warning; the broad one logs a traceback, because reaching it means the list above it is incomplete. `openai.OpenAIError` is named among the specific ones rather than left to the backstop, so a provider timeout is a warning and not a stack trace; every documented openai exception subclasses it, checked against the installed 2.52 rather than taken from the documentation. The cost is that `tagging.py` imports `openai` while `vision.py` is "the project's only path to OpenAI" — that claim is about who *calls* the provider, and it still holds.

**Trade-off accepted:** **A batch tags serially.** Starlette awaits one response's background tasks in order, so twenty files take twenty tagging runs end to end. `asyncio.gather` would fix it and is not built: `STAGE-1` says one task per item, parallelism multiplies concurrent OpenAI calls, and five photographs — the acceptance criterion — comfortably fit. Recorded at 1.7, where the polling that will notice it gets built. **`onupdate` is Core-level, not a trigger**, so it applies to ORM flushes and `update()` statements and not to raw `text()` SQL or anything typed into `psql`; database-level truth is a trigger and its own migration. **The task takes a `session_factory` that production never passes**, which is a parameter existing for tests — accepted because the alternative is a task that cannot be bound to the transaction `conftest.py` rolls back, and would therefore commit real rows out of a test run. And **`error_message` is not cleared on a successful write**, so 1.4's retag of a failed row must clear it; that is recorded in `STAGE-1` 1.4 rather than pre-built here, because at 1.3 a row is only ever `processing` when this runs and the line would be dead code.

## 089 — One validator, two policies, and the message that has to be re-framed between them

**Decision:** `PATCH /items/{id}` merges the request over the stored row, clears `CATEGORY_DEPENDENT_FIELDS` **before** validating when the category changes, and answers `422` on any `errors` **or** any `coerced` entry. `ItemUpdate` types every field permissively — `str`, `int`, `bool` — with `extra="forbid"` and a stripped, non-empty `display_name`; an empty body is a `422`. `DELETE` archives idempotently and answers `200` with the full item; retag answers `202` with `status: "processing"`, `409` `item_edited` when `user_edited` is true and `?force=true` was not passed, and leaves the tag columns alone. `user_edited` is never cleared. `_store` clears `error_message` on the success path. `GET /items/stats` is declared above `/{item_id}` and excludes archived rows.
**Alternative:** Typing `ItemUpdate` with the vocabulary enums. Validating the request alone, as `04` described it. Clearing after validation and classifying the coercions. Reporting the cleared fields in a third key. Clearing `user_edited` on a forced retag. Nulling the tag columns at enqueue. A second `409` for a retag against a `processing` row. `never_worn = total`.

**Reasoning — the two callers of `validate_tag_dict` differ in one respect: whether there is anyone to tell.** The function applies no policy. It returns `errors`, `coerced` and the resulting `tags`, and the caller decides what a failure means. The vision path accepts coercions because the source is a model and there is nobody to ask — rejecting fifteen fields over one trouser word would throw away fourteen good tags — so the record goes to the log and to `attributes["tagging"]`. `PATCH` refuses them because the source is a person with a form open, and a `200` whose body differs from what was typed is the worst answer an editing UI can give. That is why the report's shape is the right primitive: a validator returning a boolean could not serve both, and a validator that applied policy could serve only one.

**The clearing happens before validation, and that ordering is load-bearing.** With it, `PATCH {"category": "shoes"}` on a pair of jeans never produces a coercion at all: the five impossible values are gone before `validate_tag_dict` sees the dict, so **every** coercion that fires concerns a value this request actually sent, and "any coercion is a `422`" is a rule with no exceptions. Clearing afterwards would mean sorting coercions into "caused by the stored row" and "caused by the request" — a `kind` on `TagIssue`, which is exactly the field 086 refused to add and 1.2a's literal pins would have to be reopened for.

**A message written for one policy is wrong under the other, and nothing had noticed.** The vocabulary's coercion reasons end *", set to null"* — accurate for the vision path, false in a `422`, where nothing was set to anything and the request was refused. Reusing one validator across two policies has this cost and it is not visible until both callers exist. The route cuts the clause rather than the vocabulary dropping it, because the clause is true where it was written and the log and `attributes` record still want it. `test_the_422_does_not_claim_the_value_was_set_to_null` is what fails if someone passes `issue.reason` straight through.

**`ItemUpdate` is typed permissively so that one function decides what a value means.** Typing `category: Category` would answer membership in Pydantic's words and category-dependence in the vocabulary's, for the same field, in two different message shapes. The line drawn instead is **Pydantic owns shape, the vocabulary owns meaning**: an unknown key, a value of the wrong kind, a blank `display_name`. `extra="forbid"` is not decoration — `validate_tag_dict` only inspects keys it knows, so `PATCH {"colour_primary": "navy"}` would otherwise be a `200` that changed nothing (030). The `display_name` rule is the one narrowing: the validator checks only `isinstance(str)`, and a blank name is a tile with nothing written on it — the one failure in the set a user sees rather than a developer (086).

**The clearing stays unannounced, and "silent" overstates what it is.** 030 accepted no explicit warning and put the mitigation in 1.9's editor. That holds, with one clarification worth having: because the response is the full `ItemResponse`, the five nulls are in the body the client just received. It is unannounced, not hidden. A `{"item": …, "cleared": [...]}` envelope would break one-shape-for-one-resource to report a fact already present in the shape.

**`user_edited` is never cleared, and the cost is stated in `02` rather than reconstructed.** Clearing it on a successful retag is tempting — the values are then the model's and the guard protects nothing. It is refused because `02` gives the column a second, historical job that a reset destroys; because doing it in `_store` would fire on the upload path, where it is a no-op today and a silent eraser the moment anything else writes; and because it opens a race in which a `PATCH` against a still-`processing` row is overwritten by the background task *and* loses the flag saying an edit happened. The accepted cost: a hand-corrected item needs `force` on every later retag, forever.

**Retag leaves the tag columns alone, and allows a re-enqueue against a `processing` row.** A retag that fails writes `failed` without touching the tags, so a previously-good item keeps what it had — nulling them at enqueue would destroy good data to make a failure look tidier, and `02`'s "all NULL until tagging completes" is narrowed to say so. Allowing the re-enqueue costs a fraction of a cent and a last-write-wins race between two tasks on one row. Refusing costs a row whose owning process died sitting `processing` until the sweep clears it, up to ten minutes, with the one action that fixes it unavailable for exactly that window — the worse failure, and the one a user actually meets. `?force=true` keeps its single meaning from `04`.

**Trade-off accepted:** **A `PATCH` against a `processing` row can be overwritten seconds later and nothing detects it.** The task holds no lock and checks no status. Documented in `STAGE-1` at both 1.4 and 1.9 rather than guarded, because the guard that matters is not opening the editor on a processing tile, which `05` already does not. **The `db.commit()` before `add_task` is not defended by a test** and cannot be under `conftest.py`'s fixtures, where the route's session *is* the test's and the task is recorded rather than run; named in `STAGE-1` 1.4. **`by_category` and `by_color` do not sum to `total`**, because rows with a `null` tag are counted in one and not the others, and a category with no items is absent rather than zero — zero-filling is the client's. And **`never_worn` reports `0` when the honest arithmetic says `total`**: with no wear columns until migration `0003`, every item is unworn, and a number that is correct today and changes meaning when the columns land is worse than a placeholder `04` already prints.

## 090 — The empty state ships its call to action inert; the wardrobe ships no FAB

**Decision:** `wardrobe.page.ts`'s empty state renders **Add your first items** as a real `<button>` with no click handler, which task 1.6 wires to the upload sheet. The floating **+ Add items** button in `05-FRONTEND-SPEC.md`'s wardrobe mockup is not built at 1.5 at all.
**Alternative:** Build both, neither, or gate the CTA behind a `disabled` attribute.
**Reasoning:** These two look like the same case and are not, and the distinction is worth stating because it will recur at every screen split across tasks. **The test is ownership, not affordance.** `STAGE-1` 1.5's acceptance line requires the empty state and its call to action, so the CTA is inside this task's brief and shipping the state without its button would fail the task on its own terms — leaving it inert for one task is the smaller cost, and it is what makes the empty state reviewable at all. No task requires a FAB at 1.5; it appears only in a mockup that spans three stages, so building one would be adding an affordance nobody asked for, which is the thing `CLAUDE.md` forbids. `disabled` was rejected because it is a claim about state — it tells a user the action is unavailable *right now*, which is false; the action does not exist yet, and a control that says "not now" when it means "not built" is a worse lie than one that does nothing.
**Trade-off accepted:** For exactly one task there is a button on screen that swallows a click silently, and a user testing 1.5 by hand will press it. Named in `STAGE-1` 1.6, which owns closing it. The same reasoning will be needed again at 2.2 (weather strip) and 1.8 (filter bar), both of which appear in this screen's mockup and neither of which 1.5 builds.

## 091 — A processing tile keeps its photograph, and `05`'s grid legend is amended to say so

**Decision:** An item whose `status` is `processing` renders its `image_url` at reduced opacity with a "Tagging…" label over it. It is not replaced by a blank skeleton. `05-FRONTEND-SPEC.md`'s grid legend, which read `▓▓▓▓ = skeleton, status processing`, is corrected in the same commit.
**Alternative:** Ship the skeleton the legend specified.
**Reasoning:** The legend was written before the response shape was settled and assumes the client has nothing to draw. It has: `image_url` is computed from `image_public_id` (`DECISIONS.md` 050), and the `public_id` exists from the moment `POST /items/upload` answers — so the photograph is on the wire in the *first* response, before any tagging has happened. Rendering a grey block instead means the picture a user has just taken visibly disappears and is replaced by a placeholder, which reads as the upload having failed. The same document argues the other way three sections later: `05` §3 requires local previews from `URL.createObjectURL` and says the returned rows replace them, and 1.6's own text says the user should never see an empty screen while uploading. A skeleton is the right control for content that does not exist yet; here it would be hiding content that does.
**Trade-off accepted:** The three tile states are distinguished by opacity and an overlaid label rather than by silhouette, so they are less distinct at a glance across a full grid than a skeleton would be — and opacity alone is not an accessible signal, which is why each non-ready state also carries text. Two documents disagreed and one has been changed rather than left to be rediscovered; if the skeleton is ever wanted back, this entry is the argument to overturn.

## 092 — The frontend branches on the error `code`, and `item_edited` is the first one it reads

**Decision:** `wardrobe.store.ts` chooses its retag failure message by reading `error.error.code === 'item_edited'`, not by reading `error.status === 409`. Anything else, including a body with no code at all, falls to the general message.
**Alternative:** Branch on the status, which is what `login.page.ts` does for its `401`.
**Reasoning:** `CONVENTIONS.md` describes the codes as "stable strings the frontend can branch on", and until now nothing branched on one — every frontend error path read a status. The 2026-08-18 audit's O-1 is the reason it matters: `item_edited` was named at task 1.4 specifically to give this branch something to read, and the audit's argument for striking `tagging_failed` was that **a code with no producer invites a branch that can never be taken**. The mirror image is a producer with no consumer, which is what `item_edited` would have been. The status would work here — `409` has exactly one meaning on this endpoint — and that is precisely why it is the weaker choice: it works by coincidence of there being one conflict, and the coincidence is not written down anywhere. The login precedent stands because it predates any code being defined for that path.
**Trade-off accepted:** A response that carries the right status and a missing or misspelled body key degrades to the general message rather than the specific one, silently. That is the intended failure direction — a user reads "we couldn't start tagging again" instead of the precise reason — but it means a backend typo in a `code` string is invisible from the frontend, and nothing compares the two lists. The same hand-mirrored-constant problem `CONVENTIONS.md` already records for the upload limits and the password rules, with the same absence of a check.

## 093 — Retag state is per item, and the binding is what needed the test

**Decision:** `WardrobeStore` carries `retrying: Signal<ReadonlySet<string>>` and `retagErrors: Signal<ReadonlyMap<string, string>>`, both keyed by item id, so a spinner and a failure message land on the tile that owns them. Two page-level tests assert placement across two failing tiles.
**Alternative:** One `isRetrying` boolean and one `retagError` string for the whole screen.
**Reasoning:** The global version is about eight lines shorter and tells a user that something failed without telling them which garment — on a grid where several tiles can fail at once, from an upload where each item tagged separately, that is not a message. What is worth recording is not the choice but the **evidence**, because the store-level tests turned out to defend almost none of it. Two mutations were run against the delivered code: binding the spinner to `store.retrying().size > 0` instead of `.has(item.id)`, and giving every tile the first entry in the error map instead of its own. **Both survived the entire suite** — 108 tests, all green, with the per-item claim broken in the template. The store was thoroughly tested and the *binding* was not tested at all, and every test in the file that looked like it covered this used a single item, where a global signal and a per-item one are indistinguishable. Two tests now render two failing tiles and assert both the presence and the absence.
**Trade-off accepted:** Two collections where one boolean would do, and both must be kept in step with the item list — nothing removes an entry when an item disappears from the wardrobe, so a `retagErrors` key can outlive its row until the next `load()` replaces the collection. Harmless while the map is only ever read by id from a rendered tile; it would become a leak if anything ever iterated it.

## 094 — One page of 200, no pagination, and the header states the server's total

**Decision:** `WardrobeStore.load()` sends `limit=200` — `04-API-SPEC.md`'s documented maximum — and never sends `offset`. The header renders `total` from the response rather than the number of loaded rows.
**Alternative:** Take the default of 100; paginate; or render `items().length` in the header.
**Reasoning:** The explicit limit is required rather than chosen: `05-FRONTEND-SPEC.md` filters client-side over the loaded collection, the parameter defaults to 100, and `01-ARCHITECTURE.md` sizes a realistic wardrobe at 80–150 — so taking the default silently filters over the first hundred items and reports wrong counts with no error anywhere. Both ends of that are now defended by backend tests at 1.5 (`test_the_list_defaults_to_a_hundred_items`, `test_the_limit_cap_is_two_hundred_and_over_it_is_rejected`), because the parameter's behaviour is the frontend's assumption and nothing asserted it. The header takes `total` because `total` counts the filter rather than the page, so above 200 items it is the only truthful number on the screen.
**Trade-off accepted:** **A wardrobe above 200 items shows the newest 200 and says nothing about the rest.** `05-FRONTEND-SPEC.md`'s "the whole wardrobe fits in memory" stops being true there, and the header will state a count larger than the number of tiles, which reads as a rendering bug rather than as a page boundary. Nothing this project ships reaches it — the demo seed is 64 items (1.10) — and the fix is `offset` plus either a second request or a "load more", which no task owns. Recorded rather than built, and named here so the first person to see a 250-item wardrobe knows where to look.

## 095 — `I18nService` has no plural rule, so the caller picks the key

**Decision:** Counts render through two keys — `wardrobe.count.one` and `wardrobe.count.other`, `wardrobe.tagging.one` and `wardrobe.tagging.other` — with the component choosing between them. `I18nService` is unchanged.
**Alternative:** Add plural support to the service, or accept `"{{count}} items"` for every count.
**Reasoning:** `DECISIONS.md` 058 built a forty-line service with interpolation and nothing else, and this is the first screen with a count in it, so the gap surfaced here rather than being predicted. One string reads "1 items", which is a visible defect on the wardrobe of anyone who has photographed exactly one garment — and on the tagging line, which passes through 1 on its way to empty every single time a batch finishes. Adding a plural rule to the service is the wrong size of change for this task and the wrong shape for the project: real pluralisation is per-locale (`Intl.PluralRules` has six categories, and Hebrew, which 012 names as the next locale, uses more of them than English), so a rule written for English now would have to be replaced rather than extended. Two keys and a ternary is what ngx-translate's escape hatch (058) would replace cleanly.
**Trade-off accepted:** Every count in the application needs two keys and a conditional at the call site, and nothing enforces the pairing — a `.other` key with no `.one` beside it renders as its own key name on exactly one value of the count, which is the hardest case to notice. If counts spread past a handful of screens, this is the point to reconsider and adopt `Intl.PluralRules` inside the service.

## 096 — The stale `register` test is deleted rather than repaired

**Decision:** `auth.service.spec.ts`'s `accepts a null display_name at the service boundary` is removed. Nothing replaces it.
**Alternative:** Repair it — cast the `null`, or widen `AuthService.register` back to `string | null`.
**Reasoning:** The test asserted that the service forwards a `null` display name to `POST /auth/register`. That behaviour was deliberately removed at task 0.10: `DECISIONS.md` 072 tightened `RegisterRequest.display_name` to a stripped, non-empty `str`, and commit `21e0f36` narrowed `AuthService.register`'s parameter to `string` in the same change — but left the test behind, where it has been a **type error** ever since. Widening the service back would recreate exactly the mismatch 072 exists to close, in the direction 072 calls out: an API looser than its only client. Repairing the test with a cast would keep a test whose subject no longer exists. **"Nothing is lost by deleting it" was checked rather than asserted, because the two tests are not equivalent and the first version of this entry implied they were.** They differ twice: the deleted test covers `null` where the standing one covers a trimmed string, and it calls the service directly where the standing one goes through the page. The first difference is the dead behaviour and is not recoverable by design. The second was measured — three mutations to `auth.service.ts`'s register body, run from a pristine copy against the full suite:

```
display_name: -> displayName:      1 failed  RegisterPage > posts the trimmed display name with the other two fields
display_name dropped entirely      1 failed  RegisterPage > posts the trimmed display name with the other two fields
posts to /auth/login instead       6 failed  including that test
```

Every mutation the deleted test could have caught is caught by a named test one layer up, so the service's body-building is defended transitively and not merely nominally. **A replacement service-boundary test was offered and declined on that evidence:** a test that duplicates a proven assertion costs suite time and buys no coverage. The reasoning is recorded here rather than only the removal, so that a later reader does not find a deleted test with no argument beside it and restore it.
**Trade-off accepted:** The suite loses one test and drops from 72 to 71 pre-existing tests. The larger finding is not this decision but what let it stand for four commits, and it is recorded in `AUDITS.md` as **O-13** rather than here: `ng test` is the only command in this project that type-checks a `.spec.ts` file, and it is in no pipeline, because `.github/workflows/` is empty and the CI `06-TESTING-STRATEGY.md` describes runs `ng lint` and `ng build` — neither of which reads spec files at all.

## 097 — Previews are their own collection, and nothing is ever a synthetic `Item`

**Decision:** `WardrobeStore` gains `pending: Signal<readonly PendingUpload[]>` — a local key, an object URL and a filename — rendered by `PendingStrip` **above** the grid. The returned rows go into `items` and the strip empties. No `Item` is ever fabricated for a file that has not been uploaded.
**Alternative:** Build synthetic `Item` objects with invented ids and `status: 'processing'` so the previews sit in grid position, which is what `05-FRONTEND-SPEC.md` §3's "replace the previews with the returned rows" literally describes.
**Reasoning:** `item.model.ts` mirrors `ItemResponse` field for field and `DECISIONS.md` 059 is explicit that the server's names are kept precisely because one shape means one source of truth. A synthetic row needs an `id` and a `short_id` that no server issued, on the one model whose contract is that everything in it came off the wire — and `track item.id` would then key the grid on a fabricated value. The cost is downstream rather than local: 1.7's polling filters on `status`, 1.8's filters run over `items()`, and both would have to learn that some members of that collection are not rows. A separate collection makes the boundary a type rather than a convention. The filename in `PendingUpload` is not decoration — it is the only thing distinguishing one preview from another to a screen reader, and the only thing an oversized-file message can name.
**Where it lives:** `PendingUpload` is declared in `wardrobe.store.ts`, not beside `PendingStrip` as first planned. `core/` imports nothing from `features/` anywhere in this project — twenty imports run the other way and none run back — and putting the type next to its component would have made the store the first inversion, for a type alias. Keeping it out of `item.model.ts` was the part that mattered; where it went instead was decided by the import graph.
**Trade-off accepted:** For a second or two the newest garments sit in a strip above the grid rather than in grid position, and the replacement is a visual swap rather than an in-place morph. `05` §3 is amended to say so. A later task that wants true in-grid previews is overturning this entry, not filling a gap.

## 098 — The sheet is a plain element because the test environment cannot open a `<dialog>`

**Decision:** `upload-sheet.ts` is a `div` positioned over the page with `role="dialog"`, shown and hidden by an `@if` on the parent. It is not a `<dialog>`. Separately: **a camera capture leaves the sheet open and a gallery pick closes it.**
**Alternative:** `<dialog>` with `showModal()`, which is the correct platform element and brings focus trapping and `Esc` for free.
**Reasoning:** Measured at 1.6 against the installed jsdom 28.1.0: `HTMLDialogElement` exists, and `showModal`, `show` and `close` are all `undefined` — only the `open` content attribute works. A dialog-based sheet therefore cannot be opened by any test in this project, which would put the entire component behind the by-eye pass at exactly the moment `AUDITS.md` O-14 is arguing that half the previous screen was never looked at. **This is a testability constraint changing a design decision, and it is recorded as a decision rather than left as a preference** — the platform element is the better one, and it is not being rejected on its merits. The two costs are real and are paid by hand: focus is not trapped and `Esc` does not dismiss, so the backdrop and an explicit **Done** button are the two ways out.
**On the open/close asymmetry:** `STAGE-1` 1.6 and `05` §3 both specify only the camera — *"the sheet stays open after a camera capture so the next garment can be shot immediately"* — and neither says what the gallery does. The gallery is `05` §3's "wardrobe-filling path": after picking twenty photographs the user's next move is watching them arrive, and a sheet covering the grid hides precisely that. One rule for both inputs would be easier to explain and wrong for one of them.
**Trade-off accepted:** A hand-rolled modal that a browser would have given us, with the two accessibility behaviours it would have brought. If `browsers` is ever configured on `@angular/build:unit-test` — the builder supports it — this entry is the argument to revisit, because the reason for it disappears.

## 099 — Upload failures are keyed by `code`, and the filename in `detail` is not rendered

**Decision:** `wardrobe.store.ts` maps `unsupported_file_type`, `file_too_large`, `upload_failed` and `validation_error` to four i18n keys and falls back to a general message for anything else, including a body with no `code`. The server's `detail` string is never rendered.
**Alternative:** Render `detail`, which `04-API-SPEC.md` deliberately fills with the offending filename.
**Reasoning:** 092 settled the branch — the documented code, never the status — and this is that rule's second application, with four codes instead of one. What is new is the conflict it exposes, and it is a genuine one between two documents. `04-API-SPEC.md` line 100 puts the filename inside `detail` *so that a client can show it*; `CONVENTIONS.md` says the frontend never renders a raw error and every failure path has a written message. `05-FRONTEND-SPEC.md` §3 specifies no error surface for this sheet at all, so neither document was overruled by a third. The rule wins because it is the general one and because `detail` is not a translatable string — it is English, assembled server-side, and 012 names Hebrew as the next locale.
**Trade-off accepted:** **A `415` on one file out of twelve cannot say which file.** The message reads "One of those files isn't a JPEG, PNG, WebP or HEIC photo, so none of them were uploaded", which is true and less useful than the server's answer. This is reachable in practice, because `accept` is a hint the user can override and the server identifies type from the file's own bytes rather than its extension. The honest fix is a `field`-style key on the error envelope, which `04-API-SPEC.md` explicitly refuses ("there is no third key"), so it is recorded here rather than worked around.

## 100 — The `202` carries no `total`, so the client moves its own count

**Decision:** on a successful upload, `WardrobeStore` prepends the returned rows to `items` and increments `total` by `response.items.length`.
**Alternative:** re-issue `GET /items` after the upload, or render `items().length` in the header.
**Reasoning:** 094 put the server's `total` in the header because it counts the filter rather than the page and is therefore the only truthful number above 200 items. `ItemUploadResponse` is `{ items }` and nothing else — verified against `app/schemas/item.py` — so nothing in the `202` moves that number, and a header left alone silently understates by the size of every batch. Re-loading would be a second round trip that also discards the strip-to-grid continuity for no gain. Prepending rather than appending follows from `GET /items` ordering `created_at DESC, short_id`: these are the newest rows, and appending would drop a fresh upload below a hundred older items, which reads as the upload having done nothing.
**Trade-off accepted:** The count is now maintained in two places — the server's on load, ours on upload — and they can disagree if a request partially succeeds. `04-API-SPEC.md` forecloses that particular drift, because a batch is all-or-nothing and a failure rolls the rows back. 094's ceiling is unchanged and slightly worsened: uploading into a 200-item wardrobe pushes `items()` past the page size, so the grid holds rows that a reload would not return.

## 101 — The upload limits are a third hand-written mirror, and the mutation run found the test was worthless

**Decision:** `upload-sheet.ts` declares `MAX_FILES_PER_REQUEST = 20` and `MAX_UPLOAD_BYTES = 10 * 1024 * 1024`, checks the whole selection before posting, and refuses the entire batch for one bad file.
**Alternative:** post and let the server reject; or filter the offending files out and send the rest.
**Reasoning:** `05-FRONTEND-SPEC.md` requires the check because the server rejects the **whole** request for one bad file, so an unchecked selection costs the user twenty uploads to be told about one. Refusing the whole selection rather than filtering is the same reasoning applied to ourselves: a sheet that quietly sent nineteen of twenty files would behave differently from the API it is guarding, and the user would have to notice a missing garment to find out.
**The finding is about the test, not the code.** `CONVENTIONS.md` has recorded since 0.8 that nothing compares a Python setting to a TypeScript constant. What nobody had noticed is that nothing was comparing the TypeScript constant to *anything*: every size expectation in `upload-sheet.spec.ts` was written in terms of `MAX_UPLOAD_BYTES`, imported from the module under test. A mutation changing the arithmetic to `1000 * 1000` moved the expectations with it and **all 155 tests stayed green** — the mebibyte/megabyte error this project has a whole section about, undetected by the file that exists to detect it. The number is now transcribed as a literal from `CONVENTIONS.md`'s own sentence and one test pins the constant to it.
**Trade-off accepted:** The drift that is actually detectable is unit confusion, not divergence from `config.py`. If someone changes `MAX_UPLOAD_MB` to 15 on the server, the sheet still says 10 and nothing anywhere notices. That remains true, and `CONVENTIONS.md` still names the only real fix: an endpoint that publishes the limits.

## 102 — The poll is two requests, and the second one is why a tile ever shows a tag

**Decision:** the loop sends `GET /items?status=processing&limit=200` every two seconds, compares the **ids** in the answer against the ids it was waiting for, and re-issues the full `GET /items?limit=200` whenever one of them is missing. The second request is what writes `items` and `total`.
**Alternative:** the single filtered poll all three documents described; a `GET /items/{id}` per finished item; or polling the unfiltered list and deriving `processing` client-side.
**Reasoning:** `01-ARCHITECTURE.md`, `05-FRONTEND-SPEC.md` and `STAGE-1` 1.7 all specified one request, and `STAGE-1`'s acceptance criterion requires *"All 5 become `ready` with correct-looking tags … no page refresh"* while `06-TESTING-STRATEGY.md`'s third E2E journey states it outright — *"polling resolves, tiles show `display_name`"*. **A body filtered to `status=processing` contains only rows that are still processing**, verified against the route: it can say an item has left the set and can never say what it became. One request cannot satisfy both sentences, and the documents disagreed with each other rather than with the code. Per-id `GET`s would answer fastest and turn one poll into up to twenty requests, which is the opposite of 007's argument for polling at all. Dropping the filter would be the least code and would leave the `status` filter — a behaviour `STAGE-1` assigns to this very task — with no consumer anywhere in the project. Ids rather than a count, because a batch landing while the first finishes leaves the count unchanged with the membership entirely different, and the reload would never fire.
**Trade-off accepted:** tags arrive one poll late, bounded by the two seconds 007 already called invisible, and a finishing batch costs two round trips instead of one. The reload replaces the whole collection, so an upload committed *after* the reload's request went out but before its answer came back is wiped from the grid until the next reload — a window of one round trip, on a path where the user has just watched their previews turn into tiles. Not guarded, because the next poll restores it and a guard means reconciling two collections by id on every response.

## 103 — The run object is the guard, and there is no polling boolean

**Decision:** `WardrobeStore` holds one private nullable field — the run — carrying the deadline, the timer handle and the in-flight subscription. `run === null` is what "not polling" means. The effect starts a run when there is none and stops it when nothing is awaited.
**Alternative:** the effect in `05-FRONTEND-SPEC.md`'s sketch, which has no guard at all; or a boolean beside the handle.
**Reasoning:** the sketch calls `startPolling()` on every run of an effect that reads `processing()` — and `processing()` is computed from `items()`, which every poll response changes, so it would start a new loop on every response. Something has to say "already running".
