A boolean is two facts about one state kept in step by hand, and the failure mode is a flag that says false while a timer is still armed. **A run cannot exist without the timer and request it owns**, so the two cannot drift: one place creates it, one destroys it. Keeping the deadline on the run rather than in a second timer means the hard stop is a comparison rather than a race between two timers.
**Trade-off accepted:** a mutable object held in a field, in a store that is otherwise signals throughout — deliberately not a signal, because nothing renders it and a signal would make every timer reschedule a change notification. `05`'s sketch is left in place and annotated rather than replaced, so the gap between the shape that was designed and the shape that ships stays visible.

## 104 — The loop is re-armed after each response settles, not run from an interval

**Decision:** the next poll is scheduled when the current step finishes — including its reload, if one fired — rather than by a fixed 2-second interval.
**Alternative:** `setInterval(2000)` or `rxjs.interval(2000)`, which is what "every 2 seconds" reads as.
**Reasoning:** a fixed interval against a server that answers slower than the interval stacks requests, and the first thing that breaks is the reload decision, which compares the answer against what was awaited *when that request went out*. Re-arming after the response makes "one poll in flight" a property of the loop rather than a hope about latency — and it is what lets `HttpTestingController.verify()` mean anything in the tests, since a stacking loop leaves requests outstanding that no test flushed.
**Trade-off accepted:** the real spacing is 2 seconds **plus** the round trip, so on a cold Render instance the loop is slower than the documents say. That is the correct direction to be wrong in: the alternative is more requests to a server that is already struggling. The 3-minute deadline is measured in wall-clock time and does not stretch with it, so a very slow server reaches the hard stop after fewer polls.

## 105 — Giving up is a collection beside `items`, and it is not a status

**Decision:** when the deadline passes, the ids still being waited for go into `stoppedWaiting: Signal<ReadonlySet<string>>` and the run stops. Nothing in `items` is written. `ItemCard` renders the state from `status === 'processing' && stoppedWaiting`, and the string is **"We stopped waiting. It may still finish."** with no `--color-danger`.
**Alternative:** `STAGE-1` 1.7 and `05-FRONTEND-SPEC.md` both say to mark the rest **failed in the UI**, which read literally means writing `status: 'failed'` onto those rows.
**Reasoning:** a client-written `status` is a row no server issued, inside the one collection whose contract is that everything in it came off the wire (059, 097). The cost is downstream and concrete: 1.8 filters `items()` and 1.9 opens an editor from it, and both would inherit a row whose `status` no `PATCH` can reconcile. 097 refused a synthetic `Item` for a preview; this is the same boundary arriving by a second door, and a `Set` of ids beside `retrying` and `retagErrors` (093) is a shape this store already uses twice. **The copy is the other half of the decision and not decoration.** The server still calls the row `processing` and 1.3's startup sweep will not touch it for another seven minutes, so it may well still be tagging — "Tagging failed" would be a false statement about the database, and 057 reserves the danger token for something being wrong. The tile keeps the retry, which is a genuine re-queue: `04-API-SPEC.md` allows a retag against a `processing` row and answers `202`.
**Trade-off accepted:** the screen and the database disagree for up to seven minutes by design, and the tile says so rather than hiding it. A `stoppedWaiting` id outlives its row until the next `load()` — the conjunctive read is what makes that harmless, exactly as 093 accepted for `retagErrors` — and `retag` prunes its own id on success, without which the retry on an abandoned tile would do nothing visible.

## 106 — The poll never writes `total`, and a failed poll is not news

**Decision:** the poll's response is read for ids only. `total` is written by the reload and by `load()`, never by the poll. An error on either request is swallowed: no `loadError`, no banner, and the loop keeps its schedule until the deadline.
**Alternative:** write the poll's `total`; stop on the first error and show `wardrobe.error.load`; count consecutive failures and stop after N.
**Reasoning:** `04-API-SPEC.md` says `total` *counts the filter, not the page* — and this request's filter is `processing`, so its total is the size of the batch. 094 put the server's number in the header because it is the only truthful one above 200 items and 100 has the client move it on upload, which already makes two writers; a third, meaning something else entirely, would drop the header to "3 items" mid-batch and back again, which reads as a count bug. **No document ruled either way**, which is why this is written down rather than assumed. On the error: `04` documents no failure codes for `GET /items`, so 092's rule — branch on the code — has nothing to read here, and the deployment target is Render's free tier, where a cold start answers slowly or not at all. A red banner over a grid that is rendering correctly is the worse of the two wrong answers, and the deadline already bounds a poll that never succeeds.
**Trade-off accepted:** a wardrobe whose server is genuinely down looks identical to one that is tagging slowly, for three minutes, and then every tile says we stopped waiting. That is the intended failure direction and it is not obviously right — the counter-argument is that a user with no network would rather be told. It is reachable and unmeasured, and 1.9's item detail is the next screen that could change it.

## 107 — The loop is stopped by the page, and the tab being hidden is not our business

**Decision:** `WardrobePage` registers `DestroyRef.onDestroy(() => store.stopPolling())`. Nothing listens to `visibilitychange`, `blur`, `pagehide` or `online`.
**Alternative:** pause the loop while the tab is hidden and resume on return; or reload the wardrobe on return rather than resuming.
**Reasoning:** the two halves are not the same claim. **Stopping on destroy is not optional**: `WardrobeStore` is `providedIn: 'root'` and outlives every component, so a run nobody stops keeps polling behind whatever screen comes next — and from 1.9 there will be one. Visibility handling is an optimisation for a throttle the browser is already applying more aggressively than we would, no document asks for it, and — the part worth recording — **the gate cannot tell us whether it works.** Measured at 1.7: `document.visibilityState` is overridable and `visibilitychange` dispatches, so a test would pass; it would prove that our listener runs when we dispatch the event ourselves, which is the same weak claim 1.6's object-URL row is recorded as. Building a behaviour whose only evidence is a test asserting against its own stimulus is worse than not building it, because the green tick is what stops anyone checking.
**Trade-off accepted:** a backgrounded tab keeps a loop armed and keeps sending requests at whatever rate the browser allows, and the 3-minute deadline runs down in wall-clock time whether or not the user is looking. On a phone that means a batch started and backgrounded can come back abandoned. Reachable, cheap to fix if it turns out to matter, and named here so it is a decision rather than an oversight.

## 108 — A batch arriving mid-run gets its own three minutes

**Decision:** the run remembers every id it has waited for, and the deadline is pushed out to `now + 3 minutes` whenever an id appears that it has not seen before.
**Alternative:** one deadline per run, set when the run starts, so items arriving mid-run inherit whatever is left of it.
**Reasoning:** the arithmetic decides it. 1.3 tags serially — `STAGE-1` 1.7 says so — at roughly 2–4 seconds an item, so two full 20-file batches are around 160 seconds of tagging against a 180-second budget measured from the first. That is not a margin. And **back-to-back batches are the designed path rather than an unlucky one**: 098 keeps the sheet open after a camera capture precisely so the next garment can be shot immediately, which is the flow `STAGE-1` 1.6 exists to build. Under one deadline the loop abandons items that are tagging perfectly well and the user reads it as a bug, because it is one. The tracking is close to free — the run already holds state, and the id comparison the reload decision performs each step is a different set, cumulative against instantaneous, so the two are deliberately not shared.
**Trade-off accepted:** a `Set` that grows for the life of a run, and a wardrobe where someone uploads a photograph every two minutes never reaches a deadline at all. That is the correct behaviour for a user who is actively adding clothes, and the loop still stops the moment the set empties. Someone will meet the `seen` set later and want to simplify it into the deadline; this entry is why not.

## 109 — A null tag is an unknown, not a non-match, and the predicate never reads `status`

**Decision:** `applyFilters` admits a row whose value **for the filtered field** is `null`. The rule is per field, not per row: a filter on `color_primary` tests only `color_primary`, and a row whose colour is null passes it while still being filtered on its category. `status` does not appear in the function.
**Alternative:** State the rule on status — "processing rows are exempt" — or apply it per row, exempting only a row with no tags at all.
**Reasoning:** A null tag is not a garment that fails the test; it is a garment nobody has described yet, and excluding it asserts something we do not know. C4 is what the alternative costs in practice: 1.6 renders a local preview outside the filter (097), the row that replaces it carries every tag null, and a filter stated on membership alone removes it — so the user watches an upload appear and vanish, which reads as a lost upload rather than as a filter. **Per field rather than per row was decided by a row that is a week away.** `ItemUpdate` types every field `str | None` and the route merges with `exclude_unset=True`, so `PATCH {"color_primary": null}` is *supplied* and `validate_tag_dict` skips `None` on a strict field: a `ready` item can carry one null beside four real tags, and 1.9's editor is the next task. Under a per-row reading that hand-cleared item is excluded by a filter on "black", which is the same false assertion one level down.
**What falls out of it rather than being provided by it:** the stopped-waiting tile is never hidden, because its tags really are null. That matters because 105 leaves the retag on that tile as the only way out of the state, and 1.9 is where the second one arrives. Anyone later simplifying this rule to read `status` should know that is what they would be removing — the sentence is here for that reader.
**Also settled here, and declined rather than deferred:** dimensions combine as **AND**, and category and colour are **single-valued** — tapping the selected chip or swatch clears it. Multi-select is not in 1.8's brief; it costs an OR nested inside the AND, comma parsing in the URL, and a combinatorial test surface. AND is also the only reading under which the header's "12 of 138" means anything.
**Trade-off accepted:** **A `failed` row carrying tags from an earlier attempt is hidden by a filter that does not match them, and its retry goes with it.** 089 is why such a row exists: a retag leaves the previous attempt's values in place. Clearing the filter is the way back, and it is the only way back. The alternative was a status-aware exception, and it was refused for putting a special case inside a rule that currently fits in one sentence, to reach a row the user can already reach. One test asserts the hiding, so the cost is defended rather than merely accepted.

## 110 — The filters are the store's, the URL is the page's, and it is read once

**Decision:** `WardrobeStore` owns `filters` and `setFilters`, and injects nothing from `@angular/router`. `wardrobe.page.ts` injects `Router` and `ActivatedRoute`, reads `route.snapshot.queryParamMap` **once** in its constructor, and writes the query string from the same method that sets the state, with `replaceUrl: true`. The query keys are `04-API-SPEC.md`'s own parameter names, including `color_primary`.
**Alternative:** Put the router in the store; drive the URL from an `effect` on `filters()`; use `withComponentInputBinding()`; write the URL with `history.replaceState`.
**Reasoning:** The store is `providedIn: 'root'` and outlives every component, so a store that wrote the address bar would write it from behind whatever screen came next — which is **107's failure, one collection over**, and 107 is already on disk as the argument. An `effect` needs a first-run guard so that reading the URL does not immediately write it back, plus an equality compare to avoid a redundant navigation; one method that sets the state and then writes the URL needs neither, and gives 103's shape: one place changes it. The URL is built from the **normalised** filters rather than from the emitted object, so the address bar carries what the grid is actually filtered by. `withComponentInputBinding()` was measured working and refused anyway: it is bootstrap configuration changed for one screen. `history.replaceState` was refused on measurement — it moves `window.location.search` and leaves `router.url` stale, so the two sources disagree and the gate can only see one of them. The parameter names mirror `04` on 059's precedent: ugly in an address bar is a smaller cost than two names for one thing.
**The consequence of the naming, which is the same fact as its payoff.** Because the filter object and the query string share one vocabulary, the query string is built by looping over the object's own keys — there is nothing to keep in step, because there is one name. The other side of that is that **renaming a field renames a public URL parameter**, silently and with no mapping table to fail: a bookmark or a shared link holding the old name stops filtering and says nothing, which is `04` line 119's unknown-parameter silence arriving in our own client. Not an argument against the naming — a mapping table would make the rename loud and would be the second vocabulary this entry refuses — but it is what makes these six field names part of the application's public surface rather than an internal detail.

**Trade-off accepted:** A URL is user input, and an invalid value is dropped rather than corrected — so `?category=banana` shows an **unfiltered wardrobe under a URL that claims a filter**, until the first interaction rewrites it. Normalising on arrival was the alternative and it writes the address bar on load, which is exactly what reading once exists to prevent. Reachable by anyone editing a link by hand, and defended by a test that names it.

## 111 — The header is the only count, and the third empty state is not the first one

**Decision:** Under an active filter the header reads "12 of 138 items" — the matched rows against the wardrobe total, in the one place a count already lives. Nothing else on the screen carries a number. A wardrobe with items in it and nothing visible gets its **own** state: its own string, a **Clear filters** control, and no upload call to action.
**Alternative:** Leave `total` in the header and put the match count in the filter bar; render `visible().length` alone; reuse the empty state for an empty result.
**Reasoning:** A second count location renders the wardrobe size twice on one screen. Keeping `total` as the second half of the sentence is what lets **094 and 100 both survive unchanged**: it is still the server's number, still the only one that knows about rows past the page of 200, and still the one the client moves on upload. Reusing the empty state would tell a user with 138 garments that her wardrobe is empty and offer her the camera, when the way out is the filter.
**Trade-off accepted:** Two controls now say **Clear filters** when the no-match state is on screen — the bar's and the state's. The duplicate label is the cost of the state carrying its own way out; the bar's copy is where the controls are and the state's is where the user is looking. The empty branch's `&& store.pending().length === 0` clause survives untouched under all of this, and has its own test and its own mutation, because it is the line most likely to be lost in a rewrite of this file and its failure is silent: the screen simply tells a user mid-upload that her wardrobe is empty.

## 112 — No chip counts, and `byCategory` is corrected out of `05` rather than built

**Decision:** The category chips carry no counts. `05-FRONTEND-SPEC.md` line 68's assignment of `byCategory` to this task is corrected rather than honoured. `GET /items/stats` stays unowned, with no frontend caller.
**Alternative:** Count each chip from the loaded collection, or from `stats`' `by_category`.
**Reasoning:** The header carries the count (111), so a chip count is the same fact derived twice, plus a `.one`/`.other` pair per chip under 095's no-plural-rule. Sourcing them from `stats` is worse than duplicative: it is a second number with a different denominator — `by_category` counts every non-archived row while the grid counts the loaded page — so above 200 items the two disagree, and `stats` is first on `STAGE-1`'s cut list. `byCategory` groups `visible()`, and 1.8 renders a flat grid: building it would be a computed with no consumer, which is the thing that same sentence in `05` refuses on 1.5's behalf. **Corrected the way 090 was corrected**, in this task's commit, rather than left for a reader to find assigned and absent.
**Trade-off accepted:** `05`'s state sketch now differs from the shipped store in two places rather than one (`byCategory`, and 103's run object), and both are annotated in place rather than rewritten. `GET /items/stats` remains implemented, tested and unconsumed — recorded in `AUDITS.md` beside the seven query parameters in the same condition.

## 113 — The filter bar is an inline disclosure, and O-15 is answered rather than acted on

**Decision:** `filter-bar.ts` is a chip row plus a `Filters` disclosure button that opens an inline panel in flow. It is not a sheet, and no `shared/ui/` component is extracted.
**Alternative:** A modal filter sheet, as `05`'s §Filter sheet and its mockup's `[Filters ▾]` both describe; or extracting `shared/ui/sheet` at its second caller, which is O-15's own recommendation.
**Reasoning:** A modal over the grid hides the thing being filtered, which is **098's own argument** for why the gallery path closes the upload sheet: after choosing, the user's next move is watching the result, and a sheet covering it hides precisely that. A filter is worse than an upload here, because the result changes *while the control is open*. On the extraction: `upload-sheet.ts` owns hand-rolled focus and dismissal behaviour that a shared sheet would have to carry, and refitting a shipped, tested component is a refactor inside a feature task. O-15 asked for the second caller to decide, and the second caller decided it does not want a sheet — which is an answer, not a deferral.
**Trade-off accepted:** `05`'s §Filter sheet and its mockup legend are corrected in this commit, and `shared/ui/` is still empty with two tasks having now declined to fill it. If Stage 1 ends that way, O-15's other recommendation stands: delete the line from `05`'s file tree rather than leave a map of a structure that does not exist. The chip row scrolls horizontally as the mockup draws it and **nothing scrolls it for the user** — `scrollIntoView` is `undefined` in jsdom and calling it throws, so a selected chip past the right edge stays there.

## 114 — Twenty-four label keys, and the colour a swatch is painted is not one of them

**Decision:** Seven category labels and seventeen colour labels are written into `en.json` by hand. `enum-label.pipe.ts` is not built. The swatches carry their label as `aria-label` and no visible text; the hex values they are painted live in `filter-bar.ts` as a `satisfies Record<Color, string>` map.
**Alternative:** Build the pipe now, taking O-10's recommendation early; or render the raw vocabulary values.
**Reasoning:** ~~O-10 assigns~~ — **corrected at task 1.9: O-10 covers `cloudinary-url.pipe.ts` only and says nothing about `enum-label.pipe.ts`, which appears in `05`'s file tree and in no audit item.** Citing it here attached one component's argument to another's, which is the same defect 117 found in `CONVENTIONS.md` citing 017 for a rule it does not contain. The argument itself survives the correction and is the reason the pipe is still not built: a label pipe with a hand-written map inside it is the same second copy in a second language, merely centralised. Twenty-four keys in the file that already holds every other string is the smaller thing. Raw values were refused because the vocabulary is written for a database — `light_blue` on a chip is not a label. **The `satisfies` clause is doing real work and is the reason the swatch map is not a fourth hand-mirror:** a colour added to `enums.ts` fails this file at compile time, which is more than `CONVENTIONS.md`'s other mirrored constants get.
**Superseded in part at task 1.9:** the twenty-four keys move from `wardrobe.filter.*` to `vocabulary.*`, because a screen's namespace holding vocabulary words becomes a lie the moment a second screen reads them, and eighty-two more join them there. The count of hand-written keys was never the objection; where they lived was not considered here. `DECISIONS.md` 118.
**Trade-off accepted:** The i18n keys themselves are unwatched in the usual way — a colour added to `enums.ts` compiles the swatch map and renders a chip whose label is the key string, because `I18nService.t` falls back to the key. That failure is visible on the screen rather than silent, which is the direction to be wrong in, and it is the same exposure every other string in the file has.

## 115 — The ranges are bound and coerced, because the gate's range input is not a browser's

**Decision:** Every `<input type="range">` carries an explicit `[value]`. The filter bar reports what the control said and rounds nothing; `WardrobeStore.setFilters` rounds to a whole number, clamps to 1–5 and orders the two handles. `SCALE_MIN` and `SCALE_MAX` are hand-transcribed literals.
**Alternative:** Leave the handles unbound and let the control default; round in the component; trust `step="1"`.
**Reasoning:** Measured against jsdom 28.1.0 at this task: a range with `min=1 max=5` and no `value` reads **50**, where a browser reads 3; and setting `2.7` on a `step="1"` control leaves `2.7`, because jsdom does not snap. So a test asserting an initial handle position would have been asserting a jsdom artefact, and any code trusting the browser to have snapped would be defended by nothing. Binding the value removes the first; coercing at the store's setter removes the second, at the one door into the filter state. Ordering the handles rather than refusing `min > max` follows from what the pair means: the interval between them, whichever was dragged.
**Trade-off accepted:** Two range inputs per dimension rather than a two-handle control, which no platform element provides; the pair overlaps visually and is distinguishable only by its labels. The first mutation run found the cost of writing `setFormality` and `setWarmth` as two near-identical methods: rounding inside one of them survived the whole suite, because only a warmth handle had ever been dragged with a fraction. Both are now tested. `06-TESTING-STRATEGY.md` carries the run.

## 116 — A completed edit clears a failed status, and the required set is named once

**Decision:** `PATCH /items/{id}` writes one status, in one direction: a `failed` row whose merged result carries every field in `REQUIRED_TAG_FIELDS` becomes `ready`, and `error_message` is cleared with it and only with it. `processing` is never written. `ready` is never demoted. The required set moves out of `vision.py` into `app/enums.py` as a public tuple of **ten** row fields, and `vision.py` derives its own eleven as `(*REQUIRED_TAG_FIELDS, "confidence")`.
**Alternative:** Record the gap rather than fix it, as task 1.9's orientation first recommended; let the frontend say the row is recovered; clear the status from `POST /retag` instead; or share `vision.py`'s `_missing_fields()` predicate rather than the tuple.
**Reasoning:** `03-AI-CONTRACTS.md` has promised an **Add manually** link since it was written, and `AUDITS.md` **O-3** assigned it to 1.9 to *"recover the one class of item the product cannot otherwise recover"* — a photograph the model cannot read at all. The wire had no mechanism: `update_item` wrote the fourteen tag fields and `user_edited` and touched `status` nowhere, so an item hand-tagged from the failed tile answered `200` and kept its `failed` status and its `error_message` for good. `item-card.ts` branches on status alone (089), so the tile went on saying "Tagging failed" above a full set of tags the user had just typed. That is not a screen problem and it could not be solved on the screen: a client that showed such a row as recovered would be asserting a status no server issued, which is 097 one collection over.

**The one-direction narrowness is three separate arguments, not one.** `processing` is untouched because a background task holds the row and writes every tag column when it lands (089), so a status written here would be silently overwritten by a task that never knew about it — the same race 1.9's brief already forbids opening the editor into. `ready` is untouched because an edit that clears a field is a user *answering*, not failing, and 109 already depends on a `ready` row carrying a null beside four real tags; demoting it would make the filter rule and the status rule disagree about the same null. And no other status transition belongs to this endpoint at all — the tagging path owns the rest.

**Where the required set lives, and the field that could not come with it.** `vision.py:381` already named it: `_REQUIRED_FIELDS`, eleven fields, private, with a docstring explaining that the *check* belongs there rather than in `enums.py` because `validate_tag_dict` treats absent and null alike, which is correct for a partial `PATCH` body and wrong as input to a typed object. That reasoning survives untouched — what moves is the **list**, not the check. Ten of the eleven are row fields; the eleventh is `confidence`, which is the report's name for the column `ai_confidence` (the rename is `tagging.py`'s, 028). **Requiring it here would have made this rule unfireable on exactly the row it exists to rescue:** `ai_confidence` is nullable with no default, `_store` never writes it on the failure path, and `ItemUpdate` has no such field — so a row that never tagged could never satisfy a set containing it, no matter what a user typed. Not a matter of taste: including it would have shipped a branch that could not be reached. `confidence` is a fact about an *answer*, not about a garment, and a row a person completed by hand legitimately has none.
**Sharing the tuple rather than the predicate**, because the two callers ask different questions of the same set — did the model answer everything, against is this garment described — differing by that field and by `_missing_fields`' blank-`display_name` branch, which is unreachable from `PATCH` since `ItemUpdate` strips and requires `min_length=1`. A shared predicate would be one function with two behaviours and a parameter to pick between them. `CATEGORY_DEPENDENT_FIELDS` is the precedent and this sits beside it: a tuple of field names exported from `enums.py` so a route reads the list rather than restating it.

**Measured, and it falsified the prediction made before the run.** Removing `water_resistant` from `REQUIRED_TAG_FIELDS` was declared in advance as an expected survivor: the column is `NOT NULL` with a server default, so it can never be the field that blocks a `PATCH`, and no test on this endpoint can distinguish its presence from its absence. **It was caught, by three tests in `test_vision.py`** — `test_the_required_fields_are_the_eleven_with_no_null_in_the_schema` and a parametrised row for `water_resistant` — none of which is a `PATCH` test. The reasoning about this endpoint was right and the prediction about the blast radius was wrong: **deriving one tuple from the other also shares its defence**, so the tagging path's existing tests now guard the route's list for free. That property was not an argument for the shape when it was chosen, and it is the strongest one it has.
**Trade-off accepted:** a hand-completed row is `ready` with `ai_confidence` still `NULL` and with the failed attempt's record still under `attributes["tagging"]`. Neither is cleaned up, and both are correct: no model produced that answer, and 1.11 mines that column. It also means **`ready` with a null `ai_confidence` becomes the marker for a row a person completed**, which is a fact 1.11 gets for nothing and should read deliberately rather than treat as missing data.

## 117 — Print-and-paste is retired; delivery is a written file and a printed diff

**Decision:** Every file the agent produces is written to disk, and the delivery is a printed `git --no-pager diff` over what it touched. The developer reviews what landed, then stages and commits by hand. `CLAUDE.md`'s "Delivering a file" section and `CONVENTIONS.md`'s delivery section both now say this and nothing else.
**Alternative:** Keep the paste for `backend/app/` and extend the width rule; keep it only for files above some size; keep it and drop the frontend exception instead.
**Reasoning:** The rule existed because the developer reads each file on the way past, and typing a module out is one way to guarantee that. It is not the only way, and it had a measured cost that the alternative does not: a line over the display width could not be transferred by paste with confidence, and **comments were where that failed silently** — code fails loudly, a truncated comment leaves a working file with a hole in its reasoning and nothing anywhere reports it. Task 1.2a lost six comment lines and one whole three-line comment out of a file whose every statement was byte-correct; task 1.4 lost five more from a partial block. **The replacement is not "nothing", which is the only way this would have been a loss.** It is the standard already applied to the tree before every commit — verify the tree, not the intent — and a diff is *immune* to the failure the paste introduced, because a diff is not retyped.

**The argument was already on disk and was scoped too narrowly.** Task 1.5 moved `frontend/` to write-to-disk on exactly this sentence: a `git diff` review answers what the paste was for and cannot suffer what the width rule was for. That was written as a justification for one directory; it was always an argument about the medium rather than about the directory. What it gives up is the enforced slowness of typing a module out, which is a real loss on a file whose every line is a judgement call — and the new failure mode is skimming a diff rather than reading it, which is what `CONVENTIONS.md`'s delivery section now guards against.

**017 is not superseded by this, and the citation that said otherwise was wrong.** `CONVENTIONS.md` attributed the paste rule to `DECISIONS.md` 017 in two places, and **017 is about commits**: the agent runs no git command that writes, the developer stages and commits by hand, and manual staging forces a review pass over every diff. Every word of that stands and this entry depends on it — the diff review *is* 017's review pass, now carrying the whole weight rather than half of it. Checked while writing this entry: **the print-and-paste rule never had a `DECISIONS.md` entry at all.** It lived in `CLAUDE.md` and in `CONVENTIONS.md` and borrowed 017's number, which is how a rule ends up with reasoning nobody can locate. This entry is the one it should always have had, written at its retirement.

**What is kept and what goes moot.** The 1.2a truncation, the 1.4 partial-block finding and the 1.2b correction stay in `CONVENTIONS.md` under a heading that marks them as history — they happened, and a reader meeting a deliberately narrow comment in `backend/app/` should be able to find out why it is narrow. Two rules they justified are named as moot rather than deleted: the **66-column width rule**, because nothing is retyped now, and **"printed code is read back out of the verified mirror, never retyped"**, because files are copied from the mirror rather than transcribed. The mirror itself is untouched — code is still linted, type-checked and run there before it is written into the tree. **And 1.2b's "verified is not a licence" survives intact**, pointed at the diff instead of the paste: a module that is green has satisfied nothing that a review pass was asking for.
**Trade-off accepted:** For the span of exactly one commit — `fix(api): a completed edit clears a failed status` — `CLAUDE.md` said write-and-diff while `CONVENTIONS.md` still said print-and-paste, because the rule changed mid-task and only the first document was in scope. That is the disagreement this project's own instructions say to report rather than resolve silently, it was reported, and this commit closes it. Also accepted: **whether `ruff format --check` joins the quality gate is left open.** It has been failing on the committed tree since task 1.7 on one blank line, `ruff check` has always passed, and no CI exists to run either — the same shape as **O-13**, where nothing ran the frontend suite for four commits. The one line is fixed here so the tree is clean; making the formatter a gate is a decision about the definition of done and does not belong in the middle of task 1.9.

## 118 — `cloudinary-url.pipe.ts` is built, `enum-label.pipe.ts` is not, and the vocabulary leaves the filter's namespace

**Decision:** O-10's pipe ships at its first real caller. `enum-label.pipe.ts` is still not built; instead 1.8's twenty-four label keys move from `wardrobe.filter.*` to `vocabulary.*` and eighty-two more join them there.
**Alternative:** Render the server's thumbnail scaled up and build no pipe; build a label pipe carrying its own map, as 114 considered; leave the keys where they are and add the rest beside them.
**Reasoning:** `ItemResponse.image_url` is a **thumbnail** — `w_300,h_300,c_pad,b_white` — and `04-API-SPEC.md` line 119 says so, adding that `image_public_id` "is what `cloudinary-url.pipe.ts` builds every other transform from". Item detail is the first screen that needs a different size, which is exactly the caller O-10 said to wait for. The groundwork was already on disk: `environment.cloudinaryCloudName` has been declared in all three environment files since task 0.8 and **read by nothing for nine tasks**. On the labels, 114's argument is unchanged and only its scope moved: `wardrobe.filter.color.black` was fine while one screen read it and becomes a lie the moment a second one does. A screen's namespace holding vocabulary words is the kind of thing that is free to fix now and permanent later.
**Measured, and it corrects a price quoted before the work:** the rename was estimated at "thirteen specs today". It cost **four lines in `filter-bar.ts` and zero spec lines** — `filter-bar.spec.ts` loads the real `en.json` and asserts *rendered labels* (`buttonWith('Tops')`), not key strings, so moving the values with the keys left all thirteen green untouched. The decision was taken at a price that was wrong in its own favour, which is worth recording next to the decision rather than in place of it.
**Trade-off accepted:** The four transform strings are now a **third** hand-written copy — `services/storage.py`, `07-DEPLOYMENT.md`'s table, and this pipe — with nothing comparing them, which is `CONVENTIONS.md`'s hand-mirrored-constant problem without even 114's `satisfies` clause to watch it. The mitigation is the one 101 bought: the pipe's spec transcribes all four **from the document by hand** and never reads `TRANSFORMS`, so mutating any string fails a named test. `thumbnail` is included with no caller, deliberately: the server sends that URL and a client rebuilding it would be deriving what it was handed.

## 119 — Every save sends all fourteen fields, and the cascade is the client's

**Decision:** `TagEditor` submits every field of `ItemUpdate` on every save, as the form holds them. Changing the category empties `subcategory`, `rise`, `fit`, `length` and `layer` on screen, and those five leave as explicit nulls.
**Alternative:** Send only what changed, and let the server's clearing branch do the work it already does.
**Reasoning:** `STAGE-1` 1.9 requires that the user **see five empty fields before saving**, which a request-diff cannot deliver — the clearing would happen server-side, after the decision. Sending everything makes the two agree by construction: the route's clearing branch is guarded by `field not in changes`, so with all fourteen supplied **it never fires**, and the five nulls on the wire are the five blanks on screen. It also makes unreachable the `422` that `STAGE-1` 1.4 documents as a trap — a category-dependent field with no category anywhere — because `category` is always sent.
**Trade-off accepted:** A save writes every column whether or not the user touched it, so two people editing one row cannot merge; last write wins on all fourteen fields rather than on the ones each of them changed. This is a single-user product at Stage 1 and the row has no version to compare, so the alternative is not available rather than rejected. The five-field list is also the **one place the client repeats a backend list** — `CATEGORY_DEPENDENT_FIELDS` — and it is repeated because it drives what the user sees, not because it duplicates a server behaviour that would otherwise happen: with all fourteen sent, this list is the only thing that clears them.

## 120 — The edit waits, because 097 says what an optimistic row would be

**Decision:** `WardrobeStore.edit` sends the `PATCH` and writes nothing until the response lands, then replaces the whole row.
**Alternative:** Write the edit optimistically and roll back on failure.
**Reasoning:** An optimistic row is an `Item` no server issued, in the collection whose entire contract is that everything in it came off the wire — and it is **worse than the synthetic row 097 refused**, because a preview is visibly not a row where a guessed edit is indistinguishable from a real one. The server also decides two things this client cannot: whether the five dependent fields were cleared, and whether the row has just stopped being `failed` (116). Guessing either produces a screen that is briefly wrong in a way nothing corrects until the next load.
**Trade-off accepted:** A save on a slow connection shows no change until it lands; the button says "Saving…" and that is the whole of the feedback.

## 121 — A delete removes the row here and moves the count only if it was counted

**Decision:** `archive(id)` removes the row from `items()` and drops both id-keyed collections. `forget(id)` decrements `total` **only** when the row was in `items()` to begin with.
**Alternative:** Always decrement; or leave `total` alone and let the next `load()` fix it.
**Reasoning:** The `200` on `DELETE` carries no `total`, exactly as the `202` on upload carries none — so the client moves its own count, which is **100 inverted**. The provenance test is what a deep link makes necessary: a row fetched by id never entered `items()` and was never in `total`, so decrementing for it would understate the wardrobe by one until the next load. This is also the first operation in the project that removes a row from `items()` without a `load()`, which makes it the first real test of the leak **093 accepted in writing** — so both id-keyed entries are dropped here rather than left to outlive their row.
**Trade-off accepted:** **None of this is observable on screen today.** `WardrobePage`'s constructor calls `store.load()` on every arrival and renders a loading line while it runs, so a delete that mutates the store correctly and one that does not look identical after the navigation. It is built anyway because the store is a model rather than a cache, and it stops being unobservable the moment anything keeps the grid alive under the detail screen. The tests are therefore store-level, and that was declared before the mutation run rather than discovered by it.

## 122 — One retag control, ungated, and the `409` is produced rather than avoided

**Decision:** Item detail has one **Tag this again** button that always sends the unforced request. A `409 item_edited` opens a second step naming what will be discarded, and only that step sends `?force=true`.
**Alternative:** Show the control only when `user_edited` is true and force immediately; or two controls, one forced.
**Reasoning:** Acceptance criterion 6 is *"`retag` on an edited item returns `409` without `force`"*, and a client that forces whenever `user_edited` is set **never produces that `409` from the UI** — the criterion would stay a route test forever. Asking first is also the honest order: the conflict is the server's answer, not a state the client should predict. `items.api.ts` defaults `force` to `false` and the grid tile cannot pass it at all, so the only path to a forced retag runs through a sentence that says what it costs.
**Trade-off accepted:** Two round trips on every retag of an edited item, and **the second step recurs forever** — `04-API-SPEC.md` is explicit that `user_edited` is never cleared, not by a forced retag and not by the tagging that follows one. So the badge is a fact about the past and the button is a choice about the future, and the label must not imply the button changes the badge. That is C4 from this task's orientation, accepted rather than solved.

## 123 — The category select can say "not chosen yet" and cannot say "clear this"

**Decision:** The category select renders an empty placeholder option **only while the stored value is null**. It disappears after the first save, and the save refuses while it is selected. The other nine vocabulary selects carry a real "—" that clears.
**Alternative:** No empty option ever, and let the select default to its first entry; an always-available "—" on category too; block the whole editor until a category is chosen.
**Reasoning:** These two requirements looked like one and are not. **O-3 opens this editor from a failed tile where every tag is null**, so the control must be able to represent "not chosen yet" — and a select with no empty option renders `Tops`, so a save would write `category: "top"` **without the user choosing it**. That is silent data invention on the one screen built to stop it. But offering that same option on an item that has a category is offering to clear it, and clearing a category is a `200` that also nulls all five dependent fields, leaving a `ready` row with no category that matches every chip in 1.8's filter bar. Conditioning the option on the stored value separates the two exactly.
**Trade-off accepted:** The wire still permits `PATCH {"category": null}` and this client simply declines to offer it — a control not offered, not an exception to a rule. **109 is unchanged**: the per-field null rule is about what a filter does with a null, and nothing here changes that.

## 124 — The subcategory list narrows by category; nothing else does

**Decision:** The subcategory select offers only the chosen category's values, read from `SUBCATEGORIES` in `enums.ts`. Every other select offers its whole vocabulary regardless of category.
**Alternative:** Mirror `FIELD_APPLIES_TO` and `VALUE_APPLIES_TO` and narrow `fit`, `length` and `rise` too.
**Reasoning:** `SUBCATEGORIES` is a **value** mirror that has existed in `enums.ts` since task 0.8, and `enums.ts`'s own header says it mirrors values and not rules. The category-dependent rules are a different thing and 085 decided they stay server-side; copying them here would be a second copy in a second language with nothing comparing them — the failure `CONVENTIONS.md` records three times over, and the alternative the stage brief explicitly offers and this task explicitly declines.
**Trade-off accepted:** The `fit` select offers `skinny` on a tank top and the `rise` select is offered on a pair of shoes. Choosing one is a `422`, which renders as 128's single message. The screen therefore lets a user make a mistake it could have prevented, in exchange for not holding a copy of the rules that would be wrong the first time the vocabulary moves.

## 125 — `display_name` is a text input, and the stage sentence is corrected rather than obeyed

**Decision:** The ten vocabulary fields are selects, `formality` and `warmth` are 1.8's range controls, `water_resistant` is a checkbox, and `display_name` is a free-text input. `STAGE-1` 1.9's *"never a free-text input"* is amended to say what it means.
**Alternative:** Obey it literally and ship no name field anywhere in the product.
**Reasoning:** The sentence is about **tags** — the closed vocabulary, where free text would let a user write a value no filter, stylist or query could ever match. `display_name` is not a tag: it is the alt text, the grid's accessible name, and the only human-readable thing on a row with no tags — which is precisely the row this screen exists to rescue. It is also the field 1.11 plans to mine for vocabulary words the schema discarded, and registration is otherwise the only screen in the project that writes free text at all. A select cannot produce it.
**Trade-off accepted:** One field on this screen accepts anything, trimmed, with a blank sent as `null`. The server's own `StringConstraints(strip_whitespace=True, min_length=1)` is the backstop, and the trimming here means a user typing spaces clears the name rather than being refused.

## 126 — The delete confirms in two presses, not in a dialog

**Decision:** The delete button arms on the first press and deletes on the second, disarming on blur and on any other interaction. No `window.confirm`, no modal.
**Alternative:** `window.confirm`; a modal confirmation; no confirmation with an undo.
**Reasoning:** **Measured at this task: `window.confirm` in jsdom 28.1.0 is a function that returns `undefined`.** A delete written as `if (confirm(...))` therefore never proceeds under any test in this project — the destructive branch would read as tested and never run. That is **098 with a sharper edge**: the same class of finding that made the upload sheet a plain element, now deciding a guard rather than an element. The modal was declined **on cost rather than on principle** — `inert` is unsupported in the gate, so it would be a fourth hand-rolled focus trap, built to guard a misclick that two deliberate presses already guard. Undo is not available: the delete is a soft archive, but `04-API-SPEC.md` lists no endpoint that un-archives and forbids inventing one.
**Trade-off accepted:** A two-press control is weaker than a modal against a determined misclick, and its armed state is a label change rather than a barrier. The disarm-on-anything rule is what keeps an armed button from surviving into the next thing the user does.

## 127 — `/wardrobe/:id`, read once from the snapshot, with a fetch that never joins the collection

**Decision:** A top-level route declared **before** the wildcard, guarded by `authGuard`. The id is read once from `route.snapshot.paramMap`. The page resolves its row from `store.items()` and falls back to `GET /items/{id}`, writing that row to a local signal and never into `items()`.
**Alternative:** Subscribe to `paramMap`; nest the route under `/wardrobe`; push the fetched row into `items()` so the whole app sees it.
**Reasoning:** Nothing changes `:id` under this component — every route in is a fresh navigation from the grid — so a subscription would be machinery for a case that does not exist. **The case that would create it is named rather than left to be discovered: 1.11 adding next/prev on this screen is what breaks the snapshot read.** The route is declared above `{ path: '**' }` because below it every item link would redirect to the grid, which looks like a working app and silently loses the screen. The fetched row stays out of `items()` because that collection is the wardrobe, and a row reached by URL is not evidence about what the wardrobe contains — `total` would then disagree with it, which is 121's provenance problem written into the model instead of solved.
**Trade-off accepted:** **A deep-linked `processing` row does not live-update**, because the poll derives from `items()`. It costs nothing today: `WardrobePage`'s `DestroyRef` stops the loop on navigation away, so nothing polls on this route at all — and that same fact is what makes 121's provenance check stable, since `items()` cannot move underneath the page.

## 128 — One message for every rejected save, and the field is not named

**Decision:** A `422` on save renders a single string — *"We couldn't save those tags. Check the fields and try again."* The form keeps every value the user entered.
**Alternative:** Render the server's `detail`, which names the offending field; or mirror the category rules client-side and pre-empt the error.
**Reasoning:** `04-API-SPEC.md` puts the field name inside `detail` and `CONVENTIONS.md` forbids rendering a raw error, which is the same collision **099** met on upload and resolved the same way — our own string, keyed on the response `code`, with the untranslatable English dropped. The second alternative is 124's copy of the rules and is refused there. The common `422` is unreachable anyway: the client's own cascade clears the five dependent fields on a category change, so what remains is a user who changes category and then picks a value that is legal but wrong for it.
**Trade-off accepted:** **A user is told the save failed without being told which field caused it**, on a form with fourteen controls. Recorded as a known limitation rather than solved, exactly as 099 recorded the filename it cannot show. The mitigation is the condition attached to it: the form is seeded once and never re-seeded, so a rejection leaves every entered value in place — a rejection that also emptied ten fields would be a screen nobody tries twice.

## 129 — The tile's image is the link and the retry button is its sibling

**Decision:** `ItemCard` wraps only the `<img>` in a `routerLink` anchor carrying an accessible name. The retry button and the new **Add tags by hand** link sit beside it, never inside it.
**Alternative:** Make the whole tile a link and keep the button inside it; make the tile a button that navigates programmatically.
**Reasoning:** An anchor containing a button is nested interactive content: browsers disagree about which target a click resolves to, and screen readers announce it inconsistently. **How a user reaches item detail was specified in no document** — `05`'s grid legend gives the tile exactly one behaviour, "⚠ = failed, tap to retry", and §4 describes a screen at `/wardrobe/:id` with no route into it — so this is a decision rather than an implementation of one, and `05` gains the line. The anchor carries `aria-label` because its only content is an image: without it a grid of untagged items reads as forty identical links.
**Trade-off accepted:** The tap target for opening an item is the photograph rather than the whole tile, so the strip of the failed tile carrying its buttons is not itself a link. That is the cost of not nesting, and on a `failed` tile the **Add tags by hand** link goes to the same place.

## 130 — The seed writes its tags directly, and validates them on `PATCH`'s policy rather than the vision path's

**Decision:** `scripts/seed_demo.py` carries a committed table of hand-written tags and makes no AI call. Every row goes through `validate_tag_dict` before any row is inserted, and **any error *and any coercion* aborts the whole run**. A `ready` row must additionally carry every field in `REQUIRED_TAG_FIELDS`, a `failed` row must carry an `error_message`, and a `ready` row may not.
**Alternative:** Run the real pipeline once over the forty images and keep whatever came back; or run it with `USE_FAKE_AI=true`; or insert the table unvalidated.
**Reasoning:** `STAGE-1` §1.10 says "no AI calls", and cost cannot be what decided that — vision is about $0.0002 an image, so forty items is roughly one cent, twice that with a retry on every one. What decides it is reproducibility: a wardrobe tagged by a live model is whatever the model said that afternoon, and `PROMPT_VERSION` moves whenever `enums.py` or the prompt file does, so the demo would drift underneath its own commit. `USE_FAKE_AI` is refused by name in **081** — between 1.1 and 5.1 the fake is one white shirt, and a wardrobe of forty identical placeholder shirts is not a wardrobe. What replaces the model is the validator, and the *policy* is `PATCH`'s and not `vision.py`'s: **089** drew that line for the reason that decides it here too — the vision path accepts a coercion because the source is a model and rejecting fifteen fields over one word throws away fourteen good tags, while this table's source is a file and the fix is to correct the word. Measured: `fit: "flared"` (out of vocabulary) and `fit: "skinny"` on a top (1.2a's category rule) are both reported as *coercions*, not errors, so a check written as `report.ok` would have inserted both with `fit` silently null.
**Trade-off accepted:** **The validator proves a row is legal and never that it is true.** A black leather jacket tagged `color_primary: white` passes every check in this file. That is the same gap **081** recorded for the fake — valid is not sensible — and nothing in this project can close it; the only reader is a person looking at the grid. `tests/unit/test_seed_data.py` walks the committed table so an `enums.py` tightening turns the suite red rather than turning up at a defence, and feeds `_problems` rows it must reject so the coercion rule cannot be narrowed away unnoticed.

## 131 — Two modes, and what is committed is the ids rather than the images

**Decision:** The default mode reads `SEED_ITEMS` and writes rows, making no network call but the database one. `--upload DIR` validates every file, uploads them all, and logs a paste-ready table; it writes no rows. Image files are not committed to the repository — only the `public_id`s are.
**Alternative:** Upload on every run; or commit the forty image files (~10 MB) so the wardrobe is reproducible from a clone.
**Reasoning:** Uploading on every run puts forty new orphaned assets in Cloudinary each time and makes seeding depend on Cloudinary being reachable, which is the wrong dependency for the command that has to work on the morning of a defence. It also compounds a problem that already exists and was measured: the account holds **73 resources against 13 item rows**, so roughly sixty assets are already orphaned — the auditability `047` bought with per-user foldering, being spent. `--upload` validates every file before sending any, which is `04-API-SPEC.md`'s rule for the upload route applied to the same failure: validating as it goes leaves the assets of a failed run in Cloudinary with no row to find them by.
**Trade-off accepted:** **A clone of this repository cannot reproduce the demo wardrobe**, because the images are not in it and the `public_id`s point at one Cloudinary account. Recorded rather than solved: the images are the developer's own photographs of her own clothes, and committing ten megabytes of them to make a capstone repository self-contained is the worse trade. If the account is ever lost, the table is re-uploadable from the local folder and nothing else is needed.

## 132 — The demo user's id is pinned rather than generated

**Decision:** `DEMO_USER_ID` is the literal `01821186-a63e-4b4b-8fef-7a83d6377056`, passed explicitly on insert. `User.id`'s `server_default` only fires when no value is supplied, so this is legal and changes nothing else about the row.
**Alternative:** Let the database generate it, and give `--upload` a throwaway id for the Cloudinary folder.
**Reasoning:** `upload_image` builds the folder `bijoux/{user_id}/` and runs *before* any row exists, so an unpinned id means uploading under a user that does not exist — which `STAGE-0` §0.6 already named as "the first instance of the orphaned-asset problem that per-user foldering exists to make auditable", there as a one-off to be cleaned up afterwards and here as forty assets, permanently. `--reset` makes it worse rather than better: each reset would mint a new id, so the committed `public_id`s would name the folder of a user that no longer exists, again, every run. Pinned, the asset folder and the row's owner agree in every run forever, and a reset re-inserts against the same assets and orphans nothing.
**Trade-off accepted:** One literal UUID in a source file that has to match what is in Cloudinary. If it is ever edited, the existing assets keep the old folder name and nothing breaks — `image_public_id` is stored per row and read verbatim — but the foldering stops being the audit trail it exists to be. The constant carries the reason so an editor meets it.

## 133 — Refuse rather than upsert, and the insert order is stated rather than inferred

**Decision:** The default mode refuses with a non-zero exit if `demo@bijoux.app` exists, logging its id and item count. `--reset` deletes that one user by email and lets `fk_items_user_id_users`'s `ON DELETE CASCADE` take the items. **This script contains no unscoped DELETE and no TRUNCATE, and every write and delete is scoped to the demo user's id.** The user is flushed before the items are added.
**Alternative:** Upsert on `short_id` or on `public_id`; or delete-and-recreate silently on every run.
**Reasoning:** The database this runs against is the developer's live Neon database — the same `DATABASE_URL` Render will read — and it holds four real users and thirteen real items, three of them task 0.7 leftovers and ten of them real tagged garments. Scoping is therefore the whole safety story, and it is written down here rather than left as a habit because it is the one property whose absence is invisible until it is catastrophic. An upsert was refused because it makes `--reset` invisible: it would silently overwrite a demo item somebody edited during a rehearsal, which is exactly what the pre-defence checklist rehearses. **The flush is a finding, not a tidy.** No `relationship()` is declared between `User` and `Item` — `02-DATA-MODEL.md` never asks for one and nothing else in the project needs one — so SQLAlchemy's unit of work has no dependency edge between the two mappers, and it batched the items first: the first real run died on `fk_items_user_id_users`. It is one transaction still, so a bad row rolls the user back with it.
**And the guard was correct and silent, which is the failure a guard can least afford.** `_describe` passed the host, the database name and the row counts as `extra={...}`, and `configure_logging` installs a `%(message)s` formatter outside production — so the one line whose entire purpose is to tell an operator which database is about to be written to printed the words "Target database" and nothing else. It performed the check and never said what it found, which is worse than not checking, because the operator reads a line that looks like confirmation. Found by running the no-`--yes` form rather than by reading it; six call sites in the file had the same shape, and all six now carry their values in the message and keep them in `extra` for the JSON formatter that does read it.

**Trade-off accepted:** **Nothing tests any of this.** Measured by mutation: deleting the flush, and making the `TEST_DATABASE_URL` guard never fire, both leave the suite fully green. No fixture can point a command line at a database and assert what happened, and a test that tried would have to write a demo user into `TEST_DATABASE_URL` — which is the one thing the guard exists to refuse. The guard and its own test are mutually exclusive by construction, and that is the price of having the guard.

## 134 — Seeded rows are told apart by a second guest key in `attributes`

**Decision:** Every seeded row carries `attributes["seed"] = {"script", "version", "seeded_at", "source"}`, where `source` is the original filename. 1.11 excludes them with `WHERE NOT (attributes ? 'seed')`.
**Alternative:** Key off `user_id =` the demo user; prefix `display_name`; add a column.
**Reasoning:** `02-DATA-MODEL.md` already establishes `attributes` as the extension point and `tagging` as "a guest in one key of it", so a second guest is the shape that document describes rather than a new idea. It matters because **1.11 mines rows already written** — `display_name` for vocabulary words the schema discarded, `fit` nulls, and whether a null `color_secondary` has ever been emitted — and forty hand-written `display_name`s inside that sample are forty words that came from a person, in a measurement whose entire subject is what came from the model. Keying off the demo user was refused for a concrete reason: it stops being true the moment somebody uploads a real photograph while signed in as the demo account, which is what the pre-defence rehearsal does. A `display_name` prefix would corrupt the field the marker exists to protect. A column needs a migration and `02` forbids adding fields without asking.
**Trade-off accepted:** **A retagged seed row carries both keys**, because a retag merges `attributes` rather than replacing it. That is correct rather than a flaw — `seed` says where the row came from, `tagging` says where its current tags came from — and it is written here because the first reader to meet both will otherwise assume one is a bug. Seeded rows are also distinguishable a second way that is *not* declared and must not be relied on: `ai_confidence` is null and `attributes->'tagging'` is absent, which is true of every seeded row today and is an accident of the current data rather than a contract.

## 135 — Two failed rows behind a flag, and never a `processing` one

**Decision:** `--with-failures`, off by default, appends two rows: one `failed` with every tag null, and one `failed` *carrying* tags with two filterable fields deliberately null. No `processing` row is ever seeded, and `_problems` refuses one.
**Alternative:** Ship all forty `ready` as `STAGE-1` §1.10 specifies and demonstrate failure by retagging a blurry photograph live; or make the two failures part of the default forty.
**Reasoning:** Three shipped behaviours enter only through a `failed` row — 1.5's failed tile, 1.4's retry button, and 1.9's `failed`→`ready` promotion — and the only failed rows in the database belong to a user the demo account cannot see. The second shape earns its place separately: `02-DATA-MODEL.md` warns that a failed row can carry tags and that 1.5 must not assume otherwise, **no row in this database has ever had that shape**, and it is the only row that can demonstrate 1.8's rule that a null is never hidden by that field's filter — because `REQUIRED_TAG_FIELDS` forbids a null in `category`, `color_primary`, `formality` or `warmth` on anything `ready`, so no seeded `ready` row can ever exercise it. A `processing` row is refused because the startup sweep fails any row whose `updated_at` is more than ten minutes old and writes its own `error_message` (**088**), so a seeded one changes status on its own between the seed and the demo.
**Trade-off accepted:** The flag is off by default, so the wardrobe a defence opens is clean and the three states are one flag away rather than on screen. The cost is that the default run leaves those behaviours undemonstrated unless somebody remembers the flag; `07-DEPLOYMENT.md`'s checklist is where that is remembered.

**Amended the same day, on the grid rather than on paper. Reusing two committed `public_id`s for the failure rows was right for Cloudinary and wrong for the demo, and the difference was invisible until somebody looked at the wardrobe.** Reuse was chosen so the two extra rows orphaned no asset — sound on 131's terms, and it stays sound. What it produces on screen is a garment appearing **twice**, once `ready` and once `failed`, with nothing on either tile saying they are the same photograph in two states. To anyone who does not already know the design that is a duplication bug, and a wardrobe that looks like it double-saved two items is a worse thing to present than one that simply does not demonstrate the failed state. **The trade-off that was approved was two extra rows; two duplicated photographs is what was delivered, and that consequence was never put in front of the person approving it.** The fault is in the proposal, not the approval.

**What follows from it.** The default seed is clean and is what a defence opens: 64 rows, all `ready`, no `error_message`, no repeated `public_id` — verified on the live database after a `--reset` re-run, not asserted. `--with-failures` is unusable in its current form and should not be run. The fix is two photographs of garments **not otherwise in the wardrobe**, which also fill two of the eighteen empty subcategories (`hoodie`, `skirt`), and `FAILURE_ITEMS` then points at their own `public_id`s and duplicates nothing.

**And the honest cost of a clean wardrobe, stated so nobody rediscovers it at a defence.** Three shipped behaviours have no entry point in the demo — 1.5's failed tile, 1.4's retry button, and 1.9's `failed`→`ready` promotion with its "Add tags by hand" link — because all three are reached only through a `failed` row. A fourth goes with them and is the one that cost the most argument to get right: **1.8's rule that a null is never hidden by that field's filter is now undemonstrable by construction**, since `REQUIRED_TAG_FIELDS` forbids a null in `category`, `color_primary`, `formality` or `warmth` on any `ready` row, and the clean wardrobe measures zero nulls in all four. The live-retag alternative is refused for the reason `AUDITS.md` **O-17** already gives: `validate_tags` retries, a bad photograph usually still tags, and a demonstration that depends on an API call failing on cue is not a demonstration.

## 136 — The demo wardrobe is a button, not a prefilled form, and O-12 is superseded rather than taken

**Decision:** `/login` carries a **View the demo wardrobe** button that calls `POST /auth/login` with the seeded credentials and lands on `/wardrobe`. It sits outside the `<form>`, shares the page's `submitting()` signal, and renders a failure through the same message the form uses. `AUDITS.md` **O-12** specified prefilled credentials in the `FormGroup`; that mechanism is not what shipped.
**Alternative:** Prefill the form as O-12 specified; put the credentials in a caption and let the visitor type them; cut the affordance and delete the promise from `05`.
**Reasoning:** The button satisfies what O-4 asked for and every constraint O-12 imposed — no new endpoint, no new mechanism, the same `POST /auth/login` and the same JWT — while being honest about what it does, where a link inside your own empty wardrobe implied your wardrobe was about to fill up. The mechanism changed for a measured reason O-12 did not have: the password input carries `autocomplete="current-password"` and the email input `autocomplete="username"`, paired deliberately by **070** so password managers offer save-and-fill, so a prefilled password invites a browser to save the demo account over a visitor's stored login. A button never populates the field. It sits outside the form because a `<button>` inside one defaults to `type=submit`, which would fire an empty sign-in alongside its own. **Every demo visitor is the same user.** There is one demo account, its state is shared and mutable, anything a visitor edits or deletes stays edited or deleted, and `--reset` is the only remedy; the button's hint says so on screen rather than leaving it to be discovered.
**Trade-off accepted:** **`DEMO_EMAIL` and `DEMO_PASSWORD` now exist as constants in two languages — `backend/scripts/seed_demo.py` and `frontend/src/app/features/auth/login.page.ts` — and nothing compares them.** This is the fourth instance of the shape `CONVENTIONS.md` records under *Limits and units*, after the upload limits, the password rules and the mebibyte arithmetic, and it fails the same way: drift produces a demo button that answers `401` against a correctly seeded database. What catches it is **only a manual check** — signing in with the button against a seeded database. `login.page.spec.ts` pins what the frontend sends (a mutation changing the password fails one test) and `test_seed_data.py` pins what the backend accepts, so each side is defended against silent edits *to itself*; neither can see the other, no test can, and none is possible without an endpoint that publishes the credential, which would be worse than the drift. Verifying it belongs on the pre-defence checklist and is there.

## 137 — The demo password is published rather than secret

**Decision:** `bijoux-demo-wardrobe`, chosen fresh for this account and reused from nothing. It is documented in `07-DEPLOYMENT.md` as a published credential, and it appears in committed frontend source because the button that signs a visitor in has to know it.
**Alternative:** Read it from an environment variable at build time; keep it out of the frontend and print it on the login screen for the visitor to type.
**Reasoning:** `CLAUDE.md` forbids writing real credentials to any file, and the resolution is not to break that rule but to notice that this is not one: it is a published login to a throwaway account, in the same class as any public API demo. Documenting it as published is what makes that true rather than asserted — a password found in source with no explanation is read as a leak. A build-time variable would hide it from the repository and not from the shipped bundle, which is security theatre with a deployment step attached. It clears `MIN_PASSWORD_LENGTH` and `MAX_PASSWORD_BYTES`, asserted in `test_seed_data.py`, so the published credential is one the register form would itself have accepted — the script calls `hash_password` directly and never goes through `POST /auth/register`, so nothing else would have checked.
**Trade-off accepted:** **Anyone who can read the deployed site can sign in as the demo account and spend the project's OpenAI credit**, because the button hands them an authenticated session and `POST /items/upload` queues a real tagging call per file. `04-API-SPEC.md` names a `429`/`rate_limited` answer to exactly this question and **`AUDITS.md` O-5 records that the limit is unbuilt**. O-5 is extended rather than a new item, and it now says what this decision makes true: the rate limit is a precondition for deploying, not a Stage 5 nicety.

## 138 — Every seed image is the developer's own photograph, and one file had to be removed to make that true

**Decision:** The seed wardrobe is photographed entirely by the developer — her own garments, on a hanger against a white wall or laid on a couch. `samples/shoes_heels_black.jpg` is excluded from the seed and should be deleted from `samples/`. This supersedes the hybrid answer given at orientation, which was "your own photographs where possible, catalogue shots for the gaps".
**Alternative:** Keep the retailer catalogue shots for the categories the wardrobe is short of; or shoot the fourteen missing subcategories.
**Reasoning:** The pitch of this project is *photograph your own clothes*, and a demo wardrobe padded with retailer product photography demonstrates something the application does not do — flat-lay studio images on seamless grey, of garments nobody owns. It also removes a licensing question from a submitted capstone, which is a better place to be than having an answer ready for it. **The claim was not true when it was made and that is why this entry exists.** `samples/` was re-inventoried file by file at 1.10 and 58 of 59 files are 1536×2048-class phone photographs modified on 24–25 August; exactly one — `shoes_heels_black.jpg`, 1800×2600, unmodified since 16 August — is a survivor of the original catalogue import, and it was opened and confirmed by eye as a studio shot of black satin slingbacks. A sentence saying "everything here is mine" would have been false by one file, and the one file was in the category the count is thinnest in.
**Trade-off accepted:** **Eighteen of the vocabulary's thirty-eight subcategories have no photograph and will not get one**, and one formality band is nearly empty with them. There is no `shirt`, `sweatshirt`, `hoodie`, `skirt`, `leggings`, `cargo`, `jumpsuit`, `cardigan`, `vest`, `puffer`, `flats`, `loafers`, `tote`, `clutch`, `backpack`, `scarf`, `hat` or `sunglasses` in the folder, so **formality 1 holds a single item** — there is almost no loungewear to photograph that has been photographed — and `accessory` has a belt and two necklaces. Counted against `SUBCATEGORIES` in `enums.py` rather than from memory; the three numbers this sentence carried before that count were all wrong, and 141 records why. The demo wardrobe is a real wardrobe with real gaps rather than a designed one, and the gaps are recorded in `STAGE-1` §1.10 so that a filter returning nothing is met as a known property rather than as a bug. The alternative was to mis-tag a garment upward or downward to fill a band, which would put a false row in the one table the whole project is a demonstration of.

## 139 — A real garment whose colour the vocabulary cannot name, found before 1.11 rather than by it

**Decision:** `samples/top_tank.jpeg` is a **turquoise** satin cami. `turquoise` is not one of `ColorPrimary`'s seventeen values, and the nearest legal value is `blue`, which is what the seed row carries. The gap is recorded here as a 1.11 input rather than silently absorbed.
**Alternative:** Add `turquoise` to the vocabulary; or tag it `green`, which is equally near and equally wrong.
**Reasoning:** This is the same shape as `fit: "flared"` at task 1.1 — a real attribute of a real garment that the closed vocabulary has no word for — and it is the first instance found on the **colour** axis, which `02-DATA-MODEL.md` treats as settled. It matters because 1.11's whole subject is what the vocabulary is missing, and until now every candidate came from model output. This one came from a person looking at her own wardrobe, before the golden set exists, which makes it evidence of a different kind: the vocabulary is short a word regardless of what the model does with the image. Adding the value was refused here because `02-DATA-MODEL.md` is authoritative and a vocabulary change lands there first, then in `enums.py`, then in `enums.ts` — three hand-maintained copies with nothing comparing them, which is not a change to make mid-task on one garment.
**Trade-off accepted:** **The demo wardrobe contains one row whose colour is wrong on purpose**, and a visitor filtering on `blue` gets a turquoise top. Two things follow for 1.11. The seed marker (**134**) makes this row excludable, so it does not pollute a measurement of what the *model* cannot name. And the interesting question this raises is one only the golden set can answer: whether the model, shown the same garment, returns `blue`, returns `green`, or returns an out-of-vocabulary word that gets coerced to null — three different failures that would look identical in a column of nulls. Written into `STAGE-1` §1.11 as a named case to include.

## 140 — Formality 1 has exactly one item, measured after a per-item re-audit

**Decision:** The seed wardrobe carries **one** formality-1 item — `bottom_shorts_white.jpeg`, satin elasticated-waist lounge shorts with a broderie-anglaise hem. Everything else in the folder sits at 2 or above. The band is not empty and is not padded.
**Alternative:** Leave the original reading, in which formality 1 was empty and 2 held twenty-five items; or move a ribbed cotton tank or a pair of jeans down to 1 to give the band weight.
**Reasoning:** The first pass anchored the floor too high **and** the ceiling too low, and both were found by opening the photographs rather than by reasoning from filenames. `02-DATA-MODEL.md`'s own examples decide most of it: t-shirts, jeans and sneakers are named at formality 2 verbatim, so those stay. What moved is everything the filename called a "tank": seven of the ten are **satin or velvet camis**, which are going-out tops at 3, not casual basics at 2 — only the two ribbed cotton tanks and one brown ribbed tank are genuinely 2. Three of the four "shorts" are tailored or bouclé and belong at 3. The single genuine loungewear item is the satin lounge shorts, which the filename `bottom_shorts_white` gave no way to distinguish from the tailored `bottom_shorts_white2` sitting beside it. Net effect: formality moved from `0 / 25 / 19 / 10 / 4` to **`1 / 18 / 29 / 12 / 4`**, and the mode moved from 2 to 3. Nothing was retagged to fill a band; every move is a garment that was mis-read.
**Trade-off accepted:** **The formality slider's bottom stop shows a visitor exactly one tile — a pair of white satin shorts.** That is a thin band rather than an empty grid, and it is honest: this is a wardrobe of going-out clothes with almost no loungewear in it, which is what the owner photographed because it is what she owns. Recorded rather than fixed, because the fix would be either a photograph that does not exist or a tag that is false. The related finding is that **`solid` covers 55 of 64 rows** and `stripes`, `checks`, `graphic` and `other` have no item at all — the pattern axis is the least exercised dimension in the seed and no filter reads it today.

## 141 — Filename-derived tags failed on more than half the rows checked, and three vocabulary gaps turned up before 1.11 ran

**Decision:** Every one of the sixty-four seed photographs is tagged from the **image**, not from its filename. The twenty-four rows that had been tagged from filenames alone were re-opened and re-tagged; nothing ships on a filename's word.
**Alternative:** Keep the twenty-four, on the grounds that a filename its owner wrote is good evidence and opening sixty-four photographs is slow.
**Reasoning:** The sample that decided it was the bags: six of six filenames named the wrong subcategory, including two this project had already accepted from an earlier inventory. Re-opening the remaining twenty-four gave the rate — **thirteen of twenty-four rows carried at least one wrong field.** Subcategory 3, colour 2, warmth 3, formality 3, material 2. **The asymmetry is what makes this worth writing down.** A wrong `subcategory` aborts the run before anything inserts, because `validate_tag_dict` refuses a subcategory the category does not admit — so it is loud and free. A wrong `colour`, `warmth`, `material` or `pattern` is a legal value in the right column: it inserts silently, passes every check in `scripts/seed_demo.py` and `tests/unit/test_seed_data.py`, and is discovered when somebody filters at a defence and the grid disagrees with the photograph. Seven of the thirteen bad rows were wrong only in a silent column. **The sharpest single finding: `sweater` in a filename carried no warmth information at all.** Two of the three "sweaters" are summer knits — a cream pointelle and an open waffle mesh — and both had been tagged warmth 4 from the word alone; they are 3 and 2. `warmth` is the field the whole Stage 4 packing feature keys on.
