**Trade-off accepted:** **A user can see six ready items in the grid and be told the wardrobe is too small** — two pyjama sets and four garments is a real case, and the message does not explain the arithmetic. `04-API-SPEC.md` now says "usable" rather than "ready" so the document matches the code, and the honest alternative was a `502` for the same user, later and after paying for it. Returning the `id` also means a client can hold a look row's identity before Stage 3 has any endpoint that accepts it, which is one stage of a field that reads as unused.

## 173 — `home_location_missing`, and the weather sentence belongs to `weather.py`

**Decision:** `400 home_location_missing` when `home_lat`/`home_lon` are `NULL`, added to `CONVENTIONS.md`'s code list and `04-API-SPEC.md`. `summarize_forecast(forecast) -> str` lands in `app/services/weather.py`, built from `temp_max_c` rounded to a whole degree, transcribing `03-AI-CONTRACTS.md`'s two examples — `18°C, no rain.` and `12°C, rain 4mm.` — with no condition word, and using `build_rule`'s own `> 1` mm threshold.
**Alternative:** Reuse `forecast_unavailable` for the missing location. Answer `422`, since a request that cannot be served is arguably invalid. Accept `lat`/`lon` on the request body. Build the summary string in the route, or in `stylist.py` beside the prompt. Include the condition word, or the minimum temperature, or both.
**Reasoning:** **A separate code** for `geocoding_unavailable`'s reason one task earlier: the two conditions say different things and only one of them is fixed by the user, on a screen that exists. `422` was refused because the body is well-formed — the account is what is incomplete — and `04-API-SPEC.md` reserves `422` for the request. **Coordinates stay off the body** because `04` prints the body and there are none in it; 151 made the home location one field, so the route reads three columns or none. **The summary lives in `weather.py`** for `requires_outerwear`'s reason at 2.5: the module that owns the units owns the sentence about them, and a route that decided what a millimetre reads like would be business logic in a handler. **`> 1` rather than `> 0`** is the load-bearing detail — at 0.5 mm the summary must not say "rain" above a rule that says nothing about rain, because the prompt tells the model to obey the rule exactly and a contradiction one line above it is the kind of drift nothing would catch. `temp_max_c` follows 142.
**Trade-off accepted:** **The demo account cannot ask for a look until somebody runs a `PATCH /me`** — O-20 measured that the live row still has no home location, and this decision turns that from a `500` into a `400` with a message, but not into a working demo. **The condition word is dropped**, so the model is never told it is foggy; `build_rule` reads rain and wind and nothing else does, which is 004's design and not a gap this task should widen. And a fifteenth code is a fifteenth branch for the frontend to write at 2.8.

## 174 — The profile screen is scheduled at 2.10a, and the weather strip carries the entry point

**Decision:** `05-FRONTEND-SPEC.md` §8's profile screen becomes task **2.10a**, between the anchor and the swap, scoped to the frontend alone. Task **2.12** is widened: the weather strip is the general, non-anchored way into `/stylist`, and its tap target renders whether or not the account has a forecast to print.
**Alternative:** Leave §8 to Stage 5 as a `KNOWN-ISSUES.md` entry, which is `AUDITS.md` O-6's own fallback. Place it after 2.12, which is where O-6 proposed it. Renumber 2.11 and 2.12 rather than inserting a lettered task. Give the wardrobe header its own "Style me" button beside the strip, as a task of its own. Scope 2.10a to the home city and leave height, sizes and style notes to Stage 5.
**Reasoning:** **The backend half is already built, and that was measured rather than assumed.** `PATCH /me` has accepted all nine profile fields since 2.2 (149), `GET /me/locations/search` has shipped with no caller since the same task, and `seed_demo.py` writes the demo account's Tel Aviv — so what is missing is a screen and not a mechanism, exactly as O-6's second half says. That is why 2.10a adds no Python and no seed change. **It goes ahead of 2.11** because `POST /looks/suggest` answers `400 home_location_missing` without it (173) and the only way to clear that today is `curl`: the swap improves a look the user can already get, this decides whether she can get one, and 2.11 is on the cut list while this cannot be. **Lettered rather than renumbered**, following 2.6a: 2.11 and 2.12 are named across seven documents and renumbering them would be a rewrite of all of them to buy nothing. **The entry point stays inside 2.12** rather than becoming a task of its own, because `/stylist` already opens on the general request form and the strip is already the tappable route `05` draws — what was missing was the guarantee that it renders when there is no forecast, and that is a sentence in 2.12 rather than a screen. Without it the two gaps close in a circle: no home city means no forecast, no forecast means no strip, no strip means no way to reach the screen that sets the home city.
**Trade-off accepted:** **Half a day added to a stage already past its 5–6 day target**, and 2.10a is the task most likely to be pushed despite being the one that must not be. The live demo row inserted on 2026-08-25 still has no home location whatever this schedules — that is O-20's `--reset` or one `PATCH`, not a task. The strip now has two jobs, a forecast and a link, so a change to either touches one component. And 2.10a builds a form over six fields the stylist reads only two of; scoping it to the home city alone was refused because the same form, the same endpoint and the same commit cover the rest, and `03-AI-CONTRACTS.md`'s `USER PROFILE` block has been structurally empty since 2.4 for want of exactly this screen.

## 175 — The role vocabulary is `04`'s six, and a dress has none

**Decision:** `top · bottom · outer · shoes · bag · accessory` is adopted verbatim from `04-API-SPEC.md` into `02-DATA-MODEL.md`, `app/enums.py` (`Role`) and `frontend/src/app/shared/models/enums.ts` (`ROLES`), with a test on each side transcribing the list. `outerwear` the category is `outer` the role. **`dress` gets no role**, and the ↻ badge is therefore not drawn on a dress tile; `05-FRONTEND-SPEC.md`, which draws a badge on each item, is annotated. The category→role map lives in `enums.ts` alone. `AUDITS.md` **O-25**'s vocabulary half closes.
**Alternative:** Add a seventh `dress` role and widen `replace_role` in `04`. Give a dress the `top` role, which is the closest lie. Draw the badge on a dress and send the locks and the exclusion with no `replace_role`, which the endpoint accepts. Keep the map in `enums.py` as well, or in both.
**Reasoning:** **The six are already on the wire**, so widening them is a contract change 2.11 does not need and `04` is authoritative for the request body. **A dress is genuinely not a single-item swap**: rule 2 admits *a top and a bottom* **or** *a dress*, so replacing the dress can legally come back as two garments, which is a different look — `replace_role`'s sentence in the prompt ("Replace only the X") has no true reading for it. Sending locks with no role was refused because the prompt would then say nothing about what may move while the LOCKED list pins everything else, which is a request for the look that already exists. **The map is `enums.ts`'s alone** because the badge is the only thing that derives a role: an unused `ROLE_BY_CATEGORY` in `enums.py` would be the speculative code `CLAUDE.md` forbids, and the mirror rule is about *values*, which are mirrored.
**Trade-off accepted:** **A dress look can only be re-rolled whole** — the one garment that carries an entire outfit is the one the swap cannot improve, and "Try again" is all it has. The hand-mirror grows a fourth list nothing compares across languages, which is 2.6a's recorded gap unchanged. And the two vocabularies now differ by more than spelling in a way a reader must hold: nine categories, six roles, and no mechanical map between them on the backend.

## 176 — `look_items.role` stays `NULL` at the task that was named to fill it

**Decision:** The column is still written `NULL` by `POST /looks/suggest`. 2.11 adopts the vocabulary and does not adopt a writer. `02-DATA-MODEL.md` and `AUDITS.md` **O-25** say so where the column is printed.
**Alternative:** Derive the role from `category` in `_persist` and write it, which is O-25's first option and now costs one map lookup. Backfill the rows already suggested.
**Reasoning:** **The reader O-25 was waiting for did not arrive.** The swap reads a role off the item's own `category` in the browser, at the moment the badge is tapped, and never out of `look_items` — so the column would be filled for Stage 3's aggregation, one stage early, which is exactly what 170 declined to do. **170's argument survives the vocabulary intact**: a role is derivable from a row the table keeps, where `position` was not, and that asymmetry is what made `position` written and `role` not. Adopting a list is not the same act as filling a column, and only one of the two had a caller today.
**Trade-off accepted:** **O-25 stays open on its index half with a column that is decided but empty**, which reads oddly in a table: the DDL comments a vocabulary the database has never seen a value from. Stage 3 pays for it — the first `role` reader must either backfill or derive at read time, and it will have to choose in a task that also owns the two indexes.

## 177 — `locked_unavailable`, and a role with no locks is the schema's own `422`

**Decision:** A new code `locked_unavailable` (`422`) for `POST /looks/suggest` when any `locked_item_ids` entry names nothing in the wardrobe that would be sent — added to `CONVENTIONS.md`'s list and `04-API-SPEC.md`. `replace_role` without `locked_item_ids` is a Pydantic `model_validator` on `LookSuggestRequest`, so it is the generic `validation_error`. `exclude_item_ids` entries that resolve to no row are **dropped**, not refused.
**Alternative:** One code covering both `422`s. No new code at all, leaving both to `validation_error`. Reuse `anchor_unavailable`. Refuse an unknown excluded id too, for symmetry with the locks.
**Reasoning:** **The line is which of the two a correct client can provoke.** The badge locks the garments that were on screen a moment ago, and one of them can be archived from another tab in between — a real user with a real look, told "check the occasion and the date" by `validation_error`, learns nothing. A role with no locks is a body the badge cannot build, so it needs no sentence of its own and gets the schema's. **Reusing `anchor_unavailable` was refused** because the frontend branches on the code to choose a message, and the two situations need different ones. **An unknown exclusion is dropped** because a lock and an anchor are promises about what the look *will* contain, which an id naming no row makes unkeepable, while an exclusion is a promise about what it will not — and a garment the stylist is never shown is already excluded from every look it can build. Both `422`s are raised before the forecast, on `_anchor`'s rule from 2.10.
**Trade-off accepted:** A seventeenth code in `CONVENTIONS.md`'s list, and a seventh branch in the stylist store's error map. The two `422`s on one endpoint now answer differently for reasons a reader has to look up, and the `locked_unavailable` message has to be vaguer than the anchor's — it names no garment, because the request names several and the route stops at the first.

## 178 — Rule 9 enforces the base-top line, and the prompt line is rewritten to match it

**Decision:** `03-AI-CONTRACTS.md`'s validation table gains **rule 9** — no look contains two items that are `category: top` and `layer: base` — implemented in `_two_base_tops` and called last in `_violation`, in the table's order. In the same commit the system prompt's OUTPUT line changes from *"Never place two `base` tops in the same look unless one is explicitly a layering piece"* to *"Never place two tops tagged layer `base` in the same look. A second top is allowed only when it is tagged `mid`, the layering piece"*, in `03` and in `app/prompts/stylist_system.md` together. No new error code: the retry carries the violation and a second failure is the existing `502 stylist_failed`. `AUDITS.md` **O-28** opens and closes here.
**Alternative:** The rule alone, leaving the prompt as written and letting the retry do the work. The prompt alone, on the argument that a styling preference is not a correctness rule. Read `category: top` only, or `layer: base` only. Fix it in `_normalised` by dropping the second base top instead of rejecting the look.
**Reasoning:** **Both, because each alone fails in a way the other prevents.** The rule alone is correct and expensive — the model does this frequently, so the retry would be spent routinely and a second offence answers `502` to a user who has already waited four to eight seconds. The prompt alone leaves the state O-28 is about: an OUTPUT line with nothing behind it, where every other line in that block has a rule. **The rewrite is what makes the retry rare**: *"unless one is explicitly a layering piece"* asks for a judgement the model can make in prose while returning two rows both tagged `base`, and the new wording names the tag the validator reads, so the prompt and the rule can no longer disagree about what a layering piece is. **Both columns are read** because `LAYERS_BY_CATEGORY` gives `top` the answer `None`: `category` alone would refuse the overshirt the prompt permits, and `layer` alone would refuse the jeans in every look. **Not a coercion in `_normalised`**: dropping an item would hand the user a look the model did not build and rule 2 has not seen, and 164 keeps normalisation to case alone.
**Trade-off accepted:** **The prompt changes, which perturbs every future comparison against a golden set** — free only because 1.11 is still unrun, the same licence 2.6a spent, and it will not be free again. Two files carry the prompt and must be edited together; nothing tests that they match, which is 158's recorded cost paid a second time. And rule 9 will occasionally reject a look a stylist would defend — a fine-knit tank under a heavy shirt is a real outfit when both rows say `base`, and the wardrobe's tagging, not the look, is what would be wrong there.

## 179 — Rule 9 becomes one composition rule, absorbs rule 3, and teaches the fake

**Decision:** Rule 9 is widened from *two base tops* to **one item per slot**: one `outer` layer item, one `base` top, one `bottom`, one `dress`, one pair of `shoes`, one `bag`, at most two accessories, and no separate `top` or `bottom` beside a `dress`. It is a table — `SLOT_RULES` — read in one function, `_slot_conflict`. **Rule 3 is absorbed into it** and keeps its number in `03-AI-CONTRACTS.md` as a struck pointer. `_fake_items` gains the same rule, so it never joins a dress with the pair. No new error code, no renumbering, no endpoint change. `AUDITS.md` **O-28** widened and closed again.
**Alternative:** A second rule for bottoms beside rule 9's tops, and a third for the dress. Leave rule 3 standing and let rule 9 cover only the slots it does not. Renumber the table so the absorbed rule leaves no gap. Put the dress exclusion in rule 2, which already knows about dresses. Leave the fake alone and record it as an audit item.
**Reasoning:** **The bug was never about tops.** 2.11a fixed the instance it was shown; the class is that the table counted no slot at all — rule 2 asks whether a bottom is *present* and never how many, so two pairs of jeans passed every rule there was. A per-slot rule written one slot at a time would have been a third task on the same bug. **A table rather than seven loops**, because what varies between slots is only which column names them, and `LAYERS_BY_CATEGORY` is why the columns differ: `outer` is a layer question (a cardigan under a coat is legal), the base top needs both columns (`top` is the one category with no layer answer), and the rest are plain categories. **The dress clause stays in rule 9 rather than rule 2** because it is a *count*, not a completeness check: rule 2 says nothing is missing, rule 9 says nothing is worn twice, and a dress beside jeans is the second question. **Rule 3 keeps its number** — eight documents, a test and three code comments name it, and a renumbering buys nothing but broken references, which is 167's lesson about migrations one document along. **The fake was fixed rather than filed** because generalising the rule is what broke it: an anchored or locked dress produced a dress-plus-pair look, so every such request under `USE_FAKE_AI` — the flag the E2E job runs with — would have answered `502`. Measured by running it, not by reading it.
**Trade-off accepted:** **A shirt worn open over a dress is now refused**, because the rule reads categories and not intent; a coat over a dress is unaffected, and the honest answer for the shirt is that this wardrobe cannot express it. **The violation messages changed shape** — "the look contains 2 bottoms and may contain at most 1: …" — so two existing assertions moved with them. **Rule 3's number is now a pointer to a rule with a different number**, which a reader has to follow once. And the fake has now been taught three rules; the next one to arrive will find `_fake_items` harder to reason about than the stylist it stands in for, which is **O-27**'s open question and not this task's.

## 180 — The weather strip carries a labelled link, and fetches for itself

**Decision:** `WeatherStrip` is a component beside `pending-strip.ts`, rendered above every branch of the wardrobe page. It holds the weather line **and a labelled `Style me` link into `/stylist`** rather than being tappable as a whole, and the link renders in all three states: a forecast, no home location (the temperature is replaced by a `/profile` link), and a home location whose forecast has not arrived or failed (nothing on the left, silently). It reads `home_lat`/`home_lon` off `AuthService.currentUser()` and calls `GET /weather` itself, prints the day's **high**, and imports `todayInLocalTime` from the stylist's form. No glyph. `05-FRONTEND-SPEC.md` is annotated where it drew the strip as the tap target and drew an emoji.
**Alternative:** Wrap the whole strip in one anchor and drop the `/profile` link from the degraded state, or move that prompt outside the strip. Pass `StylistStore.weather()` in from the wardrobe page and call `loadWeather` there. Render `temp_min–temp_max` as the stylist's form does. Map the eight conditions to eight emoji. Hide the strip entirely when there is no forecast.
**Reasoning:** **The degraded state is what decides the markup.** `STAGE-2` §2.12 requires a prompt linking to `/profile` inside the strip and the tap into `/stylist` in the same breath, and an anchor inside an anchor is not a document — so one of them has to be a labelled control, and it is the one that must never disappear. **Hiding the strip was refused outright**: it is the only route into `/stylist` in the application, and the account most likely to have no home location is the one that has never been to `/profile`. **Fetching for itself** keeps the wardrobe screen from depending on the state container of a screen it links to; the cost is ten lines that `StylistStore.loadWeather` also has, and the alternative is a coupling in the opposite direction to every other line in this codebase. **The day's high** is the number `summarize_forecast` prints to the model (142), so the strip and the prompt cannot disagree about today on one screen; a range would be a second reading of the same forecast. **No emoji** because eight conditions have one glyph between them in the mock, and a sun over *Rain* is a wrong statement where no glyph is only a missing one.
**Trade-off accepted:** **The strip is busier than `05` drew it** — a line and a button where the mock has one tappable row — and on a narrow screen the link wraps to its own line. The fetch is duplicated in the sense that two components now ask the same question of the same account, and a third caller would be the moment to extract it rather than now. And a user whose forecast fails sees a strip with no weather in it and no explanation, which is deliberate but is the state most likely to be reported as a bug.

## 181 — Migration 0004, the feedback constants, and the CHECK spelled as raw DDL

**Decision:** Migration `0004_feedback` adds `looks.feedback` (`SMALLINT`, no default), `looks.worn_at` (`DATE`), `items.wear_count` (`INT NOT NULL DEFAULT 0`) and `items.last_worn_at` (`DATE`), and builds **O-25's two indexes** — `idx_looks_user_id` and `idx_look_items_item_id` — after `02-DATA-MODEL.md` prints both. The `feedback` CHECK is named `ck_looks_feedback_values` and written as `op.execute("ALTER TABLE looks ADD CONSTRAINT …")` rather than `op.create_check_constraint`. `FEEDBACK_UP = 1` and `FEEDBACK_DOWN = -1` are `Final` constants in `app/models/look.py`, and the `CheckConstraint` is built from them. `AUDITS.md` **O-25 closed**.
**Alternative:** `ck_looks_feedback_range`, matching the three existing `_range` names. `op.create_check_constraint` with the bare name `feedback_values`, letting the convention expand it. The two indexes deferred to 3.2 and 3.5 in a migration `0005`, renumbering `trips` to `0006`. `MIN_FEEDBACK`/`MAX_FEEDBACK`, following `height_cm`. A `server_default` of `0` on `feedback`. The literals `-1` and `1` inside the constraint string, as `0001` spells `height_cm`'s.
**Reasoning:** **`op.create_check_constraint` produces the wrong name here, and this is the one place in the project where it would.** `env.py` sets `target_metadata = Base.metadata`, and alembic builds the constraint on a `MetaData` carrying that object's `naming_convention` — so the literal `ck_looks_feedback_values` goes in and **`ck_looks_ck_looks_feedback_values` comes out**, the `ck` template applied a second time to a name that already has it. Measured with `alembic upgrade --sql` before the file was written, not inferred: `0001`–`0003` never met this because `0001` spells its CHECKs inside `create_table`, where no expansion happens, and `0002`–`0003` add none. Raw DDL keeps `02-DATA-MODEL.md`'s rule that a migration spells its constraint names literally, which the alternative would have quietly broken in the direction of a name nobody chose. **`_values` rather than `_range`** because `feedback IN (-1, 1)` is set membership: `_range` would be a false description in the schema, and a false name is worse than an inconsistent one. **The indexes are `0004`'s because Stage 3 has one migration** — a second would renumber `0005_trips` again, and 167 measured that sweep across eight documents and three code comments — and O-25's own condition, that `02` print a schema before a migration builds it, is honoured in that order. **`FEEDBACK_UP`/`FEEDBACK_DOWN` rather than `MIN`/`MAX`** because there is nothing between them to admit; `height_cm`'s two numbers describe an interval and these two describe a set, and naming them as bounds would invite exactly the `BETWEEN` that admits `0`. **No `server_default` on `feedback`** so that an unrated look is `NULL`: with `0` as a default, "nobody rated this" and a neutral rating would be one value, and 3.5's count of rated looks would need a predicate that the absence of a default gives it free.
**Trade-off accepted:** **The raw-DDL CHECK is the only constraint in the project not built through an alembic op**, and it is invisible to anything that reads the migration structurally — a reader comparing `0004` to `0001` sees two spellings of one idea. **The index names are spelled twice, in `0004` and in `__table_args__`, and nothing compares them** — `test_db_naming.py` closes that seam for constraints and cannot for indexes, because `Table.constraints` does not hold one; recorded on O-25 rather than fixed. Both follow `idx_items_wardrobe`'s spelling rather than the convention's `ix_` prefix, so the project now has an index-naming precedent that its own `naming_convention` does not generate. And **four columns now exist that nothing reads**: `wear_count` and `last_worn_at` until 3.4 and 3.6, `feedback` until 3.3, `worn_at` until 3.4 — which is the same shape as `look_items.position` at 2.7 and is defensible for the same reason, that a migration is the one artefact it is expensive to add late.
**Corrected at 3.3:** this entry promised that "3.3's `Literal[FEEDBACK_UP, FEEDBACK_DOWN]` imports them", giving one definition honoured in three places *with the compiler between them*. That spelling does not type-check — PEP 586 admits literal values only, and `Final` does not exempt a name — so the schema transcribes `Literal[-1, 1]` and `tests/unit/test_look_schemas.py` compares `get_args` against the constants. The claim about the **`CheckConstraint`** is unaffected and still holds: the migration and the model are built from the constants themselves. What is withdrawn is the third place and the word *compiler*. `DECISIONS.md` 183.

## 182 — `LookResponse` is one shape for every look, and the heart is not optimistic

**Decision:** `SuggestedLook` is renamed `LookResponse`, gains `is_saved`, and is what `POST /looks/suggest`, `GET /looks` and `PATCH /looks/{id}` all return. `feedback` and `worn_at` stay off it until 3.3 and 3.4. `GET /looks` mirrors `GET /items` exactly — `{looks, total}`, `created_at DESC`, default `100`, cap `200` — with `id` as the tiebreaker instead of `short_id`. `LookUpdate` takes `is_saved` and `title`, `extra="forbid"`, and **refuses `null` on either** through a `model_validator`. On the client, a new `LooksStore` owns `listSaved` and `update`; the heart writes through it and is **not optimistic**; `/saved` renders its own rows rather than reusing `LookCard`.
**Alternative:** A second response type for the persisted reads, leaving suggest's wire shape alone. Adding `feedback` and `worn_at` now, so the class changes once instead of three times. `is_saved: bool = False` on `LookUpdate`, relying on `exclude_unset` and never reading the default. Extending `StylistStore` rather than adding a second store. An optimistic heart. Reusing `LookCard` on `/saved` with two inputs turning off its swap badges and its "Try again".
**Reasoning:** **One row described twice is the thing that drifts.** 3.3 and 3.4 both answer with this object and Stage 4's trip looks are the same rows again, so a second near-identical type would have four writers and nothing keeping them in step. The cost is a real wire change — every suggest response now carries `is_saved: false` — and it is a **truthful addition** rather than padding: the row genuinely is unsaved at that moment, read off `row.is_saved` rather than written as a literal. **`feedback` and `worn_at` wait**, which means this class changes in three consecutive commits; that churn is the price of one-task-at-a-time and is cheaper than two fields no endpoint writes and no screen draws. **`null` is refused by a validator rather than by a default that is never read**: `is_saved` is `NOT NULL`, so an accepted `null` is an `IntegrityError` and a `500` with no `code`, and `ItemUpdate` types its fields the same way while meaning the opposite by them — there, clearing is the point — so the difference is stated in code rather than left to be inferred. **A second store because they are different jobs**: `StylistStore` makes a look, `LooksStore` changes one that exists, and the clearest evidence is that the stylist screen uses both at once. **Not optimistic** because `STAGE-3` asks for that at 3.3 and not here, and because the response *is* the look: rendering it is exact, where a locally toggled copy is a guess that happens to be right.
**Trade-off accepted:** **The stylist page now merges two stores by id** — `StylistStore` holds what the stylist answered and `LooksStore` what `PATCH` answered, and neither can hand the other a look, so a five-line `computed` prefers the newer. Without it the heart fills and empties again on the next render. **Adding `is_saved` to the suggest response broke no test**, because nothing anywhere pinned what a look on the wire contains; a test now transcribes the key set from `04-API-SPEC.md`, and it is the reason 3.3 and 3.4 will not land the same way silently. **`/saved` duplicates no logic but shares no component either** — the two presentations of a look will drift if one is restyled, which is accepted because the alternative was a card carrying inputs whose only purpose is to disable its own features. And **the screen has no entry point**, the third in this project: `AUDITS.md` **O-29**.

## 183 — `feedback` is transcribed not imported, `null` clears it, and every control on the card is optimistic

**Decision:** `LookUpdate.feedback` and `LookResponse.feedback` are `Literal[-1, 1] | None`, transcribed from `FEEDBACK_UP`/`FEEDBACK_DOWN` rather than importing them, with `tests/unit/test_look_schemas.py` comparing `get_args` against the constants. **`feedback: null` clears the rating** and is the one field of the three that may be cleared; `_UNCLEARABLE` names the other two. `LooksStore.update` becomes **optimistic for every control**, takes the whole `Look` rather than an id, and rolls back exactly on failure — which **supersedes 182's "not optimistic"** for the heart. The stylist page's error line reads both stores. `test_feedback_is_refused_until_task_3_3` is deleted.
**Alternative:** `Literal[FEEDBACK_UP, FEEDBACK_DOWN]`, as 181 promised. A `Feedback(IntEnum)`. A rating that can only be replaced, never withdrawn. Optimistic thumbs beside a non-optimistic heart, honouring 182 literally. A second store method for the optimistic path. Passing an id and having the store look the row up.
**Reasoning:** **181's spelling does not type-check and this was measured, not guessed**: `error: Parameter 1 of Literal[...] is invalid`. PEP 586 admits literal values only and `Final` does not exempt a name — and Pydantic accepts it happily at runtime, so nothing but `mypy` reports it, which is why 181 could promise it in good faith. An `IntEnum` type-checks and was rejected for a different reason: `enums.py`'s `Vocabulary` base is a `StrEnum`, so `Feedback` would fork the one place this project keeps closed vocabularies, which is a larger drift than swapping compiler-enforcement for test-enforcement. **`null` clears** because `NULL` is a real state — unrated — and the only one of the three fields whose empty value a screen can render; a mis-tap that could not be withdrawn would permanently change what 3.5 tells the stylist, which is the opposite of what feedback is for. **One optimistic path rather than two behaviours** because `STAGE-3` asks for optimism at 3.3 and a single `PATCH` method that was eager for one field and patient for another is a difference no reader could reconstruct from the code; consistency inside one method is worth more than consistency with an entry written one task earlier, and saying so here is cheaper than leaving the seam. **It takes the whole look** because the optimistic render has to merge into something, and the stylist screen's look lives in `StylistStore` where this store cannot reach it.
**Trade-off accepted:** **The compiler is no longer between the constants and the schema** — a transcribed literal with a test between them is exactly the shape `CONVENTIONS.md`'s "limits and units" section collects instances of, and this is the fifth. It is the honest description: 181 claimed three places and the compiler, and there are two places and a test. **A flaky network now flips a control and flips it back**, which is the cost of optimism and is honest state rather than a bug. **The rollback restores `updated` to `null` rather than to the pre-tap look**, because on a first tap it *was* null and leaving a value behind would start overriding a card the store never changed. And **a failed save said nothing on the stylist screen from 3.2 until now** — the error line read `StylistStore` alone; found by 3.3's rollback test rather than by review, and fixed for both controls in this commit.

## 184 — Wearing is one date on the look, forward-only on the item, and the one control that is not optimistic

**Decision:** `POST /looks/{id}/wear` takes a required `date` with **no upper bound**, and writes in one transaction. The look is updated by a conditional statement — `UPDATE looks SET worn_at = :date WHERE id = :id AND worn_at IS DISTINCT FROM :date RETURNING id` — and the items are incremented **only if that returned a row**. `items.last_worn_at` moves with `GREATEST(COALESCE(last_worn_at, :date), :date)`, never backwards. `is_saved` is not consulted. `LookResponse` gains `worn_at` and `ItemResponse` gains `wear_count` and `last_worn_at`, so both are on every payload that carries an item. On the client, `LooksStore.wear` is **not optimistic**, and the saved-looks button stays after a successful tap, disabled and reading "Worn today", comparing against the browser's `todayInLocalTime()`.
**Alternative:** Reading `look.worn_at` in Python and branching on it. `rowcount` instead of `RETURNING`. A plain assignment for `last_worn_at`. A `409` on a second date, or overwriting `worn_at` without incrementing. Refusing a future date. Defaulting `date` to the server's today. Gating the endpoint on `is_saved`. A `look_wears` table keyed `(look_id, worn_on)`, which would make idempotency exact. An optimistic wear, honouring 183. Hiding the button once the look is worn.
**Reasoning:** **The guard is a `WHERE`, not an `if`, because the failure it prevents is a race.** Read-then-write leaves a gap exactly wide enough for the second of two rapid taps: both read `NULL`, both increment, and the counts are wrong with nothing raised anywhere. `IS DISTINCT FROM` rather than `!=` because the column is `NULL` on every look never worn and `NULL != date` is `NULL`, which is not `TRUE` — the first wearing would never record. `RETURNING` rather than `rowcount` because `Session.execute` is typed `Result[Any]` and `rowcount` belongs to `CursorResult`, so the obvious spelling needs a cast; the mutation that swapped `IS DISTINCT FROM` for `!=` killed nine tests. **`GREATEST` because 3.5 reads that column**: it asks which garments were worn in the last three days, and a correction entered for last Tuesday that dragged a garment worn yesterday backwards would silently un-hide it in the next prompt. The consequence is stated rather than hidden — a look's `worn_at` and its items' `last_worn_at` may disagree, and both are true. **A different date is a genuine second wearing** because it is: the person wore the outfit twice. **No future-date check** because the client sends the local today its browser is standing in, and east of UTC that is routinely the server's tomorrow — a refusal would be the one thing `CONVENTIONS.md` forbids, a `422` a correct client provokes. **Not optimistic, and this is a narrow exception to 183 rather than a reversal of it.** That entry's argument was that a toggle should answer the finger and can be rolled back; none of its three premises holds here — wearing is not a toggle, the response changes `wear_count` on every garment and the client cannot derive those numbers, and there is no previous state to restore because nothing is written before the answer. A guess that can be neither checked nor withdrawn is not optimism.
**Trade-off accepted:** **Idempotency reaches exactly one date deep.** `looks.worn_at` is one column, so Monday → Tuesday → Monday counts three wearings; the column cannot remember a day it has overwritten and nothing else in the schema does. `test_idempotency_reaches_back_exactly_one_date` asserts that number so the limitation is a decision with a name rather than a bug someone finds later, and `02-DATA-MODEL.md` names `look_wears` as the fix if it is ever needed. It is not built: a fourth table and a second migration in the stage that is the designated cut line, to correct a miscount that needs a user recording wearings out of order. **Two more fields on `ItemResponse` means every item payload in the application grew**, including the hydrated items inside every look, and **three backend fixtures failed to construct until they were written in** — the collection error named all three at once, which is the server finally getting the property the frontend has had since 3.2, where the compiler failed ten spec fixtures for the same reason. `app/services/serializer.py` names every field it prints, so neither field reaches the stylist prompt. **The button contradicts 183 in the code and says so in a comment**, which is the second time this project has taken a documented exception to a rule one task old. And **the last-worn date is rendered as a raw ISO day** — this application has no date-formatting layer, and adding one for a single line would be the speculative abstraction `CONVENTIONS.md` forbids.

## 185 — The preferences block needs three *rated* looks, and "recently" is relative to the day being dressed

**Decision:** The learned-preferences block is omitted until the user has **three looks with a non-`NULL` `feedback`** — thumbs-down counts toward the threshold exactly as thumbs-up does. `_CATEGORY_NAMES` carries **all nine** members of `Category`, and `tests/integration/test_looks_suggest.py` pins the dictionary against `Category.values()`. The recency window is the **requested date and the two calendar days before it, closed at both ends**, rather than a window anchored on the server's `date.today()`. **Archived garments are excluded from both halves** of the derivation, matching the filter `_wardrobe` already applies.
**Alternative:** `STAGE-3` 3.5's literal "3 liked looks", counting only `feedback = 1`. Counting saved looks toward the threshold as well. A `.get(category, category)` fallback instead of completing the dictionary. `date.today()`, as first written. An open-ended `last_worn_at >= since` with no upper bound. Leaving archived rows in, on the argument that a preference outlives the garment.
**Reasoning:** **A thumbs-down is a statement about this wardrobe, and it is half of what the block prints.** Gating on likes alone would mean a user who rated three looks down — a user the stylist most needs to hear from — is told nothing, while the "Disliked:" line the same block advertises could never appear before three *other* looks were rated up. `NULL` is the only value that means nothing was said, which is exactly the distinction `DECISIONS.md` 181 refused a `server_default` in order to keep. **The dictionary is completed rather than made forgiving**, because a `.get` fallback would print a raw enum value into a prompt and nothing would ever report it; a missing key should be loud, and the test is what makes it loud at the right time. `Category` gained `swimwear` and `sleepwear` at 2.6a and the dictionary was written with seven of nine — and `Item.fit IS NOT NULL`, the query's own filter, selects **precisely the six categories that carry a fit**, two of which were the missing ones. Reachable the moment `STYLIST_EXCLUDED_CATEGORIES` is emptied, which `DECISIONS.md` 169 documents as supported. **The window follows the request** because asking on Wednesday for Saturday's outfit is asking what will be stale on Saturday, and because `date.today()` is the *server's* calendar day, which 184 established one task earlier is not reliably the user's — the client already sends the date it means. **Closed at the top** because `POST /looks/{id}/wear` deliberately accepts a future date (184), so an open window would report a garment worn next week as already repeated. **Archived rows are dropped** because `_wardrobe` will never show that garment to the model again, so a preference learned from it can only describe outfits the stylist cannot build.
**Trade-off accepted:** **The threshold now admits a user whose entire history is negative**, and the block that results has a `Disliked:` line and nothing else — honest, and thinner than the section header promises. **The two halves are still scoped differently**: recency runs over the styleable wardrobe (`ready`, unarchived, not an excluded category) while the fit aggregation filters archived only, so a `processing` row or an excluded-category garment can still teach a preference. That asymmetry is deliberate — a preference is about a garment the user *owns*, not one the stylist can reach today — but it is the seam to reopen if the block ever reads oddly. **A `NULL` `category` on a row with a non-`NULL` `fit` would still be a `KeyError`**; the query does not exclude it, and no such row can exist while `category` is required for `ready` and cannot be cleared (123). **And the fixture that pinned the recency line was anchored on `date.today()` while requesting `2026-03-14`** — it passed only because the window was anchored on the same wrong clock, which is the shape `06-TESTING-STRATEGY.md` calls a derived expectation.

## 186 — The wear numbers are `ready`-scoped, `most_worn` is one object or `null`, and cost-per-wear is struck rather than faked

**Decision:** `GET /items/stats` reads `items.wear_count`. Over the rows that are the caller's, **unarchived and `ready`**, a single statement counts `worn` (`wear_count > 0`) and `never_worn` (`wear_count = 0`) as a partition; `most_worn` is the worn half ordered `wear_count DESC, short_id ASC`, limited to one. `most_worn` is typed **`MostWornItem | None`** — `id`, `display_name`, `wear_count` — replacing the `list[ItemResponse]` that `04-API-SPEC.md` had printed as `[ { item } ]` since Stage 0. Swimwear and sleepwear are **not** excluded. **Cost-per-wear is removed from `STAGE-3` §3.6** rather than built as a placeholder. Four tests replace `test_the_wear_numbers_are_zero_until_stage_3`, which is deleted.
**Alternative:** Ship `never_worn` alone, as `STAGE-3` §3.6 asks. Two separate count queries instead of one `FILTER` pair. Keep `list[ItemResponse]` and return a one-element list. Keep the wide shape as a single object. Add `short_id` or `image_url` to the narrow one. Scope the wear numbers exactly as `item_stats` already does — every status — or exactly as `_wardrobe` does, excluding swimwear and sleepwear too. Break ties on `last_worn_at DESC`. Fold `never_worn` into the existing grouped query as `count(*) FILTER (WHERE …)`. Ship `cost_per_wear: null`, or `0`, to keep the field the stage file promised.
**Reasoning:** **The placeholder was struck because there is no price and there is no plan for one.** `02-DATA-MODEL.md` lists purchase price among the future `attributes` keys, nothing writes it, and no screen collects it — so every spelling of the field is a number the application invented. A `null` would be the honest one and would still put a labelled cost-per-wear row on a panel whose entire premise is telling the user something true they did not know; the first person to see it would reasonably conclude the app knows what their clothes cost. Removing it from the plan is cheaper now than a field with no producer, which `CONVENTIONS.md`'s error-code section already refused once in `tagging_failed`'s name. **One object rather than a list of one** because the two shapes answer different questions and the list was answering neither: a caller holding `most_worn` has to decide whether an empty array means *nothing worn* or *nothing returned*, and `null` says the first without being asked. **Three fields rather than twenty-odd** because the panel draws a line and a link, `id` is what `/wardrobe/:id` routes on, and the narrowing costs nothing — nothing was ever written against the wide shape, on either side of the wire. `short_id` and `image_url` were considered and left out on the rule that a field is cheap to add and expensive to remove. **`ready` is the one filter the wear numbers add**, taken from `_wardrobe`; a `processing` row has never been worn in the arithmetic sense and has no `display_name` to put on a panel, so counting it would inflate the one number the whole feature exists to print. **The category exclusion is deliberately *not* taken from `_wardrobe`**: `STYLIST_EXCLUDED_CATEGORIES` says what the stylist may recommend, not what the wardrobe holds, and `total` and `by_category` in the same response body count swimwear — a `never_worn` that did not would put two numbers on one panel that no reader could reconcile. **`short_id` breaks ties** because `GET /items` already uses it for the same reason, and because `last_worn_at DESC` would be a second nullable column to reason about for a tiebreak nobody will look at. **`worn` is not in the stage file and is here anyway**, because *"34 items you have never worn"* is a numerator with no denominator on the screen and every number already in the body answers *of how many?* wrongly — `total` counts `processing` and `failed` rows the wear numbers exclude. The pair is counted in **one statement** rather than two, so it partitions its population by construction: at `READ COMMITTED` two separate counts can straddle a concurrent `POST /looks/{id}/wear` and fail to sum to the rows they were counted over.
**Trade-off accepted:** **`total - never_worn` is still not the number of items the user has worn**, because the two are scoped differently — `worn` is that number, and the response now carries two plausible denominators where it carried one. That is stated in the route docstring, in `04-API-SPEC.md` and here, because it is the arithmetic a panel author will do by instinct. **The response now describes an item in two widths** — `ItemResponse` everywhere else, `MostWornItem` in this one field — which is the first time this project has narrowed a shape rather than reused one, and the opposite of the judgement 182 made for `LookResponse`. It is defensible only because there is exactly one reader; a second would be the moment to reconsider. **Five queries where there were three**, and the two wear counts are a `FILTER` pair while the four counts above them are three `GROUP BY` queries — one endpoint now counts rows in two idioms, which is the price of a partition that holds under concurrency. **The queries stay in the route handler**, against `CONVENTIONS.md`'s rule that routes call services: `item_stats` already had three inline, and extracting two would leave four counts in the handler and two behind a function. And **`most_worn is None` is asserted only in the archived-exclusion test**, where the sole worn garment is archived — one branch, one test, and it is covered for a reason the fixture states rather than because nothing was planted.

## 187 — The saved-looks entry point is a fifth bespoke link, against `AUDITS.md` O-29's written recommendation

**Decision:** `/saved` gets a **Saved** anchor in the wardrobe's account row, beside **Profile**, copying that link exactly — `inline-flex min-h-11`, same classes, new i18n key `wardrobe.saved`. No navigation component, no link from the look card, no FAB. `05-FRONTEND-SPEC.md` §2 gains the account row it had never drawn, with both links. `AUDITS.md` **O-29 stays open** and records this as its fifth instance; its stale "`/profile` is still in that state" sentence is corrected in the same commit, as is `app.routes.ts`'s comment on the `/saved` route.
**Alternative:** Build the navigation component O-29 asks for and delete all five bespoke controls. Leave `/saved` unreachable until Stage 5 owns the navigation. Link to `/saved` from the look card's heart, where saving happens. A FAB. Close O-29's entry-point half on the grounds that every screen is now reachable.
**Reasoning:** **O-29 asked for the navigation and this is not it, which is why the item stays open.** The argument for shipping the link anyway is scheduling, stated plainly: `STAGE-3` is the **designated cut line**, no stage file anywhere owns a navigation component, and `05-FRONTEND-SPEC.md` §2 — the section O-29 reserves for it — draws a header with no nav in the mockup, so building one here means designing it as well as writing it. Against that, an unreachable `/saved` makes **all three of Stage 3's user-facing features invisible**: a first-time viewer cannot see a saved look, and therefore cannot see that rating or wearing exists either. A stage that is complete and invisible is worse than a stage with one more link in it. **The look card was refused** because saving happens there and *finding what you saved* does not; the same fifth control, put in a place nobody returns to, and it would put navigation inside a component that is rendered in two screens. **A FAB was refused** because §2 already has one, for **Add items**, and two floating buttons on one screen is a worse answer than two anchors in a row that already holds two controls. **Closing O-29's entry-point half was refused** because the item was opened over a count of three and closing it at five would record the count going up as the resolution — the opposite of what it says.
**Trade-off accepted:** **The debt is now larger and more expensive to pay**, and this commit is what made it so: §2 was the one place the navigation was going to be written, and it has now been edited to describe the thing that stands in for it, so whoever builds the nav deletes prose as well as five controls. **The account row holds three controls in a `flex-wrap`**, which on a narrow screen wraps to two lines — acceptable for a row whose contents were already two, and the reason both links keep `min-h-11` rather than becoming plain inline text. **Nothing tests that the row is reachable end to end**: `wardrobe.page.spec.ts` asserts each anchor's `href` and label, and no E2E covers the journey, because `STAGE-5` 5.3 owns the eleven journeys and this is a link. Measured by mutation in the mirror — deleting the anchor fails `links to the saved-looks screen` and nothing else. And **the i18n key is a bare noun**, `Saved`, where every other label in the row is a noun or a verb phrase that names its destination; it reads as a filter rather than a screen, and the honest fix is the navigation, not a longer string.

## 188 — The insights panel hides when nothing has been worn, counts against the tagged wardrobe, and holds no store

**Decision:** `/wardrobe` gains `app-wardrobe-insights`, between the weather strip and the pending strip. It calls a new `ItemsApi.stats()` **once, in its constructor**, holds the response in a private signal and **owns no store**. It renders a `<section>` only when `worn > 0`; on `never_worn > 0` the line is *"{{count}} of your {{ready}} tagged items have never been worn"* where **`ready` is `worn + never_worn`**, on `never_worn === 0` it is *"You have worn everything in your wardrobe"*, and a second line names `most_worn` and links to `/wardrobe/:id`. A failed request renders nothing and says nothing. Six `wardrobe.insights.*` keys; `ItemStatsResponse` and `MostWornItem` mirror the backend schema field for field.
**Alternative:** Print `never_worn` alone, exactly as `STAGE-3` §3.6 writes it, leaving `worn` with no caller. Use `total` as the denominator. Put the stats on `WardrobeStore`, or in a new `StatsStore`. Soften the brand-new-user state with an onboarding line — *"wear a look and Bijoux starts counting"* — instead of hiding the panel. Hide the panel on `never_worn === 0` too, rather than printing a second sentence. Refetch whenever `items()` changes. Gate the request on the wardrobe having loaded, so an empty account does not ask.
**Reasoning:** **The denominator is the whole of why `worn` exists (186), and `total` cannot be it.** The wear numbers are `ready`-scoped and `total` counts every status, so a wardrobe with two failed uploads would put *"of 136"* on this panel under a header saying *"138 items"* — two counts of one wardrobe, on one screen, differing by rows the grid is simultaneously showing as failures. Naming the population is what makes that gap legible rather than arithmetic, and **"tagged" is the word the grid already uses**: a `processing` tile says *Tagging…* and a `failed` one offers to tag again. **Nothing worn is not an insight.** The only sentence available on a brand-new wardrobe is *"you have never worn any of your 40 items"*, which the user knows, and which is false about her life rather than about the data — the application has not been told yet. A panel whose premise is telling her something true she did not know has nothing, so it renders nothing; the onboarding line was refused because no document asks for it and it is an empty state dressed as a statistic. **`never_worn === 0` is softened rather than hidden** because it is the same statistic at its far end and it is genuinely rare — hiding it would throw away the one moment the panel gets to report success. **No store, on `WeatherStrip`'s precedent (180).** `WardrobeStore` is `providedIn: 'root'`, outlives the page, and holds the collection the poll and the upload path mutate; a `ready`-scoped snapshot beside `total` in it would be a second count of the same wardrobe with no owner for reconciling the two. A `StatsStore` is a root singleton with one consumer and one field, which is the speculative abstraction `CONVENTIONS.md` forbids. **One fetch, at construction**, because Angular re-creates the component on every entry to `/wardrobe` — so a wearing recorded on `/stylist` is reflected on the way back — and because refetching on `items()` would fire a request per poll tick.
**Trade-off accepted:** **The numbers are a snapshot and can be wrong on screen.** An archive made on the item detail screen and returned from within one visit leaves the panel a garment ahead; an upload does not, because a new row is `processing` and outside the `ready` scope until tagging finishes. **A brand-new account still pays for the request** whose answer is guaranteed to render nothing — gating it on the wardrobe having loaded was the alternative and it is the coupling this entry spent its main argument refusing. **Every fixture in `wardrobe.page.spec.ts` now answers a second request**, thirty-eight of them, through one `flushStats()` in `create()`; the wardrobe nothing has been worn in is the shape it flushes, so the page's own tests never render the panel and its states are tested in one place. **The panel is a third bespoke block in `features/wardrobe/`** with no shared card or panel primitive behind it — `AUDITS.md` **O-15** is the same shape one component over, and this does not close it. And **the copy carries a number the wardrobe header does not**: two counts on one screen that a reader must reconcile through the word *tagged*, where printing `never_worn` alone would have carried none.

## 189 — Trip packing gets its own response schema, beside `outfit_recommendation` rather than inside it

**Decision:** Stage 4 builds a **second** `json_schema` for the trip call. `STYLIST_SCHEMA` is left exactly as 2.4 built it and 2.5 narrowed it: no `packing_list`, no `day`. `suggest_looks` stays one function serving both callers, with the schema a parameter rather than a constant. The trip schema's `name`, its day object and its `packing_list` shape are 4.3's to settle with `03-AI-CONTRACTS.md` open beside it; this entry fixes only that there are two schemas and not one.
**Alternative:** Extend `outfit_recommendation` with `packing_list` and `day`, which is what `03-AI-CONTRACTS.md` has described since Stage 0 — one schema, one validator, one shape. Or extend it and make the two new fields nullable through `anyOf`, so a single-day call can answer `null` to both.
**Reasoning:** **Strict mode has no optional property.** `_object()` derives `required` from `properties` precisely so the two cannot drift, so every field added to `STYLIST_SCHEMA` is a field the model must emit on **every** call — and `POST /looks/suggest` is the only caller for the whole of Stages 2 and 3. That is the exact condition **157** refused when it deferred `packing_list`: *"shipping it now would mean the model emitting `packing_list: null` on every call this project makes for two stages."* Extending the schema at Stage 4 does not escape that sentence, it reinstates it — the single-day path would carry a packing list for a trip of length one for the rest of the project. The `anyOf` variant is the same thing spelled longer: a field that is null on every single-day call is what 157 measured the cost of, and **163** struck `day` on top of it after the model filled it from the date. `03`'s "a single day is a trip of length one with no packing list" is a sentence one schema cannot make true and two can. The reuse that matters is not the schema anyway: `_LOOK_PROPERTIES` is a dict, `validate_look_response`'s rules 1, 2 and 9 already loop over `response.looks`, and `suggest_looks` sends whatever schema it is handed — so the second schema is a second *shape*, not a second contract.
**Trade-off accepted:** **Two schemas that must be kept in step, with nothing but a shared dict between them.** A change to what a look object holds has to be made in both places, and the compiler cannot see it — the same drift `CONVENTIONS.md`'s "Limits and units" describes, one layer up. `validate_look_response` now judges two response shapes, and whether that is one function taking `expected_days` or two functions is 4.3's decision rather than this one's. **`AUDITS.md` O-26 is not closed by this and is not answered here**: `Look.occasion` still has no reader on the single-day schema, and a per-day trip occasion gives the field a reader only in the schema this entry declines to merge it with. O-26 asked for the decision at Stage 4 and it is still owed, now on the narrower question of whether the single-look field survives alone.

## 190 — `end_date <= today + 14`, capped in the picker, with no climate-average fallback

**Decision:** A trip is refused unless its **last** day is within fourteen days of today. The frontend date picker caps the range at task 4.5, and `POST /trips/pack` enforces the same bound. Stage 4 builds **no** seasonal or climate-average fallback — neither as data nor as a suggested alternative in the error body.
**Alternative:** `04-API-SPEC.md`'s literal wording, which bounds `start_date` at fourteen days ahead and says nothing about the end. Or bind against the measured horizon, `FORECAST_HORIZON_DAYS = 15`, and spend the extra day. Or build the fallback both `04-API-SPEC.md` ("offer seasonal averages as a fallback message") and `STAGE-4` 4.2 ("rather than guessing from seasonal averages") gesture at from opposite directions.
**Reasoning:** **The three documents did not close, and the gap is eleven days wide.** `STAGE-4` 4.2 says cap requests at 14, `04-API-SPEC.md` says `start_date` no more than 14 days ahead, and `weather.py` carries `FORECAST_HORIZON_DAYS = 15` measured against the live provider on 2026-08-26. Under the `start_date` reading, a legal fourteen-day trip beginning on day 14 ends on day 27 — thirteen days past anything Open-Meteo will answer for — so the bound has to bind the **last** day or it does not bind the forecast at all. Fourteen rather than the measured fifteen buys one day of margin against a horizon that rolls forward daily and was measured on a single day: a range built in a browser at 23:50 local and evaluated on a server already into tomorrow loses exactly one day, and the failure presents as an outage rather than as a boundary. **The fallback was never data.** Both documents that mention seasonal averages mention them to refuse them, and the feature's whole reliability argument (**004**) is that the weather rule is a pure function of numbers a provider measured — a look built on a climate average is a look built on a number nobody took, dressed as a forecast. Refusing plainly is what `STAGE-4` 4.2 already asks for.
**Trade-off accepted:** **A user planning three weeks out is told no rather than given something.** That is the correct answer and it is still a refusal on the project's signature screen. **The picker cap makes the API's own rejection unreachable from a correct client**, which is `replace_role`-without-locks' shape (**177**) — its only exercise is its test, and the day the picker breaks is the day the bound stops being tested by anything a user does. And **"14" now lives in two places with nothing comparing them**, the picker's `max` and the request schema, which is the fifth instance of the copy `CONVENTIONS.md`'s "Limits and units" section exists to record.

## 191 — The rate limiter is Stage 5's, and Stage 4 builds none

**Decision:** Task 4.4 ships `POST /trips/pack` **unthrottled**. No counter, no `429`, no `Retry-After`. The mechanism and all three of `04-API-SPEC.md`'s limits — 10/hr on `POST /trips/pack`, 30/hr on `POST /looks/suggest`, 100/hr on `POST /items/upload` — are written into `STAGE-5-qa-deploy.md` § 5.2, beside the integration tests that already existed for them, with a commit checkpoint of its own.
**Alternative:** Build the counter at 4.4 for its one endpoint, which is what `STAGE-4` 4.4's constraints line reads as. Or build it at 4.4 for all three, closing the table in the stage that first names it.
**Reasoning:** **`04-API-SPEC.md` already records this gap and says what to do about it** — *"whoever writes the rate limiter should add it to a stage file first"* — and this is that instruction honoured before the code rather than discovered after it. Building it at 4.4 for one endpoint is the worst of the three states available: the table in `04-API-SPEC.md` would read as implemented while two of its three rows stayed exactly as unprotected as they have been since 0.7 and 2.7, and nothing would record which row was the real one. **5.2 already owns the tests** — *"integration: … rate limits"* has been in that task since Stage 0 — so building the mechanism at 4.4 puts a feature one stage ahead of the only tests that defend it, which is the deferral-with-a-named-owner shape `CONVENTIONS.md`'s definition of done permits, pointed backwards. And `STAGE-4`'s own instruction is to protect the schedule of the signature feature.
**Trade-off accepted:** **The most expensive call in the project ships with nothing capping it.** `POST /trips/pack` is one model call carrying the whole serialised wardrobe for up to fourteen days, and between 4.4 and 5.2 a demo account can issue it as fast as it can be tapped — which is the precise question `04-API-SPEC.md` says the table exists to answer in a defence. This is an exposure with a date and an owner rather than an omission, and it is the second time this project has accepted one on this table: `/auth/*` is still unthrottled and still recorded as such.

## 192 — Task 4.6a: a swap on a trip look is local to its day, and it moves the packing list

**Decision:** A new task **4.6a**, between 4.6 and 4.7 and out of scope for the session that fixed the three decisions above. The ↻ badge comes to the trip look card, reusing Stage 2's plumbing unchanged — `locked_item_ids`, `replace_role`, `exclude_item_ids`, rules 7 and 8. Three properties fix what it means: the new item replaces the old one **on that day only**; the packing list is **recomputed** so the new item is in it and the old one leaves if no day still wears it; and where the old item *is* still worn on other days, the UI **names those days**. Whether the recomputation is the server's or the client's is 4.6a's own question and is not settled here.
**Alternative:** Send the user to `POST /trips/{id}/repack`, which already exists and already replaces every look. Propagate the swap to every day the old garment appears on. Leave the packing list alone and let the next load reconcile it.
**Reasoning:** A wrong garment on Thursday is the same problem `POST /looks/suggest`'s swap was built for at 2.11, and repacking the trip to fix it discards six correct days to change one — as well as, per **191**'s trade-off, spending the most expensive call in the project. **Local to the day, because a trip's looks are not interchangeable:** each was built against its own weather rule, so propagating one day's substitution applies a judgement made at 12°C and rain to a day the model dressed at 19°C and dry. **The packing list has to move or the feature states something false** — `STAGE-4`'s acceptance criteria require that every look item appears in the packing list and every packed item appears in a look, and a swap that leaves the list untouched breaks both within one tap. **Naming the other days is what reuse costs**, and it is the one part of this that is not obvious: the feature's whole claim is that garments repeat, so pulling the jeans off Tuesday does not take them out of the suitcase when Thursday and Saturday still wear them. Without that line the user swaps expecting to pack less, packs exactly the same, and reads the reuse summary as a lie.
**Trade-off accepted:** **A seventh task in a five-day stage whose file says to protect its schedule**, added deliberately rather than discovered. Each tap is a model call against the whole wardrobe, on the endpoint **191** just decided to ship unthrottled. And the packing list becomes a derived value with two possible owners — recomputed on the server it costs a round trip and a write, recomputed in the client it is a second implementation of arithmetic the server already did once, and 4.6a has to choose one knowing that `trips.packing_list` is a stored `JSONB` column either way.

## 193 — `trip_packing_plan`, one look dict plus `day`, and `occasion` struck from both schemas

**Decision:** The trip schema's `name` is **`trip_packing_plan`**, beside `garment_tags` and `outfit_recommendation`. Its look object is `_LOOK_PROPERTIES` — the same dict `STYLIST_SCHEMA` builds from — **plus `day`**, a 1-based ordinal within the trip where day 1 is `start_date`. `packing_list` is `{ "item_ids": [...] }` and nothing else: no `by_category`, and **no `reuse_summary`**, which Python computes from the ids and the looks. The ids the model answers with are `short_id`s on both sides of the schema; `pack_trip` maps them to row UUIDs before anything is stored. **`occasion` leaves the look object on *both* schemas**, closing `AUDITS.md` O-26 by removal. The reuse target `min(days * 4, days + 8)` stays prompt-only and is measured in `docs/eval-results.md` rather than enforced.
**Alternative:** Name it `packing_recommendation`, matching its neighbour's grammar. Keep `occasion` on the trip look, which is the one reader O-26 said could reverse the strike. Ask the model for `reuse_summary`, or for `by_category`, since it is holding the whole plan anyway. Make the reuse target rule 12 and retry when it is missed. Send `short_id`s straight into `trips.packing_list` and resolve them on read.
**Reasoning:** **`occasion` fails 157's test on the trip path too, and that is the whole of O-26's question.** The request carries `occasions: [{"day": 1, "occasion": "work"}, …]`, so the route already knows which occasion belongs to which day; the model's echo is the same unread string one path along, and in strict mode every property is one the model must produce and be paid for on every call. **`day` is the opposite case and is why 163 struck it rather than deleting the idea**: it now has a reader — one look per day of a trip, the day strip in `05-FRONTEND-SPEC.md` §7, and rule 10 — and a message that states the numbering in the same breath as it asks for it, which is what the single-day path could never offer. **`reuse_summary` is arithmetic**, and asking a model for a count means checking the count, so the check may as well be the answer. **The reuse target is not a rule** because a wardrobe that cannot dress seven days from twelve garments should answer with what it can build rather than spend the one retry and then a `502` on arithmetic — 158's reasoning about the weather rule, one field along. **UUIDs are stored rather than `short_id`s** because `scripts/seed_demo.py` regenerates `short_id` per row, so a stored plan keyed on it points at *different garments* after a reseed, where a UUID points at the same row or at none.
**Trade-off accepted:** **A third schema-shaped thing to keep in step**, and `_LOOK_PROPERTIES` is shared by copy rather than by inheritance — a change to what a look holds is still two edits with no compiler between them, which 189 already recorded as the price of two schemas. **`03-AI-CONTRACTS.md` now describes a field that `app/services/stylist.py` still emits** until this task's code commit, which is the state this project usually treats as a defect; it is bounded to one commit and O-26 is left open until the code lands rather than marked closed early. And the acceptance criterion *"the packing list contains strictly fewer items than `days × 4`"* is now explicitly a measurement rather than a guarantee — a reader who takes it for a rule will look for a validator that does not exist, which is why `03` says so in words.

## 194 — Rules 10 and 11, a second run order, and a day prefix keyed to position

**Decision:** `03-AI-CONTRACTS.md`'s validation table gains **rule 10** — `sorted(look.day for look in looks) == list(range(1, expected_days + 1))` — and **rule 11**, no two looks composed of the same set of item ids. Nothing is renumbered. Rule 4's `expected_days` becomes a parameter, rule 5 is widened to **both** directions, and rule 6 becomes per-day, look *i* against day *i*'s rule. The trip path runs **1, 2, 4, 10, 5, 6, 9, 11**; the single-day path is unchanged at 1, 2, 4, 6, 7, 8, 9. Every trip-path violation is prefixed `day N: `, where **N is the look's 1-based position in the returned array**, not its returned `day`.
**Alternative:** Renumber the table so the run order and the numbering agree. Keep rule 10 in numeric position, after rule 9. Prefix with the model's own `day`, which is what a reader expects the words "day 3" to mean. Leave rule 11 to the prompt, which has asked for it since Stage 0. Leave rule 5 in the one direction `03` printed.
**Reasoning:** **Rule 10 is not rule 4 with different words.** Seven looks numbered 1, 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6 satisfy the count exactly and leave day 7 undressed and Monday wearing two outfits — and the failure is invisible in the response, because every look looks complete. **It has to run before rules 5 and 6**: rule 6 pairs look *i* with day *i*'s weather rule, and that pairing means nothing until the ordinals are known to be `1..n`, so hoisting it is a correctness requirement rather than a preference. Renumbering to match was refused on rule 3's own precedent — eight documents, a test and three code comments name these numbers, and a renumbering that buys nothing is how a reference becomes wrong. **The prefix is keyed to position because rules 1 and 2 run before rule 10**, which is exactly when the returned ordinals are what is not yet trustworthy: two looks both claiming day 3 would produce two violations naming day 3, and the undressed day would be named in none of them. After rule 10 passes the two numbers are equal, so nothing is lost by the choice. **Rule 11 is a rule rather than prompt-only** because the model reaches for a repeat precisely when the reuse instruction bites hardest — the cheapest way to pack twelve items across seven days is to wear Tuesday twice — which is 178's finding about the base-top line: a prompt line with nothing behind it is a line the model can talk itself out of. **Rule 5 is widened** because `STAGE-4` asks for both directions and only the second is one a user feels: a garment worn on Thursday and missing from the packing list is a garment left at home.
**Trade-off accepted:** **The table's numbering and its execution order no longer agree**, and a reader has to be told twice — once in the list and once in the paragraph under it. **A violation can now say "day 3" about a look that calls itself day 5**, which is correct and is a sentence someone will have to think about; it is the right thing to send a model that has just mis-numbered its own answer. And the single-day and trip paths now differ in which rules run at all, so `validate_look_response` has two shapes of truth to hold — the asymmetry 164 already accepted between the two contracts' validators, one layer deeper.

## 195 — One trip object, `days[]` as the join, and `DELETE /trips/{id}` is a hard delete

**Decision:** `04-API-SPEC.md` gains a single **trip object** answered by all four trip endpoints, with the trip's looks always a **sibling key** rather than a field. It carries `days[]` — one entry per day with the ordinal, the date, the occasion, the four forecast numbers, the `condition`, the weather `rule` and a **`look_id`** — and `packing_list` with row UUIDs and the `reuse_summary` object. `trips.occasions` and `trips.forecast` are not on the wire. `POST /trips/pack` answers `missing_pieces` and **no `message`**. `GET /trips` is specified and ships with no caller. `DELETE /trips/{id}` is a **hard delete answering `204`**, relying on `0005`'s cascade.
**Alternative:** Pair `days[i]` with `looks[i]` positionally and save a field. Give `GET /trips` a lighter summary shape. Add `for_date` or `day` to `LookResponse`, which every look endpoint already answers with. Answer `message` for symmetry with `POST /looks/suggest`. Archive a trip with an `is_archived` column, as `DELETE /items/{id}` does.
**Reasoning:** **Something has to join a look to its day, and `LookResponse` carries neither a day number nor `for_date`** — checked against `app/schemas/look.py` rather than assumed. The positional pairing is an ordering contract nothing enforces, and it fails silently the first time a day has no look, which is a state a partial repack or 4.6a's swap can produce; widening `LookResponse` was refused because 182 made it one shape for every look and a trip is not a reason to change what `GET /looks` answers. **One shape in the list too** is 034's rule, the one the user object and `GET /looks` already follow; the cost is bytes on an endpoint nothing calls. **No `message`** because `trips` has no column for it and `05-FRONTEND-SPEC.md` §7 has no line that renders it — a field that vanishes on reload is the "no column, no renderer, no task" test that struck `confidence` and `day`. `missing_pieces` passes the same test differently: it describes *this run*, and `POST /looks/suggest` already answers it unpersisted. **A hard delete** because a trip is referenced by nothing but its own looks, `trips` has no `is_archived` to set, and the cascade is already declared — where an item is archived because every look that ever wore it points at the row.
**Trade-off accepted:** **`GET /trips` sends every day's forecast for every trip in the list**, to an endpoint no Stage 4 screen calls — specified because this document is authoritative and the heading has stood since Stage 0, and recorded as uncalled rather than quietly dropped. **`days[].occasion` duplicates `trips.occasions`** in a second shape, which is a copy with nothing comparing the two; the column stays the request as it arrived and the wire stays the merged view. And **the hard delete is what `AUDITS.md` O-32 is about** — it destroys looks that were saved, rated or worn, and that consequence is documented under the endpoint and left to 4.4 rather than decided here.

## 196 — `pack_trip` takes the wardrobe and the preferences block, and `TripRequest` lives in the service

**Decision:** `async def pack_trip(user, wardrobe, request, preferences=None) -> PackingResult`, widening `STAGE-4` 4.3's printed `pack_trip(user, trip_request)`. `TripRequest` is a **frozen dataclass inside `app/services/packing.py`**, not a Pydantic model in `app/schemas/trip.py`. The trip user message **does** carry the learned-preferences block. The stage file is annotated with the widened signature in the task's code commit.
**Alternative:** Keep the two-argument signature and let `pack_trip` query for the wardrobe itself. Put `TripRequest` in `app/schemas/trip.py` now, ahead of 4.4. Omit the preferences block from trips, on the grounds that a trip is not the screen 3.5 was built for.
**Reasoning:** **The printed signature and "a pure service with no DB access" cannot both hold**: the wardrobe is a query, and so is the preference block, which reads rated looks and their items. Something has to pass them in, and the alternative — a `Session` inside `packing.py` — would put the most expensive call in the project behind a function that cannot be unit-tested without a database, which is exactly what `stylist.py` holds no `Session` in order to avoid (162). **`TripRequest` is a dataclass in the service** because `schemas/trip.py` is 4.4's and a wire model written a task early is a contract invented by the task that does not own it; `StylistContext` is the same shape — the route maps its Pydantic body into a frozen dataclass the service defines. **The preferences block is carried** because `03-AI-CONTRACTS.md` puts it in the user message rather than in one endpoint's message, and a user whose stylist has learned they dislike bodycon dresses does not stop disliking them in Berlin.
**Trade-off accepted:** **A four-argument service function** where the stage file printed two, and a signature a reader has to reconcile against `STAGE-4` — annotated there rather than left to be discovered. **`TripRequest` and 4.4's eventual `TripPackRequest` will be two descriptions of one body**, with the route mapping between them and nothing comparing them; that is `StylistContext`'s cost paid a second time, and it is what keeps the service callable from a script with no request in flight (044).

## 197 — The stylist's shared helpers split by layer, not into one module

**Decision:** Four helpers leave `app/api/v1/routes/looks.py` at task 4.3, into **two** modules rather than one. `app/services/stylist_runner.py` takes `attempt` and `judged` — the call-and-retry loop — and imports no `fastapi` and holds no `Session`. `app/api/v1/routes/_stylist_shared.py` takes `styleable_wardrobe` and `stylist_failed`, which know about `Session` and `ApiError`. `_persist` stays in `looks.py`: the trip variant is a different transaction and is 4.4's. `_attempt`'s `attempt: int` parameter is renamed `number`, because a public function cannot take a parameter that shadows its own name; the log key stays `"attempt"`.
**Alternative:** One module, `app/services/stylist_helpers.py`, with a written carve-out from **044** for an `ApiError` factory in `services/`. One module, `app/api/v1/stylist_support.py`, where `ApiError` and `Session` are already at home. Or no new module at all — move the loop into `services/stylist.py` beside `suggest_looks`, which would reopen **164**.
**Reasoning:** **The consumers are not the same set, and that is what decides it.** `pack_trip` needs the retry loop and is a service; it does *not* need the wardrobe query, because **196** passes the wardrobe in, and it does not need `stylist_failed`, because services raise their own exceptions (**044**). `routes/trips.py` at 4.4 needs the other two and reaches the loop through `pack_trip`. So a single module in `services/` would put an `ApiError` factory where 044 says it may not go — `storage.py` and `vision.py` both carry comments explaining why they don't — and a single module in `api/v1/` would make `services/packing.py` import `app.api.v1`, inverting the one-line rule in `01-ARCHITECTURE.md` that routes call services. **Splitting satisfies both**, and the line between the halves is legible in one question: *does it know what a `502` is?* Moving the loop into `stylist.py` was refused because 164 deliberately kept the retry out of that module, and reversing a decision is not something a refactor commit should do silently — it stays available if a later task wants it argued.
