**Trade-off accepted:** **Two files where one was asked for**, and a reader has to learn which half lives where. **The `_` prefix is a convention this project has not used before** for a non-router inside `routes/`, and nothing enforces it — `router.py` imports by name, so a module that never gets imported is inert rather than caught. And **the test seam moved**: 33 integration tests installed their fake by patching `suggest_looks` on the route module, and the call is now one module along. That is one line in one fixture, but it is the part of this refactor that is not "imports and call sites", and it was found by running the suite rather than by reading the diff — a fake installed on a module the call no longer reads would have sent real requests to OpenAI instead of failing loudly.

## 198 — The context picks the schema, which narrows 189's "schema a parameter"

**Decision:** `suggest_looks(wardrobe, context, correction=None)` keeps one signature and **dispatches on the type of `context`**: a `TripContext` selects `TRIP_SCHEMA` and the trip message builder, a `StylistContext` selects `STYLIST_SCHEMA` and the single-day one. `validate_look_response` dispatches the same way, on the same argument, to choose between the two rule orders. No schema parameter is added to either signature.
**Alternative:** 189's literal wording — `suggest_looks(wardrobe, context, schema=STYLIST_SCHEMA)` — with the caller passing `TRIP_SCHEMA` for a trip. Or two public functions, `suggest_looks` and `suggest_trip`, with the shared body extracted.
**Reasoning:** **A schema parameter is a second seam that can disagree with the first.** The message and the schema have to describe the same contract: a trip message asking for one look per day, sent under `outfit_recommendation`, is answered with a single look and no packing list, and the reverse pairing asks for one day and validates against a schema requiring `day` on every look. Nothing types that pairing, and the failure is a `400` from the API or a validation loop that spends the retry — for a mistake the caller cannot see. Dispatching once on the context makes the wrong pairing unspellable. **Two public functions were the near miss**: they read better at the call site and would have duplicated the correction handling, the fake branch, the client construction and the exception contract, which is four things to keep in step in exchange for one. 189 wrote "the schema a parameter rather than a constant" while deciding something else — that there would be **two schemas rather than one** — and that decision is untouched; this narrows only how the second one is selected.
**Trade-off accepted:** **An `isinstance` check decides behaviour**, which is the shape this project has otherwise avoided — `Category` and `Layer` are enums precisely so that branching reads as data. Two dataclasses and a union alias is the smallest thing that types the two shapes apart, and the alternative to the check is a discriminator field on a shared class, which is the same branch with a worse name. And **`AnyContext` now appears in four signatures** — both message builders, `suggest_looks`, `validate_look_response` and `stylist_runner`'s pair — so a third context type would touch all of them.

## 199 — What the trip code made concrete: one dataclass pair, an unpersisted row, and a stored rule

**Decision:** Four things the contract did not settle, decided by writing it. `Look` gains `day: int | None` and `StylistResponse` gains `packing_list: tuple[str, ...] | None` rather than a parallel `TripLook`/`TripResponse` pair. `PackingResult` carries an **unpersisted `Trip` ORM row** with every JSON column already filled, plus `PackedLook`s holding **hydrated `ItemResponse`s**. `trips.forecast` stores the **parsed per-day list including each day's weather rule**, not the provider's body. And every rule that runs after rule 10 reads `look.day` to find its day while reporting the look's **position**, because rule 10 admits a complete-but-shuffled array.
**Alternative:** A parallel dataclass pair, exact on each path. A `PackingResult` of plain dataclasses, leaving 4.4 to build the `Trip`. `short_id`s in `PackedLook`, resolved again by the route. The raw Open-Meteo body in `forecast`, parsed on read. Rule 10 requiring the array to be *in* order, which would make position and day the same number everywhere.
**Reasoning:** **A nullable dataclass field is not what 157 refused.** 157 and 163 struck schema properties, because in strict mode every property is one the model must produce and pay for on every call; a dataclass field costs nothing per call, and a parallel type would make rules 1, 2 and 9 generic over a union to no benefit. **The `Trip` row is built here because its three JSON columns are this task's shapes** — `occasions`, `forecast` and `packing_list` — and a route that had to assemble them would be the second place that knows them. It is unpersisted because `pack_trip` holds no `Session` (196), so 4.4 adds and flushes it. **Hydrated items** because 4.4 needs `item.id` for `look_items` and the whole item for the response, and resolving twice invites two answers. **The forecast column is parsed** because `get_daily_forecast` answers parsed objects and the raw body is never in this service's hands; the **rule is stored beside the numbers** so that a trip packed under one version of the band table cannot re-render under another — the sentence the model obeyed is part of the plan, not a derivation of it. **Rule 10 stays as specified**, `sorted(days) == 1..n`, so a shuffled-but-complete answer is legal; the cost is that a look's position and its day can differ, and the two readings are split deliberately — the day finds the weather, the position names the look in a message written before any ordinal has been checked.
**Trade-off accepted:** **`02-DATA-MODEL.md`'s `forecast` comment was wrong and is corrected in this commit** — it said "cached Open-Meteo response" from Stage 0, which reads as the raw body. **A `Trip` built in a service is an ORM object crossing a layer** that otherwise only sees Pydantic shapes; it is inert until something adds it, and the alternative was a fourth dataclass that 4.4 would copy field by field. And **the position/day split is a subtlety a reader has to hold**: a violation can say "day 3" about a look that calls itself day 5, which is correct and is exactly what a mis-numbering model needs told.

## 200 — `AUDITS.md` O-32: a repack detaches, a delete cascades, and the model answers first

**Decision:** The audit's recommendation taken in full, plus an ordering it did not state. `POST /trips/{id}/repack` **detaches** every look that was saved, rated or worn — `UPDATE looks SET trip_id = NULL WHERE trip_id = :id AND (is_saved OR feedback IS NOT NULL OR worn_at IS NOT NULL)` — and deletes the rest. `DELETE /trips/{id}` **cascades**, destroying all of them. And on the repack path **`pack_trip` runs first**: the detach, the delete and the new looks are one transaction downstream of the model call, so nothing is destroyed until there is something to replace it with.
**Alternative:** Cascade on both, which is what the DDL does and what `04-API-SPEC.md` described. Detach on both. Refuse a repack with a `409` and a twentieth error code while any look is marked. Or destroy the old looks first and write the new ones into the space, which is the shape a reader writes without thinking about it.
**Reasoning:** **The two endpoints are different acts and the audit was right that they may not have the same answer.** A delete is a deliberate act on the whole trip — the user asked for it to be gone, and `trips` has no `is_archived` to set — where a repack is an edit to a trip they are keeping, and losing three days out of `/saved` to a tap meant to refresh the weather is collateral nobody consented to. `feedback` is the sharper case: `03-AI-CONTRACTS.md` requires three rated looks before the preference block is sent at all, so a repack that deleted rated looks could silently switch the stylist's learned preferences back off. **The `409` was refused** because the way out of it is for the user to un-save a look in order to repack — damaging the record to get past a guard protecting it — and because it needs a code and a screen for a case option 2 makes disappear. **The ordering is the half three written options all omitted.** All of them describe *what* a repack destroys and none says *when*, and the difference is a `502` that costs nothing against a `502` that empties a trip: the stylist can fail, and it fails after two attempts and a full wardrobe.
**Trade-off accepted:** **A detached look is a look on `/saved` that belongs to no trip**, whose `weather_note` describes a forecast for a city its row no longer names. That is a card that reads slightly oddly against signal that would otherwise be gone. **`items.wear_count` is not reversed by either endpoint** — a garment worn in Berlin was worn — which leaves a wear count no screen can reconcile against the number of looks it can show; stated under `DELETE /trips/{id}` rather than fixed, because the alternative is a lie about what the user did. And **the two endpoints now behave differently on the same rows**, which is one more thing a reader has to hold; both headings say so in their own words.

## 201 — `start_date` is unbounded, so `trip_too_long` bounds the length as well as the horizon

**Decision:** `POST /trips/pack` puts **no lower bound on `start_date`**. `trip_too_long` (`400`) therefore fires on **two** conditions rather than one: `end_date > today + 14`, which is 190's bound, **and** a trip longer than `MAX_TRIP_DAYS` days. One code and one message for both — *"Trips can span at most 14 days from today."* — which `CONVENTIONS.md` had already pinned and which happens to be true of each.
**Alternative:** Refuse `start_date < today` with a `validation_error`, which would make 190's bound sufficient on its own. Refuse `start_date < today - 1` to leave a day of timezone slack. Give the length its own code. Or leave the length unchecked and let `get_daily_forecast` answer for it.
**Reasoning:** **A lower bound on the server's calendar day is a `422` a correct client can provoke by its timezone**, which is precisely what 184 refused for `POST /looks/{id}/wear` and what `CONVENTIONS.md` says a validation error must never be — a browser east of UTC routinely calls a day by a name this server would not. **But `end_date <= today + 14` alone stops bounding anything once the start is free**: a trip beginning last March and ending next week satisfies it and is three hundred days long, which is three hundred days of forecast asked of a provider that answers sixteen, and three hundred occasions in the request body. So the length has to be checked directly, and it is also the check `STAGE-4`'s acceptance criterion actually names — *a 15-day trip is rejected at the API layer* is about length, not about the horizon. **One code** because a user cannot act differently on the two, and `CONVENTIONS.md` already fixed the message. **Not `get_daily_forecast`'s job**: that service raises against the provider's horizon and serves `GET /weather` too, and 4.2 recorded that the product bound belongs to this endpoint and the date picker.
**Trade-off accepted:** **A trip can be packed for dates in the past**, and what comes back is whatever Open-Meteo answers for them — this endpoint does not pretend otherwise and offers no seasonal-average fallback. **Trips age out of being repackable**: the day a trip's `end_date` falls outside `today + 14`, repacking it answers `trip_too_long`, which is a refusal the user did nothing to earn and the honest reading of a horizon that rolls forward daily. And **one code covers two conditions**, so a client cannot tell from `code` alone which bound it broke; the message covers both and no screen branches on the difference.

## 202 — Repack takes no body and re-geocodes the stored destination

**Decision:** `POST /trips/{id}/repack` accepts **no request body**. It rebuilds the packing request from the trip's own `destination`, `start_date`, `end_date`, `occasions` and `notes`, and passes the destination **string** to `pack_trip`, which geocodes it again. `pack_trip` keeps its `destination: str` signature.
**Alternative:** Accept an optional body so a repack could change the occasions or the notes. Reuse the stored `dest_lat`/`dest_lon` and skip the lookup. Or widen `pack_trip` to accept a pre-resolved `Location` for this one caller.
**Reasoning:** **`04-API-SPEC.md` describes a repack as re-running the same plan against a refreshed forecast**, and an endpoint that accepted new occasions would be an edit endpoint the document does not carry — the shape that struck `message` and `day` elsewhere in this project: no column, no renderer, no task. **`pack_trip` owns the geocode**, and a coordinate bypass would give the service two ways to learn where a trip is going, with one of them reachable from one caller. The stored coordinates are also the *older* answer; re-deriving them is what makes a repack a fresh run rather than a partly-cached one.
**Trade-off accepted:** **A repack can answer `destination_not_found` for a trip that packed cleanly last week** — the geocoder's index moved, or the provider now returns nothing for a string it once matched. **A trip's `dest_lat`/`dest_lon` can move between two packs**, which changes the forecast cache key and, marginally, the weather the second plan was built against. And **the repack spends a geocoder call the stored columns could have saved**, which is one request against a provider this project already calls per keystroke on the trip form.

## 203 — What the trip routes made concrete: a computed join, a one-writer reliance, and a column annotation that was wrong

**Decision:** Three things settled by writing the endpoints. A fourth — the learned-preferences helper moving into `_stylist_shared.py` — is split out into **204** rather than recorded here, because it is a decision about module boundaries where this entry is about the endpoints' own shapes. **`days[].look_id` is computed at read time** from `looks.for_date` against the trip's `start_date`, and is nullable. **`TripResponse.days` and `packing_list` are typed non-null** over two nullable columns, while `dest_lat`/`dest_lon` stay nullable. And **`Trip.forecast`'s annotation is corrected** from `dict[str, Any] | None` to `list[...]`, which is what `pack_trip` has written since 4.3.
**Alternative:** Store `look_id` on the day, or pair `days[i]` with `looks[i]`. Type every response field against its column's nullability. Leave the annotation and `cast` at the one read site.
**Reasoning:** **The join is computed rather than stored** because a stored `look_id` is a second copy of a foreign key with nothing reconciling the two, and it is nullable because a repack's detach can leave a day empty — 195 refused the positional pairing for exactly that, since it fails silently by sliding the next day's look up. **The non-null typing is a one-writer reliance**, `schemas/look.py`'s five-column note one table along: `trips.forecast` and `packing_list` are nullable because `0005` was written before their shapes were settled (189), not because a trip may lack them. `dest_lat`/`dest_lon` are different and stay nullable — `app/models/trip.py` gives a reason about the two fields themselves, that the destination is what the user typed and the coordinates are what the geocoder made of it. **The annotation was simply wrong**: `_forecast_column` returns a list, SQLAlchemy's `__init__` is untyped, so nothing compared them and `mypy` passed over it for a whole task.
**Trade-off accepted:** **`_hydrate` is duplicated between `looks.py` and `trips.py`**, deliberately: 197 drew its line at what two *routes* both need, and a private read helper each already has is not that. And **the non-null typing means a hand-written `trips` row with a `NULL` `packing_list` is a `500` rather than a field the client can branch on** — which is the same trade `_CATEGORY_NAMES` takes, and it lasts exactly as long as `POST /trips/pack` is the only writer. *Amended at task 4.6a-1, which is the event this sentence was written to be checked against: `POST /trips/{id}/swap` is the second writer of `packing_list` and writes it on every path it can answer `200` from, so the typing holds and what it rests on is now two functions agreeing rather than one existing. `forecast` still has one writer. **209**.*

## 204 — `learned_preferences` moves to `_stylist_shared.py`, and is renamed on the way

**Decision:** `looks.py`'s private `_preferences` moves into `app/api/v1/routes/_stylist_shared.py` as the public **`learned_preferences`**, taking `_preference_attributes`, `_CATEGORY_NAMES` and `MIN_RATED_LOOKS`/`PREFERENCE_LIMIT`/`RECENT_WEAR_DAYS` with it. `POST /looks/suggest` and `POST /trips/pack` both call it there. The rename is on `styleable_wardrobe`'s precedent from **197**: every call site assigns the result to a keyword argument named `preferences`, and the bare name would shadow the function at its own call site — `preferences=preferences(...)` is the line that precedent exists to prevent. Split out of **203** so that one decision is recorded in one place.
**Alternative:** Import `looks._preferences` into `trips.py` — a cross-route import of a private name. Copy it into `trips.py`, which is two queries and a nine-member vocabulary in two files with nothing comparing them. Put it in `services/stylist_runner.py` beside the retry loop. Or leave it in `looks.py` and give trips no preference block at all.
**Reasoning:** **196 is what forced the question**: it decided that the trip user message carries the learned block — *a user whose stylist has learned they dislike bodycon dresses does not stop disliking them in Berlin* — so `pack_trip` takes a `preferences` argument and something has to compute it. **197's line decides where**: *does it know what a `Session` is?* This does, since it runs two queries, so a service may not hold it — which rules out `stylist_runner.py`, and rules out `packing.py` computing it for itself, because a service importing `app.api.v1` would invert `01-ARCHITECTURE.md`'s *routes call services*. That leaves the module 197 created for exactly this: helpers two routes both need, which know about `Session` and `ApiError`. It stayed in `looks.py` through 4.3 only because it had a single caller, and this task gave it a second — the same trigger that moved the other four. **The copy was refused** on `_CATEGORY_NAMES` alone: it is a nine-member mirror of `Category` whose drift is a `KeyError` and an unhandled `500`, and duplicating it would mean two mirrors with one test between them.
**Trade-off accepted:** **A third module now holds part of what `POST /looks/suggest` does**, so a reader chasing the preferences block finds it two files from the route that assembles the prompt. **One test's import moved with it** — the vocabulary check pinning `_CATEGORY_NAMES` against `Category` now reads `_stylist_shared` — which is 197's "the test seam moved" cost paid a second time, and found the same way, by running the suite rather than by reading the diff. And **`_stylist_shared.py` is three helpers where it was two**, which is the point at which a module named for a pair needs its docstring to say what the pair became; it does.

**A note this entry carries because no other file has the right section for it.** `AUDITS.md` has no mutation-history heading — it is a dated findings log, and every mutation reference inside it points at `06-TESTING-STRATEGY.md` — so task 4.4's surviving mutation is pinned there, in the running list under *What mutation testing has actually found*, and named here so that a reader of Stage 4's decisions meets it too. **Deleting `ORDER BY for_date` from `GET /trips/{id}`'s look read left all 1088 tests green.** `04-API-SPEC.md` specifies that ordering and the route implements it; nothing measured it, because every trip test in the suite wrote its looks in the order it expected them back, so insertion order and date order were the same list. It is the **eighth survivor** this project has found and the third instance of the shape 1.8 named — *a claim exercised only in the one arrangement where it cannot be wrong* — after 1.5's per-item state and 1.8's second range method. `test_the_looks_come_back_in_date_order_however_they_were_written` plants day 3, then day 1, then day 2, and is the 1089th test.

## 205 — The trip form picks its destination, holds its draft on the page, and ends in a sentence

**Decision:** Five things about `/trips`, settled at task 4.5's orientation and answered before any code was written. The destination must be **chosen from `/me/locations/search`**, and what goes on the wire is the provider's own `name` — the coordinates the picker had are dropped, and the country is display text for the chip. The date pickers are capped at `today + 14` and **floored at nothing**. The **draft lives on `TripsPage`**, not in `TripForm` and not in a store — there is no `TripsStore`. The wait is **four cycling status lines and no skeleton**, and none of them names a duration. And success is a **confirmation panel** naming the destination and `reuse_summary`'s two counts, not a navigation: `/trips/:id` is 4.6's and does not exist yet.
**Alternative:** Let the destination be typed free, which the endpoint accepts — it takes any non-empty string. Send `"Berlin, Germany"` so the geocoder can tell the two apart. Floor `start_date` at today. Build a `TripsStore` for symmetry with the stylist. Draw a skeleton of the packing view during the wait. Navigate to `/trips/:id` now and let 4.6 fill it in, or ship a disabled placeholder screen behind it.
**Reasoning:** **A picked destination is a string the geocoder has already matched**, which is the difference between a spelling the provider resolves and one it never will — `destination_not_found` exists for a geocoder that answers and matches nothing, and this makes it nearly unreachable from a correct client. `"Berlin, Germany"` was rejected for a plainer reason: nothing has measured what Open-Meteo does with a comma, and **196**'s rule about unverified provider behaviour applies to a query string as much as to a response. **The missing floor is 201's, not an oversight**: that decision refuses a lower bound on `start_date` because the server's calendar day is not the user's, and a floor in the picker would be this screen enforcing a rule the API deliberately declines to have — the mirror image of 190's cap, which the API *does* enforce and the picker copies. **The draft is the page's because the form unmounts for the whole request**, which is `look-request-form.ts`'s arrangement and matters more here: a rejected pack must come back to a filled-in destination, four chip rows and the notes, and state held in the control would be destroyed by the very failure it needs to survive. **No store, because nothing else reads it** — `StylistStore` exists because a look is shared with `LooksStore` and with the swap, whereas this page makes one request, renders its own answer, and hands 4.6 nothing, since 4.6 loads a trip by id. A root-scoped store would also buy the reset-on-arrival problem **107** describes, for no consumer. **No skeleton because there is no shape to draw**: the stylist sketches the look card because its form becomes one, and this form becomes a sentence. **And the panel rather than the navigation** because `/trips/:id` does not exist: navigating there today falls through the wildcard to `/wardrobe`, so the user waits twenty seconds for the most expensive call in the project and lands on the grid with nothing to show for it.
**Trade-off accepted:** **`destination_not_found` joins the list of documented codes a correct client cannot provoke**, after `replace_role`-without-locks (**177**) and the picker cap (**190**) — its message is written, keyed and tested, and only its integration test exercises it. **`DECISIONS.md` 153 bites a third time**: the chip says *Berlin, Germany* and the wire says `Berlin`, so the server may geocode the other one and build the trip against the wrong forecast — the user is shown a country the request cannot carry. **The screen is a dead end between 4.5 and 4.6**: the trip is packed and stored, the panel says so, and the only way to it afterwards is `GET /trips/{id}`, which has no caller. **Fourteen now lives in three places** — the request schema, `MAX_TRIP_DAYS` in `trip-form.ts`, and the English of `trip.error.tripTooLong` — which is 190's predicted fifth instance plus the prose copy `stylist.error.wardrobeTooSmall` already accepted for *six*. And **the page-held draft means `TripsPage` is the third screen to hand-roll a status cycle**, after the stylist's; it is the same guarded interval with a different constant, and nothing shares it.

## 206 — The packing view heads with the reuse sentence, joins by id, and ships without repack or delete

**Decision:** Seven things about `/trips/:id`, settled at task 4.6's orientation and answered before any code was written. The **reuse summary is the header line**, counts and sentence together, and `05-FRONTEND-SPEC.md` §7's box is redrawn in the same commit rather than annotated. The sentence is **"You'll wear the {name} on {n} days"**, not `05`'s *"The jeans appear on 3 days"*, and it is **omitted whole** when `most_reused` is null, when `display_name` is null, or when the named item is in none of the response's looks. The **dates print as they arrive** — ISO, unformatted. `GET /trips/{id}` gets **two error branches**, `not_found` and everything else. A day with no look renders as a **gap**, every day keeps its tab, and **day 1 is selected on arrival even when it is the gap**. The **packing list is its own component**, the trip look is **inline** and is **not `LookCard`**. And the screen ships with **no repack and no delete control**, which `AUDITS.md` **O-33** records.
**Alternative:** Keep the reuse sentence at the foot of the packing list, where `05` drew it, or inline with the counts as `STAGE-4` 4.6 words it. Keep *appear* and accept the disagreement on a singular garment, or drop the verb — *"black knit sweater · worn on 3 days"*. Render *Untitled item* rather than dropping the clause. Format the dates with `Intl.DateTimeFormat`, or hard-code English month names. One error message, as `ItemDetailPage` has. Select the first day that *has* a look. Reuse `LookCard` with two inputs switching off *Try again* and the ↻ badge. Build the repack and the delete here, since the wire supports both.
**Reasoning:** **Three documents put the reuse sentence in three places** — `05`'s box at the foot, `STAGE-4` 4.6 inline with the counts, and neither in the header — and the counts half settles which end it joins: it is 4.5's confirmation panel word for word, that panel is deleted by this task, and a sentence split across two ends of one screen is worse than either placement. **"The jeans appear" cannot be written from one template**, because `display_name` is model-written text whose grammatical number is unknowable in the browser; the verb after *you'll* is invariant, which is the whole of the rewording. **The clause is dropped rather than degraded** because *"You'll wear the Untitled item on 3 days"* names nothing and takes the header line down with it — and the third case, an item in no look, is real: a repack detaches a look and its item rows leave with it (**200**). **The dates are unformatted because nothing in this project formats a date** — `item.wear.last` prints `last_worn_at` raw — and a formatter would either hard-code English months against `CONVENTIONS.md`'s i18n rule or take them from the browser's locale while every other string came from `en.json`. **Two error branches, not one**, because a 404 is the common case on this screen rather than the exotic one — a typed URL, a trip deleted in another tab — and the branch reads the code with the status beside it, since a 404 that never reached the application has no body to hold one. **Day 1 on arrival** because an opening selection that depended on the data leaves the reader working out why day 3 is lit. **Not `LookCard`**, which `saved-looks.page.ts` refused at 3.2 for exactly these reasons: it ends in *Try again*, which has no request behind a trip look, and it carries the ↻ badge, which is **4.6a's**. **And no repack or delete** because `STAGE-4` 4.6 asks for four things and neither is among them: the repack inherits the whole of `POST /trips/pack`'s surface — the wait, the four status lines, all seven codes — on a screen that now has content to lose, which is a task rather than a control.
**Trade-off accepted:** **`05-FRONTEND-SPEC.md`'s drawn sentence is not the one that ships**, and the document is corrected rather than the code bent to it — the fourth time a `05` box has been redrawn by the task that built it. **The reuse clause can vanish for three different reasons and says so for none of them**: the user sees the counts alone and cannot tell which case they are in. **The dates read as `2026-03-14 – 2026-03-17`**, which is the least attractive line on the screen and the honest one. **`trip.packed.title` is deleted** — 4.5's *"Berlin is packed."* — because the navigation replaced the panel that rendered it; the counts keys survive with one caller each. **Stage 4 ships with no way to re-pack or delete a trip** (**O-33**), so a trip whose forecast has moved is re-created rather than refreshed, and the old one stays for ever. And **the back link is the tenth bespoke navigation control** — this entry said *fifth*, which is what `AUDITS.md` **O-29** was counting at the time and is wrong; **208** deletes the link and O-29's closing census is the corrected number — shipped anyway, because a screen reached by a navigation the user cannot undo is a worse dead end than one they typed.

## 207 — The repack is one press and the delete is two, and the wait they share left `trips.page.ts`

**Decision:** Six things about `/trips/:id`'s two controls, settled at task 4.6b's orientation and answered before any code was written. Both sit in a **footer row under the packing list**, `item-detail.page.ts`'s shape for its own two actions. The **repack is unguarded** — one press into the same twenty-second wait the form shows — and the **delete arms and fires in two presses** (**126**), with the cascade written into its armed label: *"Tap again to delete. Saved looks from this trip go too."* A **failed repack leaves the trip on screen** with its message above the actions row, and takes six of the pack's seven messages plus a general fallback of its own, `trip.error.repackGeneral`. A **successful repack replaces the whole `TripDetail`**, keeping the selected day and the packing list's ticks. The delete navigates to **`/wardrobe`**. And the four status lines, the three-second interval, its teardown and the seven-code error table **leave `trips.page.ts` for `pack-wait.ts`**, which both screens import.
**Alternative:** Put the actions in the header, or behind a kebab. Guard the repack too, for symmetry, or guard neither and rely on the twenty seconds being visible. Replace the screen with the repack's failure, as the load error does. Reset the day strip to day 1 after a repack, and clear the packing list's ticks with it. Send the user to `/trips` after a delete. Keep `packErrorKey` and the status cycle in `trips.page.ts` and import them from the page, or copy the twenty lines into the second screen and let the two drift.
**Reasoning:** **`/saved` filters on `is_saved` alone** — `looks.api.ts` sends that parameter and no other — so a look **200** detaches from a repacked trip is still on the screen that lists it afterwards. That single fact settles the asymmetry: the repack has nothing to warn about, and putting a confirmation in front of it would be training for clicking through the one in front of the delete, which really does destroy those rows. **The delete's armed label is the only place the cascade is ever said to a user**, which is why it carries a sentence where `item-detail.page.ts` carries three words. **The failure keeps the trip because `pack_trip` runs first** — 200's ordering, which exists so that a `502` costs nothing — and a screen blanked by the failure would tell the user the opposite of what the server had just guaranteed for them. **The general fallback is separate because it names the act rather than a condition**: the six code-specific messages describe a wardrobe that is too small or a forecast that is missing, which read the same either side of a packed trip, whereas *"We couldn't pack this trip"* under a visibly packed trip is simply the wrong sentence — so `packErrorKey` takes its fallback as a **required** parameter rather than defaulting to the pack's, and a third caller has to decide rather than inherit. **The extraction is 197's test applied to the frontend**: the consumers are the same set — both screens need all four things — so the split 197 made on the backend does not apply, and one module is right where two were there. The factory takes the *"is it running"* signal and owns the interval itself, so a second caller cannot forget the stop half; `onCleanup` replaces 4.5's guard against starting an interval over a live one, which makes that case unreachable rather than handled.
**Trade-off accepted:** **The armed delete's label is a two-clause sentence on a button**, which is longer than any other label in the product and wraps on a phone — accepted because a screen reader announces it as the control's name, which is where a warning about a destructive act should be, and because it is only on screen while armed. **The two controls are guarded twice**, once by `[disabled]` and once by a clause in the handler, and the handler's clause cannot be reached from the rendered screen — the mutation pass leaves both standing and the tests assert `disabled` instead; it stays because a signal write only *schedules* change detection, so two presses in one frame both see an enabled button. **The packing list's ticks survive a repack**, so an item ticked off before the trip was re-planned stays ticked if it is still packed, and ids that left the plan linger unrendered in a `Set` nothing clears until the screen does. **A look detached for `feedback` or `worn_at` alone is still on no screen** — it was on none before either, but the repack now has a user-visible cause and no user-visible consequence. And **`missing_pieces` is dropped by hand** on the way from `PackResponse` into a `TripDetail` signal, because structural typing would have let the wider object in without a word; that is the one decision here the mutation pass cannot defend, since nothing renders the field either way.


## 208 — One navigation bar in the shell, gated on the user, active by prefix — and O-29 closed on a corrected count

**Decision:** Eleven things about the navigation, settled at task 4.9's orientation and answered before any code was written. `shared/ui/nav-bar.ts` is **rendered by `app.html`**, a sibling of the router outlet rather than part of any screen, and it is **one sticky top bar at every width**. It carries **five items** — Wardrobe, Stylist, Trips, Saved, Profile — plus **Sign out** last. It renders **only when `auth.isAuthenticated()`**, which is the user signal and not the token. The active item is `routerLinkActive` at its **default non-exact matching** with `ariaCurrentWhenActive="page"`. **Six back links and two account-row anchors are deleted**, with eight i18n keys; `item.back` stays as a hierarchical *up*, and the weather strip's two links and `/saved`'s empty-state CTA stay as contextual actions. The landing route is **unchanged**. And `AUDITS.md` **O-29 is closed on a census that contradicts its own prose** — ten controls across nine commits, not five.
**Alternative:** Targeted entry points instead — a **Plan a trip** button on the wardrobe, which is all the immediate need required. A bottom tab bar on mobile with a top bar on desktop. A home dashboard as the landing route. Keep the account row and add the bar beside it. Gate the bar on the route rather than on the user, or render it inside each page. `{ exact: true }` matching, which is what "the current screen" reads like in English. Keep the identity line. Close O-29 on the five it had been counting.
**Reasoning:** **A sixth entry point could not close O-29, because the count going up *is* what that item calls the defect** — it says so in the paragraph where it refuses to close at five, and every one of the five previous instances ended with the same resolution recorded and nothing fixed. The narrow option would have made this the eleventh control. **The bar is the shell's because it outlives every screen**: put in each page it is six copies to keep in step, and put in the wardrobe's section it becomes something the wardrobe owns, which is precisely how the account row happened. **The gate is the user rather than the route** so that no screen can acquire a bar by being added to `app.routes.ts`, and so that a restore in flight and a rejected token both render nothing without either being named. **Non-exact matching is the whole of the active rule and the only line here that had to be measured**: `/wardrobe` writes `?category=tops` on every filter change, and exact matching compares query parameters exactly, so the Wardrobe item would go dark the first time anybody filtered — the same rule keeps a child route lighting its parent, which is what stops the bar going blank on the two detail screens. **One bar rather than bottom tabs** because the wardrobe's **Add items** FAB is `fixed bottom-6 end-6` and a bottom bar would sit under it — a collision, not a preference. **The line between navigation and a contextual action** is what decides the survivors: **Style me** is an action on today's forecast that happens to navigate, and §2.12 requires it in all three states, where **Saved** in the account row was navigation wearing an anchor. **And the census had to be taken because the prose count was wrong three ways** — a double-booked ordinal, a control missed in the very paragraph that described the task shipping it, and back links that predated the item and were never in scope without that ever being said. Closing on five would have put the wrong number permanently in the one file whose job is to be trusted when the code and memory disagree.
**Trade-off accepted:** **`userLabel` now has no caller.** It is a `shared/models` helper with five tests of its own, and the *Signed in as …* line was its only consumer; it is left in place rather than deleted, which is dead code by `CONVENTIONS.md`'s own rule, and it is flagged here rather than removed because deleting a tested shared helper is wider than this task's brief. **The bar scrolls horizontally at 390px** — six controls do not fit — so an item can be off-screen on a phone and reachable only by scrolling the bar, which is a worse affordance than tabs would have been. **`app.html` is no longer a bare outlet**, so every screen's spec now renders inside a shell that asks `AuthService` a question, and `app.spec.ts` exists to defend a two-line template. **The identity line is gone from every screen**: who is signed in is now only visible by opening `/profile`, on an application whose demo account is shared and mutable (**136**). **Nothing E2E proves any of this yet** — `STAGE-5` 5.3 owns the eleven journeys, and the unit layer can only assert that the bar renders, points somewhere, and lights the right item. And **the mutation pass was run twice**: the first harness reported all fourteen mutations surviving, which was the harness failing to parse vitest's coloured output rather than fourteen untested behaviours, and the numbers below are the second run's. Two mutations that survived legitimately were **invalid** — they re-added a `routerLink` to a component whose `RouterLink` import the task had removed, so they rendered an inert attribute with no `href`; rewritten to restore the import, both were caught.

## 209 — `POST /looks/suggest` cannot swap a trip look, so the swap is `/trips/{id}/swap`, it re-reads no sky, and `packing_list` gets a second writer

**Decision:** Seven things about task 4.6a-1, settled at its orientation and answered before any code was written. The swap is a **new endpoint**, `POST /trips/{trip_id}/swap`, taking `day`, `item_id`, `replace_role` and `exclude_item_ids` and answering `TripDetailResponse` — the shape `GET /trips/{id}` already returns, reused rather than copied. It **reads `trips.forecast[day]`** for the four numbers, the condition and the weather rule, so it calls **no geocoder and no forecast provider** and rebuilds no rule. It runs the **single-day** rule order against a `StylistContext`. `trips.packing_list` is **recomputed on the server** in the same transaction — survivors in place, unworn ids dropped, newcomers appended. The replaced look is **detached when marked and deleted otherwise**, on `_MARKED`, with the **model call first**. `item_not_in_look` is a new `422`, the nineteenth code, and it covers a day with no look. And the wardrobe threshold here is **six**, not this module's eight.
**Alternative:** Widen `POST /looks/suggest` with `trip_id` and `day`, which is what four previous sessions assumed and `PROGRESS.md` recorded. Put `day` in the path as `/trips/{id}/days/{day}/swap`. A `TripSwapResponse` of its own. Recompute the packing list in the browser, which `192` left open as the other half of its question. Refuse a swap on a saved look with a `409`. Re-fetch the day's forecast so the swap is built on today's sky, as the repack is. Derive `replace_role` on the server from `item_id`.
**Reasoning:** **The reuse everyone assumed was never checked against the handler, and it fails four ways.** `_context` in `routes/looks.py` forecasts `user.home_lat`/`home_lon` — that body carries no coordinates by **151** and **173** — so a swap on a Berlin day would be dressed for the weather at home, and would fail *well*: rule 6 would enforce home's rule and the `weather_note` would describe the wrong city. It raises `home_location_missing` for an account that packed the trip without those columns ever being read. `_persist` writes a look with **no `trip_id`**, and `_by_day` joins on `WHERE trip_id = :id`, so the swap would be invisible on reload and would leave an orphan row per tap. And it never touches `packing_list`, which is 4.6a's second property. Widening it means branching `_context`, `_persist` and the response on whether a trip was named — a second endpoint living inside the first, not the shape addition it was described as. **What *is* reusable is everything below HTTP**, which is what **192** was actually right about: `StylistContext`, `STYLIST_SCHEMA`, rules 7 and 8, and `judged` are taken unchanged. **The stored rule is read rather than rebuilt** because **199** put it in the column for exactly this: the sentence the model obeyed is part of the plan, so a swap that called `build_rule` again would judge Thursday against today's band table while the other six days stood on the old one — invisible in the response, which is why a test overwrites the column with a sentence no three numbers produce. That also removes the geocode and the forecast, and with them four of the pack's seven codes. **The server recomputes the list** because `reuse_summary`'s tie-break is written down so that one plan cannot summarise two ways, and a TypeScript second implementation is the one thing that could break that; because the column is stored, so a client-side recompute leaves the database wrong until the next repack; and because the transaction is already open. **The order is part of the answer**, not decoration: the tie-break reads packing-list order, so survivors keep their positions and newcomers go last. **O-32's option 2 again**, and the `409` fails here for the reason it failed there — the way out is to un-save a look in order to change a shoe. **Six rather than eight** because the endpoint runs the single-day path, where rule 11 never runs; eight would answer `400` to a look the model can build, which is **172**'s mistake inverted. **`day` is in the body** because `days[]` is a computed view (**203**) and a nested collection with one endpoint under it is a resource this API does not have. **The role stays the client's** because `ROLE_BY_CATEGORY` exists only in `enums.ts` — this API validates the six values and derives none — and a map on the server would be the second copy of a table **175** kept in one place.
**Trade-off accepted:** **`trips.packing_list` now has two writers**, which is precisely the reliance `schemas/trip.py` and **203** both name, and the paragraph in each is amended in this commit rather than left to be discovered — the drift this project treats as a defect would otherwise have been three files wide. **A class called `TripDetailResponse` is answered by a `POST` that edits**; a second identical model was refused on **182**'s rule and the name is the price. **The swap is unthrottled**, like everything else on this table (**191**), and it is now the cheapest way to spend a model call in the product — one tap, the whole wardrobe, no confirmation. **A trip that can no longer be repacked can still be swapped**, because the bound is not rechecked; that is defensible — the stored forecast was taken inside the horizon — and it is one more place where two endpoints on one screen behave differently on the same row. **The fake stack is duplicated into `test_trips_swap.py`** rather than imported or hoisted into a `conftest.py`, because no test module in this suite imports another and the alternative touches a file this task has no other reason to open. And **one mutation is defended by a hand-edited row rather than by anything a user can do**: reading `occasion` off the replaced look instead of off `trips.occasions` is an equivalent mutant under every state this API can write, so the test that catches it edits the look row directly.

## 210 — The trip look becomes a component, the exclusions are per day, and two of the pack's messages could not be reused

**Decision:** Nine things about task 4.6a's frontend half, settled at its orientation and answered before any code was written. The day's look moves out of `trip-detail.page.ts` into **`features/trips/trip-look.ts`** — `app-trip-look`, a neighbour of `packing-list.ts` and **not** `shared/ui/`, and for the third time **not** the stylist's `LookCard`. The ↻ badge is the look card's corner overlay copied — `absolute end-0 top-0`, `min-h-11 min-w-11` — on the **four**-column grid 4.6 shipped, and every badge disables while one swap is in flight. The wait is a **spinner over the tapped tile alone**: no `packStatus`, no status cycle. There is **no preview, no confirmation and no undo**; a second tap accumulates. The exclusions are a **`Map<day, ids>` held on the page**, fresh on every mount and cleared by a repack. The **still-worn line is composed by the component from facts the page computes** — `trip.swap.stillWorn` over `trip.day.legend`, joined with `trip.swap.daysSeparator`, **no "and"** — and it renders under the tile grid, above the model's prose. A failed swap renders **inside the look article**, in a third error signal that is neither the load's nor `actionError`. **Nine new i18n keys**, not the seven 209's list implied: `trip.swap.action`, `.doing`, `.stillWorn`, `.daysSeparator`, `trip.error.itemNotInLook`, `.lockedUnavailable`, `.swapWardrobeTooSmall`, `.swapStylistFailed` and `.swapGeneral`. And `validation_error` is **deliberately unmapped**, falling to the general line.
**Alternative:** Render the look inline as 4.6 left it and hang the badge off the page. Reuse `LookCard` with its three controls hidden by inputs. Put the badge and its spinner in `shared/ui/`, which `05`'s file tree has named since Stage 0. Drop the trip look to three columns so the badge does not cover a quarter of a tile. Spin the badge itself instead of the tile. Animate the replacement in. Keep one exclusion list for the trip. Persist the exclusions across a remount. Compose the still-worn sentence on the page beside the header's reuse clause, or take the list separator from `Intl.ListFormat`. Put the swap's failure in `actionError`, which already exists. Reuse `trip.error.wardrobeTooSmall` and `trip.error.stylistFailed`, which are what a seven-key list required. Give `validation_error` a message of its own.
**Reasoning:** **The component exists because the block acquired state, not because the file was long**: it now owns which tile carries a badge, which tile is waiting and the sentence reporting what a swap cost, and `packing-list.ts` was extracted at 4.6 on exactly that test. It is not `shared/ui/` because it has **one caller**, which is the rule **O-15** has been answered with twice. It is not `LookCard` for 4.6's three reasons unchanged — *Try again* has no request behind it here, the heart and the thumbs belong to `/saved`, and that card groups by layer where this arranges by `look_items.position`. **The four-column grid stands** because the badge overlays the corner of a photograph rather than information, and shrinking the grid to make room for a control is the control winning an argument against the content. **The spinner covers the tile rather than the badge** because the badge is also the thing that must become unavailable, and a 44px target that keeps its hit area while meaning *wait* is a press with nowhere to go; it covers **one** tile because unlike the pack, this screen has a look worth keeping legible for four to eight seconds. **No confirmation** because the swap *is* the answer and a second tap is *not that one either*; a preview would make the conversation a form. **Per-day exclusions** because a shoe that is wrong for Tuesday's rain is the right answer for Thursday, and one shared list would narrow six days on the strength of one rejection; **fresh on every mount** because the id comes from a route snapshot, so the back button is already a new component, and making the list outlive that would mean housing it somewhere that outlives the page for a list whose whole meaning is *in this sitting*. **A repack clears them** because it rebuilds every day against a forecast the rejections were never judged against. **The component composes the sentence** because the thing that renders a string should be the thing that asks for it, and the page hands over facts — which is the shape the server already uses one boundary up, where `ReuseSummary` answers two counts and no English. **`Intl.ListFormat` is refused on 206's reasoning**: it would write the *and* and take the browser's locale with it, on a screen whose every other word came from `en.json`. **The failure goes inside the article** because `actionError` sits under the packing list and means *the whole trip's action failed*, where a failed swap costs one day nothing — the same split that already separated the load's error from the action's, taken a third time. **Two of the pack's messages could not be reused, and finding that is what the orientation was for**: `wardrobe_too_small` maps to a sentence saying *eight* while this endpoint's threshold is **six** (209), and the pack's `stylist_failed` says *"We couldn't pack this trip just now"* in answer to a tap on one shoe — which is **207**'s finding exactly, one screen along, and the reason `trip.error.repackGeneral` exists at all. A shipped message that says the wrong thing on one path is worse than a missing one. **`validation_error` gets none** because it is reachable only by naming a day the trip does not have, which no correct client can build, and a bare 422 from the request schema carries FastAPI's `detail` rather than a `code` — `pack-wait.ts` omits `home_location_missing` on the same rule.
**Trade-off accepted:** **There is no undo**, and that is the largest cost here: a garment swapped away can only be recovered by taking what the model offers next, or by re-packing the trip and losing every other swap with it. **The still-worn line renders `Untitled item` where 206 dropped the header's reuse clause over exactly that** — deliberately, because that sentence has to name a garment the reader has not touched and this one answers a press on a specific tile, but it is the same string reaching the screen that another decision refuses. **`isSwappable` is duplicated** from `look-card.ts` rather than shared, three tokens against a trips component importing from a stylist one. **The swap error, the still-worn line and the exclusions have three different lifetimes on one screen** — cleared on a day change, cleared on a day change, and kept per day — which is defensible sentence by sentence and is not a rule a reader can infer. **A `POST` is issued with no throttle from a badge a user can hold down**, inheriting 209's own trade-off on the client side; the only guard is that one request is in flight at a time. **The badge covers roughly a third of a tile's width at 390px**, which is the grid decision paid for in pixels. And **`trips.api.ts`'s header comment was wrong before this task touched it**: it said the repack and the delete *"arrive at task 4.6b"* in the commit that made them arrive, and the count line had to change here anyway.

## 211 — The stylist's Today caption formats a date, which 206 refused

**Decision:** One thing, settled at DR.4's delivery and recorded here because it contradicts a standing entry. `stylist.page.ts` builds its header caption with `Intl.DateTimeFormat`, at a **hardcoded `'en-GB'` locale**, and interpolates the result into `stylist.today` — so *"Today · Monday 1 September"* reaches the screen with its weekday and month as **formatter output rather than string-table entries**. This is a conscious exception to **206**, which refused a date formatter for the whole project, and it is bounded: the trips screen keeps its ISO dates, the day strip is dated by `day.date` unchanged, and nothing else formats a date. It is revisited when `en.json` gains a second locale.

**Alternative:** Roll the caption back to the raw ISO date, which is what 206's rule produces — *"Today · 2026-09-01"*. Put twelve month names and seven weekday names in `en.json` and compose the caption by hand. Take the browser's locale instead of pinning one. Drop the date from the caption and leave it reading *"Today"*.

**Reasoning:** **206's objection is two objections, and only one of them survives a pinned locale.** It refused a formatter that would "either hard-code English months against `CONVENTIONS.md`'s i18n rule or take them from the browser's locale while every other string came from `en.json`" — pinning `'en-GB'` answers the second half outright, since the caption cannot drift into a language the rest of the screen is not in. The first half stands, and is what this entry is for. **The information is real where 206's was not**: the trips screen prints a date the user chose and can already read off the picker, so formatting it buys nothing, whereas *"Monday 1 September"* tells the reader which day of the week they are dressing for — the one fact a stylist header can add that the form below it does not carry. **Nineteen names in `en.json` would be the honest fix and are not worth their weight yet**, on a table with one locale in it and no second one planned; composing a date by hand from those names is the work a formatter exists to avoid, and doing it early would be building the i18n layer a hypothetical second locale needs before it exists.

**Trade-off accepted:** **Two screens now disagree about dates in the same product** — the stylist says *Monday 1 September* and the trip detail says *2026-09-01* — which is visible to any user who opens both, and defensible only by the argument above. **Nineteen English words reach the screen from outside the string table**, which is the exact rule `CONVENTIONS.md` states and 206 enforced, and a translator handed `en.json` cannot find them. **The exception is guarded by nothing but this entry**: no test asserts that the formatter has one caller, so a second one costs nobody anything at the time it is written. And **the revisit has no trigger attached** beyond "when i18n grows past `en`", which is the shape of deferral this file has recorded going wrong before.

**Superseded at 220, and the exception it argued for moves rather than ending.** The dateline this formatter fed is deleted — the Ritual header is a title and a forecast, and *"Today · 1 September"* stood directly over a weather line that says the same word beside it. `TODAY_FORMAT`, `todayLabel` and `stylist.today` all go. **But a formatter comes back in the same commit, on the look card's parameter kicker**, and the exception this entry argued for is what licenses it: nineteen English month and weekday names still reach the screen from outside the string table, and a translator handed `en.json` still cannot find them. **What is stronger the second time is the information test this entry set for itself.** 211 refused 206's objection on the grounds that a formatted date is only worth its exception when it tells the reader something the controls do not — and it conceded the stylist dateline was borderline, because the picker below it already carried the day. The kicker is not borderline: it prints the date the look on screen was *built for*, which stops being the date in the picker the moment the user changes it without pressing the button. That is a fact available nowhere else on the screen, and it is the whole reason the line exists.

## 212 — Three named spacing intervals, because the application had one

**Decision:** `--spacing-hero: 4rem`, `--spacing-region: 2.5rem` and `--spacing-group: 0.75rem`, declared as `@theme static` entries beside the DR.1 colours and spelled as the utilities `pt-hero`, `gap-region` and `gap-group`. Every screen container drops `gap-6 p-6` for `gap-region px-6 pt-hero pb-region`. DR.7a applies it to the wardrobe, the stylist and the saved looks; DR.7b takes the remaining six screens. This is the first task of the **Direction C** pass and the substrate for the rest of it.

**Alternative:** Plain numeric utilities — `gap-10`, `pt-16`, `gap-3` — plus a sentence in `CONVENTIONS.md` describing the rhythm. Or a single extra interval rather than three, on the grounds that the fault was one value and two would fix it.

**Reasoning:** **The fault was measurable and it was singular.** All nine `<main>` elements in the application were `flex flex-col gap-6`, so a title sat exactly as far from its own count as it did from an unrelated region below it; nothing was grouped by proximity because nothing was closer than anything else. That is what "consistent but not beautiful" was, mechanically — order comes from a rule applied everywhere, and hierarchy comes from a rule with rungs in it. **Three rather than two**, because the failure has two halves and they need opposite corrections: regions were too close together *and* labels were too far from what they label. `hero` and `region` open the page up; `group` is the rung that pulls a label back onto its content, and it had two callers on the first three screens — the wardrobe's tagging line over its grid, and the stylist's anchor pin over the form it modifies. **Named rather than numeric**, because the whole finding is that one unnamed interval spread everywhere unchallenged: `gap-6` carried no claim about what it separated, so nothing stopped it separating everything. `gap-region` cannot be written between two chips without the author noticing they have said something false. **The numeric scale is deliberately left in place** for composition inside a component — the grid's `gap-3`, a button row's `gap-2` — because naming those would be inventing a vocabulary for decisions that carry no hierarchy. **`static` for 064's reason**, unchanged: these are theme tokens consumed across a stylesheet boundary Tailwind cannot see, and a spacing token pruned silently is a layout that quietly collapses to zero.

**Trade-off accepted:** **Two spellings now exist for 0.75rem** — `gap-group` and `gap-3` — and nothing prevents the second being used where the first is meant; the rule lives in `05-FRONTEND-SPEC.md` and in review, not in the compiler. **`pt-hero` and `pb-region` are physical block properties**, not logical ones, which is consistent with the existing `pt-6`/`py-16`/`pb-1` call sites and harmless while the writing mode is horizontal — Tailwind 4 offers no `pbs-*`/`pbe-*`, and the project's logical-property rule is about the inline axis, where `ms-`/`me-`/`border-bs`/`border-be` are still mandatory. **`4rem` is the value most likely to want tuning**: 64px above a title is generous on a 390px phone under a sticky navigation bar, and it is judged here on three screens rather than nine. DR.7b is the task that finds out, and changing it afterwards is one line. **No test asserts any of this.** No spec in the project asserts a `gap-*` or `max-w-*` class, so the whole of DR.7 is invisible to the suite by construction — 675 tests pass before and after, unchanged, and the verification is the built stylesheet and the eye.

**Amended at DR.7b — two screens take no hero band, and the auth pair is evidence the value is right.** The rung is defined as *the band above a page title*, so a screen with no page title at the top has nothing to give one to. **`login` and `register`** are `min-h-dvh … justify-center` with no navigation bar above them: a `pt-hero` would fight `justify-center` outright, so they take `py-hero` and no `pt-hero`. That substitution is worth recording rather than just making, because **`py-16` is exactly `4rem`** — the two screens were already hand-tuned to the value the token later arrived at, independently of it, which is the closest thing to corroboration a spacing constant can get. **`item-detail`** takes `py-region` for the same rule read the other way: it opens with a back link and a photograph and its `h1` sits *under* the image, so a hero band there would be 64px of air above *Back to wardrobe*. **Six screens take the band, three do not, and all three exceptions are the same sentence** — no page title at the top of the page. Stated sharply, because the test is easy to misread: **the element exists, the position does not. Position decides, not existence.** `item-detail` has an `h1` and still takes no band, because its `h1` lives under the photograph rather than at the top of the content column.

**Also at DR.7b — `py-*` is logical where `pt-*`/`pb-*` are not.** Tailwind 4 compiles `py-hero` to `padding-block`, which is the logical property, while `pt-hero` and `pb-region` compile to `padding-top`/`padding-bottom`. Nothing was designed around this and nothing depends on it — the writing mode is horizontal either way — but it means the two screens that use `py-*` are logical for free, and it is the reason not to "tidy" a `py-` into a `pt-`/`pb-` pair later.

**Group rung callers after DR.7b: six.** The wardrobe's tagging line over its grid and the stylist's anchor pin over its form (DR.7a); the trip detail's day strip over the look it selects; and three on item detail — the photograph with its name and *Style around this*, the two tag notices with the editor they are about, and the delete confirmation with the button that armed it. Every one of them is a label-or-control standing over the thing it acts on, which is the only relationship the rung is for.

**Superseded at 219 as a task in the DR pass; the pass is what is superseded, not this.** The Direction C method — a screen improved one interval, one face, one state at a time — is abandoned for whole-screen implementation from a picked mockup. The three tokens declared here survive untouched: `--spacing-hero`, `--spacing-region` and `--spacing-group` are still in `tailwind.css` and the Atelier wardrobe still spells `pt-hero`, `gap-region`, `pb-region` and `gap-group`. What 219 replaces is the way the decisions were arrived at.

## 213 — `AuthoredLine`, and the sentence that carries a content span

**Decision:** An authored sentence may interpolate content — a city, a garment, the user's name — and the interpolated value renders in the content face while the sentence around it keeps the authored one. The mechanism is **one whole key** plus a shared component. `I18nService` gains **`segments(key, params?, content?)`**, returning an ordered `readonly { text: string; content: boolean }[]`; **`shared/ui/authored-line.ts`** exports **`AuthoredLine`**, selector `app-authored-line`, which renders those segments and wraps the content ones in `font-sans`. The call site passes **two dictionaries**: `[params]` for values that stay in the authored face, `[content]` for values that switch to the system stack. It is **specified here and built at DR.10**, its first caller, on the rule that has answered `AUDITS.md` O-15 twice.

```
<app-authored-line
  key="wardrobe.weather.sentence"
  [params]="{ condition: i18n.t('vocabulary.condition.clear') }"
  [content]="{ city: user.home_city }"
/>
```

**Alternative:** Three others, and the shape of the problem is that all four are defensible until the Hebrew requirement is applied. (1) **Two keys, two elements** — the weather strip's existing idiom, which keeps the display face away from content by splitting the sentence into independent fragments. (2) **The whole sentence in the content face**, which needs no mechanism at all. (3) **Fragment the key and assemble in the template**, which is what most projects do. (4) The above. There was also a shape question inside (4): one dictionary plus a `[contentKeys]` list, rather than two dictionaries.

**Reasoning:** **(3) is disqualified by the reason the face rule exists.** A key broken into fragments cannot be reordered by a translator — `weather-strip.ts` and `wardrobe-insights.ts` both already refuse it in comments, in the same words: *"a sentence assembled from pieces cannot be reordered for a language that words it differently."* Hebrew is the whole point of 071, so a mechanism that makes sentences un-reorderable to satisfy 071 defeats itself. **(4) keeps the key whole because the split follows the placeholder, not English word order.** `segments()` walks the template's own `{{name}}` matches, so a translation reading *"{{name}}, בוקר טוב"* wraps the name in first position without anything else changing. That property is the entire reason it beat (3), and it is the thing to check first if the mechanism is ever rewritten. **(1) and (2) survive and stay available** — (1) is right where the two halves are genuinely separate lines, which is why the weather strip's reading and place are not being merged by fiat, and (2) is right whenever a sentence is content throughout. `AuthoredLine` is for the case neither covers: one sentence, mixed provenance.

**Two dictionaries rather than one plus a key list, because it makes the wrong call site impossible instead of silently wrong.** With `[contentKeys]`, forgetting to list a key is a value that renders in the authored face — no error, no warning, and the failure is invisible until somebody's name is in Hebrew. With two dictionaries a value cannot reach `[params]` without the author typing it there, so the mistake requires a decision rather than an omission. That is the same standard the `Chip` directive was built to: **state that is announced from the same input that paints it.**

**`segments()` belongs on the service, not in the component**, because `I18nService` already owns the `{{name}}` regex and a second interpolator would be free to drift from the first — the service is thirty lines with exactly one `PLACEHOLDER` constant, and it stays that way.

**The accessible name is unchanged.** Splitting a sentence into sibling spans does not change what a screen reader announces; text nodes are concatenated, so the sentence is read as one sentence. No `aria-label` is needed and none should be added — one would be a second copy of the string, free to disagree with the first.

**Trade-off accepted:** **It cannot be used in an attribute.** `aria-label`, `alt` and `title` take strings, so anywhere a sentence is announced rather than rendered, `t()` remains the call and the face question does not arise — which is correct, but it means one sentence can need both APIs. **Nothing forces a caller to use it.** A developer can write `{{ i18n.t('...', { city }) }}` and get a city in the wrong face, silently; the guard is `CONVENTIONS.md`, this entry and review, which is the same enforcement class as `gap-group` against `gap-3` (212) and `ms-` against `ml-`. **It adds a second render path for strings**, so a reader now has to know which sentences go through the component and which through `t()`, and the answer is not visible at the key. And **the component is specified before it is built**, so DR.10 may find the shape wrong at the first real call site — the two-dictionary signature is the part most likely to move.

**Superseded at 219 in the same sense, and the mechanism is untouched.** `segments()`, `AuthoredLine` and the two-dictionary call site are all unchanged, and the two callers are still the greeting and the weather sentence. 219 changes which faces the two sides of a segmented line are set in — the sentence is italic Cormorant now and the content span is Inter — and changes nothing about where the line is cut.

## 214 — Source Serif 4, and the wght-only file: 065's measurement, the opposite answer

**Decision:** `public/fonts/source-serif-4-latin-variable.woff2`, **50,924 bytes**, sha256 `286e05e5…9143f8`, taken from the Google Fonts `css2` endpoint's **latin** block at the **`wght@200..900`** spec — *not* the `opsz,wght` one. `@font-face` declares `font-weight: 200 900`, `font-style: normal`, `font-display: swap` and the subset's own `unicode-range`; `--font-prose` is the token, in the same `@theme static` block as the colours. **No preload, no italic, and nothing uses it yet.** The existing `OFL.txt` is renamed `OFL-Fraunces.txt` and `OFL-SourceSerif4.txt` is added beside it.

**Alternative:** The `opsz,wght@8..60,200..900` file at 122,168 bytes, which is what 065 chose for Fraunces from the same fork in the road. Or `@fontsource-variable/source-serif-4`. Or no second face at all, leaving authored prose in the system stack and letting size and leading carry it.

**Reasoning:** **The axis question was measured, and it answers the opposite way to 065 — for a reason that is entirely about role.** The two candidates were fetched and read: the `opsz,wght` file carries `wght 200–900` and `opsz 8–60` with a **default of 20**; the `wght`-only file carries `wght 200–900` and **no `opsz` axis at all**, so it renders permanently at that default of 20. The gap is **71,244 bytes — the opsz file is 2.4×**. For Fraunces the equivalent trade bought a *correct* optical size: a `wght`-only request there pinned `opsz` at **9**, the small-text end, for headings rendered at 24–48px, so the axis was the difference between right and wrong. Here it is the difference between right and right. **Authored prose is set at 16–17px by definition** — DR.8 is what makes that sayable, because it defines the role rather than leaving prose to be "text at whatever size" — and `opsz 20` is a text optical size; it is the coordinate every one of the family's own eight named instances sits at. **We would never move off it.** Paying 71 KB for a range 8–60 when the role occupies a two-point band around 20 is buying an axis to hold it still.

**`font-weight: 200 900` is the file's own `fvar` range, read rather than copied.** Google's CSS declares `200 900` here and happens to be right — which is luck, not a rule: for Fraunces the endpoint declared `400 700` over a file that carried `100 900`, and copying it would have clamped. The read cost one throwaway `uvx --with brotli --from fonttools ttx -t fvar` invocation and no permanent dependency; `fontTools` is not installed in this project and is not being added.

**No preload, unlike Fraunces.** 065's preload exists because Fraunces is above the fold on every screen and in the pre-bootstrap boot marker. Authored prose is empty-state bodies, hints and error messages — frequently below the fold, frequently not rendered on a given route at all. A second preload competes with the first for the same critical-path connection to fetch a face that may not be needed; `font-display: swap` is what covers the arrival.

**The `unicode-range` is byte-identical to Fraunces'**, because both come from the same endpoint's latin block. That is worth stating rather than leaving as a coincidence a later reader re-derives.

**Trade-off accepted:** **A second 50 KB binary in git with no version marker**, updated by re-running a documented command — 065's trade-off a second time, and the URL carries a `v14` and a content hash that Google may change under us. **The green is weaker than any other task in this pass, by construction.** Nothing uses `--font-prose`, so "it renders" is not assertable: what is verified is that the `@font-face` and its `unicode-range` reach the built stylesheet, that `--font-prose` survives Tailwind's pruning (it does, because of 064's `static`), that the woff2 is emitted to `dist/bijoux/browser/fonts/`, and that 675 tests, the build and the lint stay clean. **The first real rendering is DR.11.** **`opsz` is now unavailable, not merely unused** — if authored prose is ever set large, the fix is re-fetching the other file, not a CSS change. **No italic**, so any later emphasis in prose gets a synthesised oblique until a second `@font-face` is added. And **`OFL.txt` was ambiguous and is only being fixed now**: it has read *"Copyright 2018 The Fraunces Project Authors"* since 0.9 while sitting under a generic name.

**Superseded at 219, and this one is retired rather than restated.** Source Serif 4 is gone: the `@font-face` block is deleted and `--font-prose` names Cormorant Garamond, because Atelier has one serif answering both authored roles and tells them apart by style. The measurement above was sound and is worth keeping for the method. What it does not survive is a fact found while removing it — **`public/fonts/source-serif-4-latin-variable.woff2` was never in the repository**, so from DR.9 to here every `font-prose` call site has been rendering the `ui-serif` fallback, and the entry describing a 50,924-byte file was describing a file nobody could load.

## 215 — The greeting, and why it is not `userLabel()`

**Decision:** The wardrobe header opens with a line addressed to the person using it — *"Good morning, Coral"* — above the title row, in `--font-prose` with the name in the content face. Six keys: `wardrobe.hello.{morning,afternoon,evening}` and a `nameless` variant of each. The part of day comes from `greetingSlot(new Date())`, read **once in the constructor**, with morning 05:00–11:59, afternoon 12:00–17:59 and evening the rest of the clock. `AuthoredLine` and `I18nService.segments()` are built here, at their first caller. **`userLabel()` is not called and stays uncalled.**

**Alternative:** `userLabel()` in a single `wardrobe.hello` key. A time-neutral *"Welcome back"*, with no clock and one key. Hiding the greeting entirely when there is no display name. Greeting on `/saved` and `/stylist` too.

**Reasoning:** **`userLabel()` is the wrong function, and this was found by reading it rather than by shipping it.** It returns `display_name?.trim() || user.email`, so an account that never filled in a name would be greeted *"Good morning, coral@example.com"*. That fallback is correct for the "signed in as" label it was written for — a label that must never be blank — and it is exactly wrong for a greeting, where saying nothing beats saying the wrong thing. 071's own trade-off paragraph already flagged the email fallback as rendering in a different script from the name it replaces; in a greeting it is worse than a script mismatch. **It is kept rather than deleted** because DR.13's profile work is where "signed in as" semantics actually belong, and deleting six tests including the Hebrew one to reintroduce them two tasks later is churn.

**The nameless case needs its own key, not an absent parameter.** `i18n.service.spec.ts` pins the behaviour: *"leaves a placeholder visible when no value is supplied"*. One key with `{{name}}` and no name renders the literal `Good morning, {{name}}` at the reader. So the count is six keys, not three — and the line keeps its shape rather than the header changing structure depending on whether a profile field was filled in.

**The clock is read once, in the constructor**, which is `stylist.page.ts`'s rule for its dateline: the part of the day a screen was opened in does not change under the reader. The boundaries are exported as `greetingSlot` so the spec can pin them directly rather than through four fixtures that each move the system clock — **and that test earned itself immediately.** The first implementation ended `return hour < 18 ? 'afternoon' : 'evening'`, which calls 04:00 *the afternoon*: the small hours never reach the morning branch, so a single upper bound is not enough. The boundary test failed on the first run and the second bound is there because of it.

**`AuthoredLine` ships with exactly one caller, which is a conscious departure from `AUDITS.md` O-15's rule** that `shared/ui/` is earned by the second. 213 placed it there with the second caller named rather than imagined: DR.12 renders the weather strip as a sentence carrying a geocoded city. Recorded so it does not read as an oversight by whoever next applies O-15.

**The design pass's "no new tests unless a real regression is at stake" does not apply here, and 19 tests were added.** That rule governed a visual refresh, where the markup moved and the behaviour did not. `segments()` is new branching logic and every edge is a real regression case: a missing key, a placeholder with no value, a name in both dictionaries, adjacent placeholders, and **a content value that leads the sentence** — the Hebrew reorderability property the whole mechanism exists to preserve, which no other test in the project would have caught. **Four mutations, control green at both ends:** inverting the segment's content flag killed 9, removing the literal runs killed 7, dropping `[class.font-sans]` killed 4, and restoring `userLabel`'s email fallback killed 1. A fifth — forcing `isContent` to `false` — **does not compile**, because narrowing `content` is what makes the branch type-check: the type system refuses that one before the suite is reached.

**Trade-off accepted:** **The greeting is a behaviour change** — the screen renders differently at 09:00 and at 19:00 — which the design pass forbade and this pass permits; it is here rather than smuggled in. **The hour is the device's**, so a user whose clock is wrong is greeted wrongly, and there is no server time to check against. **Six keys for three greetings**, which doubles again if a fourth slot is ever wanted. **The nameless line is not a fallback anybody sees in testing** unless they sign in with a blank display name, so the branch is covered by a unit test and by nothing else. And **the whitespace risk is real and invisible**: every segment is rendered as a `<span>` precisely so that Angular's whitespace handling has only whitespace-only nodes to remove — a bare interpolation between template lines would let it introduce a space the string never had, turning *"Good morning, Coral"* into *"Good morning,  Coral"*. The test that asserts the exact `textContent` is the only thing standing between that and a shipped double space.
